,
SbLm
,

SbBvr
,

FrVr
,
FrAp
,
FrLm

r_{8}
(renaming)

RnVr
,
RnAp
,
RnLm
1
,

RnLm
2
,
RnBvr
2
,

RnId
,
RnIm
,
RnCh
,
RnCm

r_{9}
(renaming/fresh variant)

RnVr
,
RnAp
,
RnLm
1
,

RnBvr
,

FrVr
,
FrAp
,
FrLm

Fig
.
3
.
Sets of properties underlying different nominal recursors.
The crossed-out properties
FSupFv
in
r_{1}
and
SwId
,
SwIv
,
FvSw
in
r_{4}
were in the original recursors but turn out not to be needed.

Thm 9.

Consider the nine choices, for
i\in\{1,\mathinner{.\mskip 3.0mu.\mskip 3.0mu.},9\}
, of
tuples
r_{i}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{i},\allowbreak I_{i},R_{i})

given by the sets of properties
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
shown in
Fig.
3
.
(E.g.,
\mathsf{Props}_{5}
is
\{{\small\textsf{SwVr}},{\small\textsf{SwAp}},\allowbreak{\small\textsf{SwLm}},{\small\textsf{SwBvr}},{\small\textsf{FrVr}},{\small\textsf{FrAp}},{\small\textsf{FrLm}}\}
.)
Namely,
we assume that the signature
\Sigma_{i}
consists of all the
operation and relation
symbols
occurring in
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
, and:

•

\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is the category of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models and
T=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}})

•

\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
is the category of
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models and

I_{i}
is
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})

•

R_{i}:\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
is the forgetful functor sending
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models to their underlying
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models

Then
r_{i}
is an epi-recursor.
In particular,
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})
is the initial
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-model.

Next we discuss this theorem’s nine statements of epi-recursion principles.
We distinguish between five “original recursors” from the literature and four “variant recursors” obtained from those.

3.4.1.
The original recursors

As suggested by the names in Fig.
3
, five of these principles,
r_{1}
,
r_{4}
,
r_{6}
,
r_{7}
and
r_{8}
, are reformulations of the (stripped down versions of) nominal recursors from §
2.2
.

This is easy to see in the case of
r_{6}
and
r_{7}
. Indeed, after removing the term arguments of the operations and relations,
Thms.
3
(swap/fresh) and
4
(subst/fresh)
simply state, for a suitable extension of the constructor signature
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
, the initiality of the corresponding term model among
all models satisfying
\mathsf{Props}_{6}
or
\mathsf{Props}_{7}
. Moreover, Thm.
5
(about renaming recursion) is easily seen to be exactly
r_{8}
.

Seeing that
r_{1}
is the stripped down version of the
perm/free recursor (from Thm
1
) requires a bit of work. After removing
X
from (i.e., taking
X
to be
\emptyset
in Thm.
1
),
we see that
a nominal set
\mathcal{A}=(A,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{A}})
together with
\emptyset
-supported operations

\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}
can be equivalently described as a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model.
Moreover, the properties of the unique function
g:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow A

guaranteed by Thm.
1
are equivalent to those of

\Sigma_{i}
-morphisms. (App.
A

gives details.)
Thm.
1
does not actually
need
the finite-support condition
FSupFv
for the target domain—which is why in Fig.
3
we show it for
r_{1}
(and for the variant
r_{3}
discussed below) as crossed out.

Seeing that
r_{4}
is the stripped down version of the swap/free recursor (from Thm.
2
) is also not immediate. After removing the Barendregt parameterization on
X
from Thm.
2
, we obtain operations and relations that fit the pattern of full-fledged recursion, i.e., iteration plus additional term arguments—e.g.,
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow A
,

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}:(\mathsf{Tr}\times A)\rightarrow(\mathsf{Tr}\times A)\allowbreak\rightarrow A
and

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow(\mathsf{Tr}\times A)\rightarrow A
. So the situation becomes similar to that
of
r_{6}
and
r_{7}
versus Thms.
3
and
4
.
However, two of Thm.
2
’s assumptions,
(2) and (3), do not
directly fit the normal full-fledged recursion pattern. But after using that
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})={{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{1}\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{2}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)={{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t\smallsetminus\{x\}
, they are seen equivalent to:

(2)

If
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,a_{1}\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t_{1}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,a_{2}\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t_{2}

then

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,(t_{1},a_{1})\;(t_{2},a_{2}))\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{1}\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{2}

(3)

If
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,a\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t

then

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,x\;(t,a))\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t\smallsetminus\{x\}

In this form, they are seen to express a kind of full-fledged recursion that is optimized for the free-variable operator. Indeed, they
are weaker versions of ones that
do
fit the pattern:

(2)

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,(t_{1},a_{1})\;(t_{2},a_{2}))\subseteq({{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,a_{1}\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{1})\cup({{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,a_{2}\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{2})

(3)

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,x\;(t,a))\subseteq({{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\,a\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t)\smallsetminus\{x\}

(In App.
D

we show how this free-variable-specific optimization can be seen as a general enhancement available to all our discussed recursors that involve freeness or freshness.)
Removing the term arguments from the latter turns them into Fig.
2
’s
FvAp
and
FvLm
; and removing the term arguments from the other assumptions in Thm.
2
turns them into the other properties of
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
. Finally, the conclusion of Thm.
2
corresponds precisely to the
\Sigma_{2}
-morphism conditions.

Three of the properties originally postulated by

Norrish 2004
for the swap/free recursor,
SwId
,
SwIv
and
FvSw
, are not needed, meaning that the recursion theorem holds without them (hence they are crossed out under
r_{4}
in Fig.
3
.)
This is a surprising result, given the careful analysis done by Norrish
when distilling the required properties for his recursor to work. We detected this redundancy while subsuming the
(\Sigma_{4},\mathsf{Props}_{4})
-models to the more general
(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-models during recursor comparison (discussed in §
4
), so this strengthening owes to the different path
taken when proving
r_{5}
.

3.4.2.
The variant recursors

The remaining principles,
r_{2},r_{3},r_{5}
and
r_{9}
, are obtained by combining
axioms of the original recursors. They act as bridges between the latter helping their comparison,
but are also of independent interest, e.g.,
r_{9}
will be seen to be maximal with respect to expressiveness.

We call
r_{2}
a “perm/free variant” because it is another recursor based on permutation and freeness, just like the original perm/free recursor
r_{1}
. However
r_{2}
does not follow the nominal-set route of
r_{1}
(which defines the free-variable, i.e., support operator from permutation, via
FvDPm
) but instead follows the idea of the swap/free recursor
r_{4}
(using permutation instead of swapping) and postulates properties connecting the free-variable operator with permutation (via
PmFv
and
FvPm
) and with the constructors (via
FvVr
,
FvAp
and
FvLm
). In short,
r_{2}
is a hybrid between
r_{1}
and
r_{4}
. For the symmetry of presentation, under
r_{2}
the figure also shows
FvPm
—the permutation counterpart of
FvSw
, but crosses it out because, like
FvSw
, is also not needed. Another hybrid between the two is the swap-free variant
r_{3}
, which uses the swapping operator like
r_{4}
and nominal-set-like axioms like
r_{1}
.

r_{5}
is an
r_{6}
–
r_{7}
hybrid, born from the observation that

r_{6}
and
r_{7}

have similar structures, in that they both axiomatize the interaction between constructors and freshness, and between constructors and their specific operator (either swapping or substitution); of course, substitution behaves differently from swapping w.r.t. constructors, but the respective constructor-commuting properties (
SbVr
,
SbAp
and
SbLm
vs.
SwVr
,
SwAp
and
SwLm
) have a similar flavor.
The difference between
r_{6}
and
r_{7}
lies in the additional property that they use to further underpin recursion over the constructors: in one case via a congruence rule
SwCg
and in the other via a bound-variable-renaming rule
SbBvr
. However, both these latter types of rules make sense for the other operator too,
mutatis mutandis
.
As it turns out, we can replace
SwCg
with
SbBvr
in the swap/fresh recursor

r_{6}
, obtaining the swap/fresh variant
r_{5}
.
But we cannot perform the dual modification to the subst/fresh recursor
r_{7}
, where replacing
SbBvr
with
SbCg
would not give a valid recursor; the reason is that, unlike swapping, substitution-like operators need a more delicate handling of the bound variables, which
SbBvr
but not
SbCg
can achieve.

Finally,
r_{9}
is a
r_{7}
–
r_{8}
hybrid, in that it has axioms similar to
r_{7}
, but uses renaming like
r_{8}
rather than substitution.
And similarly to the case of substitution, replacing
RnBvr
with
RnCg
would not work.

We discovered these variant recursors
during the Isabelle formalization of the originals—observing the roles played by different axioms in underpinning recursion, and noting that in specific contexts some operators and axioms are interchangeable.

Proof idea for Thm.
9
.

For any
i\in\{1,\mathinner{.\mskip 3.0mu.\mskip 3.0mu.},9\}
, the only non-trivial part of the statement that

r_{i}
is an epi-recursor is the initiality theorem, i.e., the fact that
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})
is the initial
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-model.

The initiality theorems for the original recursors already have been proved in the literature (as we discussed in §
2
),
whereas
the variant recursors
r_{2},r_{3},r_{5}
and
r_{9}
are new.
We (re)proved all these recursors via the following route: First we gave direct proofs for
r_{6}
and
r_{9}
, and then we used the transformations underlying the expressiveness relations in Thm.
12
in order to infer (“borrow”) the initiality theorems for the others from the above two (which are at the top of Thm.
12
’s expressiveness hierarchy).
App.
C
gives details.

Next, we show the proof idea for
r_{9}
, which is a generalization/adaptation of that for
r_{7}
from
Popescu 2023b
.
Let
\mathcal{M}
be a
(\Sigma_{9},\mathsf{Props}_{9})
-model.
We first define
a relation
R:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow M\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
, with inductive clauses reflecting the desired properties of commutation with the constructors:

R\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;x)\hskip 25.83325pt\frac{R\;t_{1}\;m_{1}\hskip 17.22217ptR\;t_{2}\;m_{2}}{R\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;m_{1}\;m_{2})}\hskip 25.83325pt\frac{R\;t\;m}{R\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;x\;m)}

To obtain a
\Sigma_{9}
-morphism
f:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow M
, it suffices
to prove that
R
(1) is total, (2) is functional, (3) preserves renaming and (4) preserves freshness, since then we can take
f
to be the function induced by
R
.
Property (1) (totality) follows easily by standard induction on terms.
The remaining properties, (2)–(4), follow by a simultaneous inductive proof
using a form of “renaming-based induction” on terms: Given a predicate
\varphi:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
, to show
\forall t\in\mathsf{Tr}.\;\varphi\;t
it suffices to show the following: (i)
\forall x\in\mathsf{{Var}}.\;\varphi\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)
, (ii)
\forall t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathsf{Tr}.\;\varphi\;t_{1}\,\&\,\varphi\;t_{2}\rightarrow\varphi\,(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})
, and (iii)
\forall x\in\mathsf{{Var}},\,t\in\mathsf{Tr}.\;(\forall s\in\mathsf{Tr}.\;\textsf{RConnect}\;t\;s\rightarrow\varphi\;s)\rightarrow\varphi\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)
, where
\textsf{RConnect}\;t\;s
means that
s
is obtained from
t
by a chain of renamings. (So we take
\varphi
to be the conjunction of (2)–(4).) The uniqueness of
f
follows by induction on terms.
The proof for
r_{6}
is similar to that for
r_{9}
, but uses a corresponding swapping-based induction.
∎

4.
Comparing recursors

An advantage of viewing nominal recursors as epi-recursors is clear sight on their relative expressiveness.
In this section, we start with a direct means of comparing epi-recursor expressiveness
and instantiate it to our nominal recursors (§
4.1
).
Then we analyze a problematic example, semantic interpretation (§
4.2
), which suggests a gentler comparison—yielding a much flatter expressiveness hierarchy (§
4.3
).
While the kind of relationships we establish show how a recursor can replace another, they do not imply that
the converse is not true, and indeed in some cases the converse is true, making the recursors equivalent (w.r.t. a tighter or gentler comparison); but in two cases we also know that the converse is not true, meaning the
relation there is strict (§
4.4
).

4.1.
A head-to-head comparison

Def 10.

Given epi-recursors
r=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
and
r^{\prime}=\allowbreak(\underline{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},I^{\prime},R^{\prime})

with the same base category
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
and base object
T
, we call
r^{\prime}
stronger than
r
, written
r^{\prime}\geq r
, if
r^{\prime}
can define everything that

r
can, i.e.:
for all objects
B
in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
and morphisms

g:T\rightarrow B
,
g
definable by
r
implies
g
definable by
r^{\prime}
.

\textstyle{I}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\exists F}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime}}
\textstyle{I^{\prime}=F\,I}
\textstyle{R\,I=R^{\prime}\,I^{\prime}=T}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{B}}}

Fig
.
4
.
Criterion for comparing expressiveness

It is easy to see that

\geq
is a preorder on epi-recursors.
We write
r\equiv r^{\prime}
to state that
r
and
r^{\prime}
have equal strengths, i.e., both

r^{\prime}\geq r
and
r\geq r^{\prime}
hold.
We can establish
r^{\prime}\geq r
by showing how to move from
r^{\prime}
to
r
in an initial-object preserving way, as depicted in Fig.
4
:

Prop 11.

Let
r=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
and

r^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},I^{\prime},\allowbreak R^{\prime})
, and assume
F:\underline{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is a pre-functor
(i.e., a functor but without the requirement of preserving identity and composition of morphisms)
such that

R^{\prime}\circ F=R
and

F\;I=I^{\prime}
.
Then
r^{\prime}\geq r
.

Proof.

Assume
g:T\rightarrow B
is definable by
r
, meaning that
g=R\;!_{I,C}
for some
C
in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.
Let
C^{\prime}=F\;C
.
By the initiality of
I^{\prime}
and the fact that
F\;I=I^{\prime}
, we have that

!_{I^{\prime},C^{\prime}}=F\;!_{I,C}
. Hence

g=R\;!_{I,C}=R^{\prime}\;(F\;!_{I,C})=R^{\prime}\;!_{I^{\prime},C^{\prime}}
,
meaning that
g
is definable by
r^{\prime}
. ∎

(In all our examples, the above initial-object preserving pre-functor condition
will be satisfied by actual functors that are left adjoints.)
One way to read Prop.
11
’s criterion (and Fig.
4
’s picture) is the following: Thinking of
R
as a kind of “distance"
from the extended category
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
(and its initial object
I
) to the base category
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
(and the base object
B
), we have that
the smaller this distance, the more expressive the recursor. We have applied this criterion to prove the following expressiveness hierarchy:

Thm 12.

The epi-recursors described in Thm.
9
(and in Fig.
3
) compare as follows with respect to their expressiveness:

r_{6}\geq r_{5}\geq r_{4}\geq r_{2}\geq r_{1}\equiv r_{3}

and
r_{9}\geq r_{8},r_{7}
.

Proof idea.

When proving each

r_{i}\geq r_{j}
, we instantiate Prop.
11
taking
r^{\prime}=r_{i}
and
r=r_{j}
. So here
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is the category of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models,
\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
that of
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models,
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
that of
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models;
R^{\prime}

is the forgetful functor from
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models, and
R
the forgetful functor from
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models;

B=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}})
,

I^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})
and
I=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{j})
.
In each case, we must define a pre-functor
F:\underline{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}

such that

R^{\prime}\circ F=R
and
F\;I=I^{\prime}
.
This essentially means showing how to transform
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models into
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models in such a manner that
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{j})

becomes
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})
—which gives
F
’s behavior on objects, while on morphisms
F
will be the identity. Each time,
F
will transform models by preserving the carrier set and the constructor-like operators, and possibly defining
(1) permutation-like from swapping-like operators or vice versa, (2)
freshness-like from
free-variable-like operators, or (3) renaming-like from substitution-like operators;
these definitions are done just like for concrete terms (where, e.g., we can standardly define freshness from freeness). In each case, the only interesting fact that needs to be checked is that
F
is well-defined on objects: when starting with a
\Sigma_{j}
-model satisfying
\mathsf{Props}_{j}
, the result
\Sigma_{i}
-model indeed satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
. Everything else amounts to either well-known or trivial properties. Thus,
F\;I=I^{\prime}
means that the standard inter-definability properties (1)–(3) hold for terms, e.g.,
x\,\#\,t
iff
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t
; and
R^{\prime}\circ F=R
(i.e.,
F
commutes with the forgetful functors to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models) follows immediately from the fact that
F
does not change the carrier set or the constructor-like operators.
Next, we informally discuss these transformations and highlight the intuitions behind them.

r_{1}\equiv r_{3}

holds because permutation-like and swapping-like operators
correspond bijectively to each other, allowing one to (functorially) move back and forth between
\mathsf{Props}_{1}
-models and
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
-models
(
Pitts 2013
, Section 6.1)
.
For
r_{2}\geq r_{1}
, we note that

r_{2}
seems
a priori
more flexible than
r_{1}
in that it does not require the free-variable operator to be
definable from
permutation, but only to be
related to
permutation by some weaker properties; and indeed,
any

(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model can be proved to be in particular a
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model.

r_{4}\geq r_{2}
holds essentially for the same reason why
r_{3}\geq r_{1}
holds,
since the restriction of a permutation to a swapping operator carries over to their axiomatized relationships with free-variable operators,
PmFv
versus
SwFv
. (But the converse is not true because
r_{4}
lacks (does not need) some of the swapping axioms that ensure extension to a permutation operator.)

r_{5}\geq r_{4}
follows using a model transformation that
turns the free-variable operator of
r_{4}
into a freshness operator for
r_{5}
, using negation; indeed, save for the straightforwardly corresponding
FvVr
,
FvAp
,
FvLm
versus
FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm
,
the only difference between
r_{4}
and
r_{5}
is the replacement of

SwFv

with

SwBvr
;
and the former axiom implies the latter in the presence of
FvLm
.
r_{6}\geq r_{5}
follows from the fact that, in the presence of the other axioms in
\mathsf{Props}_{5}
,

SwBvr
implies
SwCg
.
r_{9}\geq r_{7}
holds because the axioms for substitution imply those for renaming (for
the straightforward restriction of a substitution operator to a renaming operator). Finally, the proof of

r_{9}\geq r_{8}
takes advantage of the fact that, in a constructor-enriched renset (structures axiomatizing renaming that form the basis of recursor
r_{8}
), freshness is definable from renaming
(
Popescu 2023b
)
.
∎

Thus, there are two recursors at the top of the expressiveness hierarchy: the swap/fresh recursor
r_{6}
and the renaming/fresh variant recursor
r_{9}
. Roughly speaking, these two recursors’ expressiveness is strong because their underlying axiomatizations:

•

keep freshness only loosely coupled with other operators
such as swapping, permutation or renaming—unlike
r_{1}
,
r_{3}
and
r_{8}
which ask that freshness be definable from them;

•

use congruence or renaming axioms that target exactly the ingredients needed for having recursion go through—unlike those of
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4}
and
r_{8}
, which employ
algebraic axiomatizations such as nominal sets, swapping structures or rensets;

•

keep the structure of their operators minimalistic and non-redundant—unlike
r_{7}
, whose operator emulates substitution, which is more than needed (since renaming would suffice).

Choosing between swapping and permutation as recursion primitives
turned out to be interesting.
The two are known to be equivalent for nominal sets
(
Pitts 2013
, §6.1)
, as reflected by
r_{1}\equiv r_{3}
.

But they are no longer equivalent when loosening the axiomatization to include freshness as a primitive—as reflected by the fact that
r_{4}\geq r_{2}
but (as we will show in §
4.4
) not vice versa. This is because the proof of the
r_{4}
recursor (by
Norrish 2004
) gets away without assuming swapping compositionality
SwCp
, which is a crucial ingredient for extending swapping to permutation. Moreover, in an indirect way, we also showed the other crucial ingredients needed for this extension, namely
SwId
and
SwIv
, are not required for recursion either.
Thus, in this case swapping-based recursion requires significantly weaker
assumptions
than permutation-based recursion.

4.2.
Semantic-interpretation example

The notion of interpreting syntax in semantic domains is a well-known challenging example for binding-aware recursion.
Let
D
be a set and
\mathsf{{AP}}:D\rightarrow D\rightarrow D

and
\mathsf{{LM}}:(D\rightarrow D)\rightarrow D
be operators modeling semantic notions of application and abstraction.
(Subject to some axioms that are not of interest here, the structure
(D,\mathsf{{AP}},\mathsf{{LM}})
is known as a Henkin model for
\lambda
-calculus
(
Barendregt 1984
)
.) An environment will be a function
\xi:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow D
.
Given
x,y\in\mathsf{{Var}}
and
d,e\in D
, we write
\xi\langle x:=d\rangle
for
\xi
updated with value
d
for
x
,
and write
\xi\langle x:=d,y:=e\rangle
instead of
\xi\langle x:=d\rangle\langle y:=e\rangle
.

The semantic interpretation
{\mathsf{sem}}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow(\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow D)\rightarrow D
should go recursively by the clauses:

(1)

{\mathsf{sem}}\,(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\,\xi=\xi\;x

(2)

{\mathsf{sem}}\,(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\,t_{2})\,\xi=\mathsf{{AP}}\,({\mathsf{sem}}\;t_{1}\,\xi)\,({\mathsf{sem}}\;t_{2}\,\xi)

(3)

{\mathsf{sem}}\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)\,\xi=\mathsf{{LM}}\,(d\mapsto{\mathsf{sem}}\;t\,(\xi\langle x:=d\rangle))

Of course, these clauses do not work out of the box (i.e., do not form a correct recursive definition yet), and here is where the nominal recursors can help. First, let us attempt to deploy the perm/free recursor
r_{1}
. To this end, we try to organize the target domain
I=(\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow D)\rightarrow D

as a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model
\mathcal{I}
.
The three desired clauses above already determine constructor operations
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{I}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}
on the set of interpretations,
I=(\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow D)\rightarrow D
, namely:

(1)

\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{I}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow I
by
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{I}}x\;i\;\xi=\xi\;x

(2)
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{I}}:I\rightarrow I\rightarrow I
by
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{I}}i_{1}\,i_{2}\;\xi=\mathsf{{AP}}\,(i_{1}\,\xi)\,(i_{2}\,\xi)

(3)

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow I\rightarrow I
by
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}x\;i\;\xi=\mathsf{{LM}}\,(d\mapsto i\,(\xi\langle x:=d\rangle))

Thus, we already have the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
component of our intended model.
Now we must define a permutation operator on
I
.
The definition is obtained by analyzing the desired behavior of the to-be-defined function
{\mathsf{sem}}
w.r.t. permutation; i.e., determining the value of
{\mathsf{sem}}(t[\sigma])
from
{\mathsf{sem}}\;t
and
\sigma
.
The answer is
(4)
{\mathsf{sem}}\,(t[\sigma])\,\xi={\mathsf{sem}}\;t\;(\xi\circ\sigma)
,
and leads to defining
\_[\_]^{\mathcal{I}}
by
i\,[\sigma]^{\mathcal{I}}\,\xi=i\,(\xi\circ\sigma)
.

Note that, towards the goal of building a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model
\mathcal{I}
, we had no other choice
on defining the operators
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
[\_]^{\mathcal{I}}
on the target domain
I
. And the free-variable (support) operator
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}
is also uniquely determined by the axiom
FvDPm
(definability of freeness from permutation).

Finally, to deploy
r_{1}
and obtain a function
{\mathsf{sem}}
satisfying clauses (1)–(4), it remains to check that
\mathcal{I}
satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{1}
. But, as it turns out,
\mathcal{I}
does not satisfy one of the axioms in
\mathsf{Props}_{1}
, namely
FCB
(freshness condition for binders). Indeed,
FCB
requires that there exists a variable
x
such that for all
i\in I
,

x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}\,x\;i)
. Applying
FvDPm
and
the definitions of
\_[\_]^{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, we see that
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}\,x\;i)
means

\mathsf{{LM}}\;(d\mapsto i\,(\xi\langle x:=d,y:=\xi\,x\rangle)=\mathsf{{LM}}\;(d\mapsto i\,(\xi\langle x:=d\rangle))

holds for all but a finite number of variables
y
.
The only chance for the above to be true is if
i
, when applied to an environment, say
\xi^{\prime}
, ignores the value of
y
in
\xi^{\prime}

for all but a finite number of variables
y
;
in other words,
i

only analyzes the values of a finite number of variables in
\xi^{\prime}
—but this is not guaranteed to hold for arbitrary elements
i\in I
.
Thus,

r_{1}
cannot be deployed directly to define semantic interpretations.

Other recursors in our list can. E.g., the perm-free variant
r_{2}
can be deployed as follows. We use the same definitions for
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
[\_]^{\mathcal{I}}\!
, but now we can choose the free-variable operator
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}\!
more flexibly, making sure that the
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-morphism condition holds for
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}\!
versus
{{\mathsf{FV}}}
, i.e., that
(5)
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}\!({\mathsf{sem}}\;t)\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t
holds.
Namely, we define
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}i
as
\{x\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid\exists\xi:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow D,\,d\in D.\;i\;\xi\not=i\;(\xi\langle x:=d\rangle)\}
. The definition identifies a natural notion of what it means for a variable to “occur freely” in a semantic item
i\in I
: when
i
actually depends on
x
, i.e., when changing the value of
x
in an input environment
\xi
makes a difference in the result of applying
i
.
And indeed, with
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}\!
defined like this,
\mathcal{I}
forms a
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model, which gives us a unique function
{\mathsf{sem}}
satisfying (1)–(5).

Thus, semantic interpretation is an example where our
“head-to-head” comparison has a visible outcome.
But there is still
an unexplored nuance here, which we discuss next.

Above, we argued that the semantic-interpretation example cannot be defined
directly
using the perm/free recursor
r_{1}
.
However, as discussed by
Pitts 2006
, it turns out that it can be defined in a more roundabout manner, after some technical hassle. The trick is to restrict the target domain
I
to a subset
I^{\prime}
on which the above defined operators do form an
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model, and use
r_{1}
to define
{\mathsf{sem}}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow I^{\prime}
.
It is interesting to look at Pitts’s definition of the subset
I^{\prime}
, because it will reveal a way to relax the expressiveness comparison between epi-recursors.

I^{\prime}
is defined as

\{i\in I\mid\exists V\subseteq\mathsf{{Var}}.\;V\mbox{ finite and }\forall x\in V.\;\forall\xi,d.\;i\;\xi=i\;(\xi\langle x:=d\rangle)\}
. Then one proves that
I^{\prime}
is closed under the constructors
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{I}},\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Moreover, for
I^{\prime}
the above problem with
FCB
disappears, roughly because all the elements of
I^{\prime}
are finitary. So
I^{\prime}
, with the same operators as those we tried for
I
, now forms a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model, and
r_{1}
recursion can proceed and define
{\mathsf{sem}}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow I^{\prime}
, hence also
{\mathsf{sem}}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow I
.

Having different nominal recursors in front of us laid out as epi-recursors, we can view Pitts’s trick in a new light. Remember that, when deploying
r_{2}
to define
{\mathsf{sem}}
, we used the operator

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}\!
, which is a laxer notion of free-variable than that allowed by

r_{1}
. An equivalent definition of
I^{\prime}
is as the set of all elements of
I
that have
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}\!
finite.
Thus, Pitts’s trick can be seen as borrowing the free-variable operator from the different recursor
r_{2}
, in order to single out a suitable target domain for deploying
r_{1}
!
One can also prove
that, on
I^{\prime}
, the nominal-logic support (defined from permutation via
FvDPm
)
coincides
with
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{I}}\!
—which means that, for the target domain
I^{\prime}
,

r_{1}
works as well as
r_{2}
.

Thus, on a subset of the target domain that is closed under constructors, the previously deemed weaker recursor
r_{1}
can simulate
r_{2}
. As it turns out, this is a general phenomenon, which we can phrase for epi-recursors as a gentler expressiveness comparison.

4.3.
A gentler comparison

Our relation
r^{\prime}\geq r
compares the strength of epi-recusors directly,
as inclusion between what
r
can define and what
r^{\prime}
can define.
The discussion ending §
4.2

suggests that this relation
may be too strict. More flexibly, we could check if what
r
can define is obtainable from what
r^{\prime}
can define
up to composition with a morphism
(which can be an inclusion, as in Pitts’s trick).

Formalizing this
for two epi-recursors
r=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
and
r^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},I^{\prime},R^{\prime})
must make sure to avoid pathological dependencies.
Indeed, a first attempt is: For all objects
B
in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
and morphisms

g:T\rightarrow B
, if
g
is definable by
r
then there exists an object
B_{0}

and two morphisms
g_{0}:T\rightarrow B_{0}
and
h:B_{0}\rightarrow B
such that
g_{0}
is definable by
r^{\prime}
and
g=h\circ g_{0}
. But this would yield a vacuous concept, rendering any epi-recursor
r^{\prime}
stronger than any other
r
: just take
B_{0}=T
,
g_{0}=1_{T}
(which is obviously definable by
r^{\prime}
) and
h=g
. So we should be careful not to allow the above “transition” morphism
h

to depend on the
r
-definability morphism
g
.
Otherwise, we would use
r
-definability itself to reduce
r
-definability to
r^{\prime}
-definability.

For producing morphisms to objects
B
of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
independently of other data, the following concept comes handy.
An
initial segment
of a category
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
is a pair
(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0},(m(C):o(C)\rightarrow C)_{C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}})})
where
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
is a full subcategory
of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
and, for each object
C
of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
,
o(C)
is an object of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}

and
m(C)
a morphism in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.
Using an ordering metaphor, an initial segment of a category provides a “smaller” object for any of its objects.
Now we can formulate our gentler relation for comparing strength,
called quasi-strength:

Def 13.

r^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\allowbreak\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},I^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is
quasi-stronger
than
r=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
, written
r^{\prime}\gtrsim r
,
when there exists an initial segment
(\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0},(m(B):o(B)\rightarrow B)_{B\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})})
of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
such that,
for all

g:T\rightarrow B
definable by
r
, there exists
a morphism
g_{0}:T\rightarrow o(B)
such that
g_{0}
is definable by
r^{\prime}
and
g=m(B)\circ g_{0}
.

Thus,
r^{\prime}\gtrsim r
says that what
r
can define is obtainable from what
r^{\prime}
can define up to composition with a morphism that only depends on the target object in the base category.
Note that we use initial segments to make sure that the morphisms that “fill the gap” between the two recursors
r
and
r^{\prime}
are given
before hand
, so that they are independent from any specific recursively defined function (in particular, preventing bogus expressiveness orderings like the one exemplified above).

\gtrsim
is a preorder weaker than
\geq
. We write
r\cong r^{\prime}
to mean that
r^{\prime}\gtrsim r
and
r\gtrsim r^{\prime}
, i.e.,
r
and
r^{\prime}
have quasi-equal strengths.

While being a reasonable weakening of
\geq
, the
relation
\gtrsim
is likely to be more costly to deploy than
\geq
. Indeed,
as suggested by our discussion in §
4.2
,
applying
r^{\prime}\gtrsim r
, i.e., using
r^{\prime}
in lieu of
r
, in particular extracting
o(B)
from
B
and using
o(B)
as a “more precise” target domain,
can
involve non-negligible formal bureaucracy in concrete situations.

Our effective criterion for checking
\geq
(Prop.
11
) can be generalized to deal with
\gtrsim
.
Given two categories
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, each with
initial segments
(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\allowbreak(m(C):o(C)\rightarrow C)_{C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}})})

and
(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}^{\prime},\allowbreak(m^{\prime}(C):o^{\prime}(C)\rightarrow C)_{C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime})})
, a functor
G:\underline{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is said to
preserve
the indicated initial segments if
G\,o(C)=o^{\prime}(G\;C)
and
G\,(m(C))=m^{\prime}(G\;C)
for all
C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}})
.

Prop 14.

Let
r=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
and

r^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},I^{\prime},\allowbreak R^{\prime})
. Assume

(\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0},(m(B):o(B)\rightarrow B)_{B\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})})
is an initial segment of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}

and
(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\allowbreak(m_{1}(C):o_{1}(C)\rightarrow C)_{C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}})})
is an initial segment of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}

such that
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
contains
I
and

R
preserves the above initial segments,
and
F:\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is a pre-functor such that

F\;I=I^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}

(where
R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
is the restriction of
R
to
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
).
Then
r^{\prime}\gtrsim r
.

The gist of this criterion (and also its proof idea)
is shown in Fig.
5
: We start with a morphism
g
definable by
r
and use the two initial segments to factor it
as a morphism
g_{0}
definable by
r^{\prime}
and a remainder morphism
m(B)
.

\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\subseteq\underline{\mathcal{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\exists F}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!_{I,C}}
\scriptstyle{!_{I,o_{1}(C)}}
\textstyle{o_{1}(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{1}(C)}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\prime}}
\textstyle{I^{\prime}=F\,I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\;!_{I,o_{1}(C)}\,=\,!_{I^{\prime},C^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}=F\;o_{1}(C)}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\subseteq\underline{\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{R\,I\!=\!R^{\prime}\,I^{\prime}\!=\!T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\,=\,R\;!_{I,C}}
\scriptstyle{g_{0}=R\,!_{I,o_{1}(C)}=R^{\prime}\,!_{I^{\prime},C^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{o(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m(B)\,=\,R\;m_{1}(C)}
\textstyle{B=R\;C}

Fig
.
5
.
Gentler criterion for comparing expressiveness

Applying the gentler comparison to our recursors (via Prop.
14
)
yields a quite surprising result:

Thm 15.

The epi-recursors described in Thm.
9
(and in Fig.
3
) compare as follows by quasi-strength:
r_{1}\cong r_{2}\cong r_{3}\cong r_{4}\cong r_{5}\cong r_{6}\gtrsim r_{8}\cong r_{9}\gtrsim r_{7}
.

Proof idea.

When proving each

r_{i}\gtrsim r_{j}
, we instantiate Prop.
14
taking
r^{\prime}=r_{i}
and
r=r_{j}
. So here
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is the category of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models,
\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
that of
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models,
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
that of
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models;
R^{\prime}

is the forgetful functor from
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models, and
R
the forgetful functor from
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models;

B=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}})
,

I^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})
and
I=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{j})
.

We define the initial segment
(\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0},(m(\mathcal{A}):o(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{A})_{\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})})
of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
as follows: For any
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-model
\mathcal{A}
we take

o(\mathcal{A})
to be its minimal submodel (subalgebra),
i.e., the one generated by

\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}
; we take
m(\mathcal{A}):o(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
to be the inclusion morphism; and we take
\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
to be the full subcategory given by constructor-generated models.
Each time, we will define the initial segment
(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\allowbreak(m_{1}(\mathcal{M}):o_{1}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{M})_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}})})

so that, for each
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-model
\mathcal{M}
,

o_{1}(\mathcal{M})
is a submodel of
\mathcal{M}
whose carrier is generated by the constructors (
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}
) and will have the other operators from
\Sigma_{j}
defined in specific ways; and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
will be the full subcategory given by the objects
o_{1}(\mathcal{M})
.
This way, it will be guaranteed that
R
preserves initial segments.

To prove the
\cong
-chain going from
r_{1}
to
r_{6}
,
thanks to Thm.
12
and the fact that
\gtrsim
is weaker than
\geq
,
it suffices to prove
r_{3}\gtrsim r_{6}
.
We proceed as follows: Given a
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-model
\mathcal{M}
of carrier
M
, we take
o_{1}(\mathcal{M})
to be
a submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
, having as carrier set the subset
M^{\prime}
of
M
generated by the constructors
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}
, having the constructors and swapping operators inherited from
\mathcal{M}
and having freshness defined from swapping in nominal style (as in
FrDSw
); crucially, this definition of freshness turns out to be equivalent to an inductive one using
FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm
, making
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
the minimal
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-submodel of
\mathcal{M}
.
Now, the pre-functor
F
is defined on objects as follows:
F\;\mathcal{M}^{\prime}

is the
\Sigma_{3}
-model having the same constructors and swapping operator as
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, and having the free-variable operator defined standardly from the freshness operator of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, via negation. (And on morphisms,
F
is the identity.)
F\;\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
:
SwVr
,
SwAp
,
SwLm
and
FvDSw
hold by construction, and
FCB
,
SwId
,
SwIv
and
SwCp
follow by induction on the definition of
M^{\prime}
. The other required properties are trivial, e.g.,
F\;I=I^{\prime}
here means that the standard definition of free-variables from freshness is correct for terms; and
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
means that
F
commutes with the forgetful functors.

To prove the
(\cong,\gtrsim)
-chain going from
r_{6}
to
r_{7}
,
again thanks to Thm.
12

it suffices to prove

r_{8}\gtrsim r_{9}
and

r_{6}\gtrsim r_{8}
. (We will no longer show explicitly the definitions of the initial segment and the pre-functor, but give the ingredients from which they can be constructed similarly to how we did above.)
For

r_{8}\gtrsim r_{9}
, we
start similarly to the proof of
r_{3}\gtrsim r_{6}
, namely for a

(\Sigma_{9},\mathsf{Props}_{9})
-model
\mathcal{M}

we take the
minimal submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
where freshness definable from renaming (via
FrDRn
) turns out to coincide
with the inductively defined version via
FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm
.
Because the carrier
M^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is the image of the unique

\Sigma_{9}
-morphism

f:\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{9})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}

ensured by the initiality of

\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{9})
,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies all unconditional equations satisfied
by
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{9})
, in particular, all the
\mathsf{Props}_{8}
properties.

Finally, the proof of

r_{6}\gtrsim r_{8}
exploits the observation that renaming is definable from swapping
not only for terms, but also for any
(\Sigma_{8},\mathsf{Props}_{8})
-model
\mathcal{M}
that guarantees the existence of fresh
variables, i.e., having its elements finitely supported:
m\,[z_{1}\wedge^{\mathcal{M}}z_{2}]
is defined as
m\,[y/^{\mathcal{M}}z_{1}]\,\allowbreak[z_{1}/^{\mathcal{M}}z_{2}]\,[z_{2}/^{\mathcal{M}}y]
where
y
is fresh (and, using the
\mathsf{Props}_{8}
axioms, the choice of
y
can be proved not to matter).
While arbitrary
(\Sigma_{8},\mathsf{Props}_{8})
-models
\mathcal{M}
do not guarantee finite support, we can again switch to a minimal
submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
that does guarantee it—and in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
the above definition indeed yields a swapping operator that together with
the constructors and freshness
satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{6}
.
∎

Thus,
\gtrsim
brings a dramatic flattening of the
\geq
hierarchy established by Thm.
12
: All the swapping- and permutation-based recursors
r_{1}
–
r_{6}
have equal quasi-strengths. The intuition for this, as we discovered during the proofs, is the following. Recall that the differences in strength (using
\geq
) between these recursors were due to: (1) looseness or tightness of their connection between swapping/permutation and freeness/freshness, (2) higher flexibility of swapping compared to permutation, and (3) more focused nature of congruence compared to an algebraic axiomatization. Remarkably, all these differences vanish if we are allowed to navigate along submodels, which
\gtrsim
enables.
This is because (as explained in the proof of Thm.
15
),
certain minimal submodels
are much more “term-like” than an arbitrary model; they generalize Pitts’s submodel definition for semantic interpretation, where nominal-style freshness coincides with other, more loosely axiomatized notions of freshness.

An interesting takeover when switching from
\geq
to
\gtrsim

is the swapping/permutation-based recursors
r_{1}
–
r_{6}

becoming
(quasi-)
stronger
than the renaming-based recursors
r_{8}
and
r_{9}
.
Indeed, defining renaming from swapping or vice versa seems impossible in arbitrary models, meaning these two types of recursors are
\geq
-incomparable. But when switching to submodels (allowed by
\gtrsim
) one direction is possible: The swapping of two variables can be defined in a renaming-based model similarly to how it is done for concrete terms, via picking an intermediate
fresh variable; and “picking fresh” is possible in minimal submodels because everything there is finitely supported.

Summary.

Epi-recursors are comparable for expressiveness by a strict relation
\geq
, saying that everything definable by one is definable by the other, and a laxer relation
\gtrsim
, saying that everything definable by one can be defined by the other with the help of an additional morphism, typically a submodel inclusion.
The handling of the semantic-interpretation example with the nominal-logic recursor was our inspiration for
\gtrsim
, and
suggests the additional overhead incurred by
\gtrsim
.
The effective criteria we used to prove these relations for concrete recursors (Props.
11
and
14
),
can be paraphrased using “is” and “has”:

•

r^{\prime}\geq r
holds if any
r
-model
\mathcal{M}

is
an
r^{\prime}
-model—in that it can be regarded (after defining the relevant operations, in a way that ensures functoriality) as an
r^{\prime}
-model.

•

r^{\prime}\gtrsim r
holds if any
r
-model
\mathcal{M}

has
an
r^{\prime}
-submodel—in that there exists a submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
that still satisfies the properties required by
r
, and can be regarded as an
r^{\prime}
-model.

The
\gtrsim
-hierarchy is significantly flatter than the
\geq
-hierarchy, sending an egalitarian message:
Most nominal recursors turn out to have the same strength
, with the only nuance that those based on
symmetric operators (swapping and permutation) are more expressive than those based on asymmetric ones (renaming and substitution).

4.4.
Negative results

Thms.
12
and
15
establish
\geq
and
\gtrsim

relationships between recursors, which essentially tell us that a recursor can replace/simulate another recursor (under a tighter or a looser notion of replacement). But how about the question of when a recursor
cannot
replace another?
The discussion in §
4.2
suggests that

r_{1}\geq r_{2}
does not hold. The next proposition
states the two negative results we know so far:

Prop 16.

r_{1}\not\geq r_{2}
(i.e., it is not the case that
r_{1}\geq r_{2}
)
and
r_{2}\not\geq r_{4}
(i.e., it is not the case that
r_{2}\geq r_{4}
).

Proof sketch.

To prove
r_{i}\not\geq r_{j}
, we must provide a
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-model
\mathcal{M}
for which the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-reduct (i.e., the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-model obtained by forgetting the operators from
\Sigma_{j}\setminus\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
) cannot be the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-reduct of any
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-model.

For
r_{1}\not\geq r_{2}
,
we take
the
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model
\mathcal{M}
to have as carrier the set
M=\mathsf{Tr}\cup A
, where
A
consist of all the streams of variables (in
\mathsf{{Var}}^{\mathbb{N}}
) whose sets of occurring variables are infinite.
We let
(t_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of terms such that all are ground (
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t_{i}=\emptyset
)
and mutually distinct. We define
\mathcal{M}
’s operators on
M
by extending the standard term operators from
\mathsf{Tr}
as follows, for any
{\mathit{xs}}\in A
(where
\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}
is the standard stream-map operator and
\mathsf{{rem}}_{y}\,{\mathit{xs}}
removes all occurrences of
y
from
{\mathit{xs}}
):

\bullet

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}{\mathit{xs}}=\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,y\;{\mathit{xs}}=\mathsf{{rem}}_{y}\,{\mathit{xs}}
for any
y\in\mathsf{{Var}}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;{\mathit{xs}}\;t_{i}=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;{\mathit{xs}}_{i}
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;{\mathit{xs}}\;m=t_{0}
for any
m\in M\smallsetminus\{t_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;s\;{\mathit{xs}}=t_{0}
for any
s\in\mathsf{Tr}

\bullet

{\mathit{xs}}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}=\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}\,{\mathit{xs}}
for any
\sigma\in\mathsf{{Perm}}

Note that, on
A
, the free-variable-like and abstraction-like operators are natural, in particular
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}
removes all occurrences of the abstracted variable. On the other hand, the application-like operator is contrived: the only interesting case is
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;{\mathit{xs}}\;t_{i}
, where application emulates the
i
’th projection, retrieving the
i
’th element of the stream
{\mathit{xs}}
; in the other cases application simply returns the ground term
t_{0}
. We can check that
\mathcal{M}
thus defined satisfies the
\mathsf{Props}_{2}
properties.
One the other hand, the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-reduct of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{Tr}\cup A
equipped with the above-defined constructor-like operators, cannot be the reduct of any
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model, i.e., there is no way to define the operators
\_[\_]^{\prime}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{Tr}\cup A
that, together with
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
, make it a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model. Indeed, if such operators

\_[\_]^{\prime}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}
existed, then the
\mathsf{Props}_{1}
axioms would imply that

\_[\_]^{\prime}
extends the standard permutation operators from
\mathsf{Tr}
and
A
,
and then that
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}\,{\mathit{xs}}=\mathsf{{Var}}
for all
{\mathit{xs}}
, which contradicts
FCB
.

For
r_{2}\not\geq r_{4}
,
we take the
(\Sigma_{4},\mathsf{Props}_{4})
-model
\mathcal{M}
to have as carrier the set
M=\mathsf{Tr}\cup\{a\}
(where
a\not\in\mathsf{Tr}
), i.e., to consist of terms plus an additional element
a
. Let
x
be a fixed variable. We define
\mathcal{M}
’s operators on
M
by extending the standard term operators from
\mathsf{Tr}
as follows:

\bullet

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}a=\mathsf{{Var}}
(the set of all variables)

\bullet

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,y\;a=\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,y\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)
for any
y\in\mathsf{{Var}}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;a\;a=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;a\;t=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;t
for any
t\in\mathsf{Tr}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;t\;a=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;t\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)
for any
t\in\mathsf{Tr}

\bullet

a[z_{1}\hskip-0.86108pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}}=\mathsf{{Vr}}\,(x[z_{1}\hskip-0.86108pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])
for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathsf{{Var}}

Thus, the free variables of
a
are the entire set of variables, and the constructor and swapping operators on
a
yield the same results as for
\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
, i.e., have
\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
act in lieu of
a
.
We can check that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
. On the other hand, the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-reduct of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{Tr}\cup\{a\}
equipped with the above-defined constructor-like operators, cannot be the reduct of any
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model, i.e., there is no way to define the operators
\_[\_]^{\prime}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{Tr}\cup\{a\}
that, together with
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
, make it a
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model.
Indeed, if such operators
\_[\_]^{\prime}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}
existed,
then the axioms in
\mathsf{Props}_{2}
would imply that

\_[\_]^{\prime}
extends the standard permutation operator on
\mathsf{Tr}
, and
also that
\_[\sigma]^{\prime}
is bijective on
\mathsf{Tr}\cup\{a\}
for any permutation
\sigma
; so the only possibility is that
a[\sigma]^{\prime}=a
for any
\sigma
; this together with
PmAp
would imply that
(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,a\;a)[\sigma]^{\prime}=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(a[\sigma]^{\prime})\;(a[\sigma]^{\prime})=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,a\;a
, i.e.,
(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x))[\sigma]=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)
, which is false for any
\sigma
that modifies
x
.
∎

Note that, if we write
>
for the strict version of
\geq
(defined as

r>r^{\prime}
iff
r\geq r^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}\not\geq r
), then assuming
r\geq r^{\prime}
, a negative
result
r^{\prime}\not\geq r
is a strictness result
r>r^{\prime}
. So from Thm.
12
, Prop.
16
and Thm.
15

we have
r_{2}>r_{1}
but
r_{2}\cong r_{1}
, and also
r_{4}>r_{2}

but
r_{4}\cong r_{2}
.
We do not yet have negative/strictness results across the board, in particular,
none for
\gtrsim
.

5.
The coinductive spectrum

Next we will shift focus from the standard terms with bindings discussed so far, which were defined inductively,
to (possibly) infinitary non-well-founded terms with bindings, defined
coinductively
, where the constructors can be applied an infinite number of times. Unlike with the inhabitants of standard coinductive datatypes, we will still identify terms modulo
\alpha
-equivalence. Rather than recursion, we will now study
corecursion
, that is, mechanisms for defining functions having terms not as source domain, but as target domain (codomain).
Building on the experience of having handled the recursors,
we will now take a more direct route, and at a faster pace:
After recalling infinitary terms (§
5.1
),
we introduce abstract epi-corecursors (§
5.2
),
then delve into the spectrum of nominal corecursor instances, connect with pre-existing nominal corecursors, and establish a hierarchy
(§
5.3
).

5.1.
Infinitary terms with bindings

Let
\mathsf{{Var}}
be a set of variables whose cardinality is
\aleph_{1}
, the first uncountable cardinal.
(Any uncountable regular cardinal would do—we only care about the existence of fresh variables for any term.)
The set
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
of
infinitary
\lambda
-terms
,
iterms
for short, is defined by the same grammar as before,

t::=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x\mid\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2}\mid\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t
,
but interpreted
coinductively
, i.e., allowing an infinite number of constructors. For example,

\;\mathinner{.\mskip 3.0mu.\mskip 3.0mu.}\,(\mathsf{{Ap}}\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\,x_{n}\,(\mathinner{.\mskip 3.0mu.\mskip 3.0mu.}(\mathsf{{Ap}}\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\,x_{1}\,(\mathsf{{Vr}}\,x_{0}))\,(\mathsf{{Vr}}\,x_{1}))\mathinner{.\mskip 3.0mu.\mskip 3.0mu.}))\,(\mathsf{{Vr}}\,x_{n}))\,\mathinner{.\mskip 3.0mu.\mskip 3.0mu.}\;

is an iterm, infinitely alternating abstractions and applications.
Similarly to terms, iterms are equated modulo
\alpha
.

In more detail, the above definition means: One first defines the set
\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
of

pre-iterms
to be (co)freely generated by the grammar
p::=\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x\mid\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}\mid\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p
under the coinductive interpretation, i.e., under the assumption that constructors can be applied infinitely.
Thus,
\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
is a standard coinductive datatype, given by the final coalgebra of the functor on sets taking, on objects, any set
A
to

\mathsf{{Var}}+A\times A+\mathsf{{Var}}\times A
(and operating on morphisms as expected;
App.
E.1

gives full details).
Then one defines the
\alpha
-equivalence relation

\equiv\;:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
coinductively, proves that it
is an equivalence, and defines
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
by quotienting
\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
to it, i.e., takes
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}=\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}/\equiv
.
Finally, one proves that the pre-iterm constructors are compatible with
\equiv
,
which allows to define the constructors on iterms:

\mathsf{{Vr}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}

and
\mathsf{{Lm}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
.
We will focus on iterms, forgetting about pre-iterms.

The iterms have been
studied in the context of
\lambda
-calculus denotational semantics,
e.g.,
the Böhm, Lévy-Longo and Berarducci trees of a
\lambda
-term
(
Barendregt 1984
)
. A bottom element
\bot
is often included in the iterm grammar, but we omit it here since it would be entirely passive in our results.

We also consider the usual operators (just like in the inductive case), namely
(capture-avoiding) substitution

\_[\_\,/\_]:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
,
(capture-avoiding) renaming

\_[\_\,/\_]:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
,
swapping

\_[\_\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
,
permutation
\_[\_]:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Perm}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
,
free-variables
{{\mathsf{FV}}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathsf{{Var}})
,
and freshness
\_\#\_:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
.

Finally, for any set
A
, let
{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(A)
denote the set of nonempty subsets of
A
.
We consider the iterm
destructor
,
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty})
, defined as follows, where we write
{{\mathsf{V}}}
,
{{\mathsf{A}}}
and
{{\mathsf{L}}}
for the three injections into the sum type
\mathsf{S}=\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty})
(so that

{{\mathsf{V}}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{S}
,

{{\mathsf{A}}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{S}
and

{{\mathsf{L}}}:{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathsf{S}
):

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{{\mathsf{V}}}\;x,&\mbox{if $t=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x$}\\
{{\mathsf{A}}}\,(t_{1},t_{2}),&\mbox{if $t=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2}$}\\
{{\mathsf{L}}}\,\{(x,t^{\prime})\mid\mbox{$t=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t^{\prime}$}\},&\mbox{otherwise (i.e., if $t$ is a $\mathsf{{Lm}}$-abstraction)}\\
\end{array}\right.\vskip-2.15277pt

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}
is the dual of the constructors, peeling off the last constructor from an iterm and returning
its arguments.
1
1

1

See page
5.3
for a discussion of alternative types for the destructor and destructor-like operators.

It is similar to the destructors for standard datatypes, except that on
\mathsf{{Lm}}
-abstractions it is nondeterministic.
This is because the
\mathsf{{Lm}}
constructor is not injective and therefore an iterm
t
could have been built in
different ways using
\mathsf{{Lm}}
.
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}
considers all these ways, i.e., returns the set of all pairs
(x,t^{\prime})
such that
t
has the form
\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t^{\prime}
.
We thus have:
t=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t^{\prime}\!\allowbreak\mathrel{\;\Leftarrow\penalty\kern-6.88889pt\Rightarrow\;}\allowbreak\!\exists K.\;{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K\mbox{ and }(x,t^{\prime})\in K
.
For iterms (and for terms too, where the destructor is defined in the same way), destructor and constructors are two faces of the same coin.
But since the models for corecursion will have to emulate the destructor,
we will look at
destructor-based (re)formulations of iterm properties.

Of the basic properties of terms listed in Fig.
2
, all except for the last group (the nominal-logic specific properties) also hold for iterms, so we will consider some of them in the context of iterms as well. The properties in this last group
are tied to the finiteness of a term’s free variables; for them to become true for iterms,
we must replace “(in)finite” with “(un)countable”.

Moreover,
Fig.
6
collects destructor-based iterm counterparts of some term properties from Fig.
2
.
Often, these are just (equivalent) destructor-based reformulations of the constructor-based properties. For example, this is the case of
SwVr
∞
,

∞
,

versus

.
Corecursion-relevant properties of
iterms. We only list properties that are counterparts of those from Fig.
2
involving constructors
and finiteness conditions. As for the others,
namely the algebraic properties of the operators,
their formulation does not change, so we will use the same notation.
For example,
SwId
from Fig.
2
denotes a property that makes sense not only for terms but also for iterms (and will, in due course, for our corecursor models too).

However, sometimes we reformulate not the original property from Fig.
2
, but a converse (or “almost converse”) of it.
For example, the converse of

SwCg
from Fig.
2
is:

\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x_{1}\;t_{1}=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x_{2}\;t_{2}
implies that there exists
z
such that
z\not\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
,

z\;\#\;t_{1},t_{2}
, and

t_{1}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]=t_{2}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{2}]
.
This converse does hold for terms, and for iterms as well. However, we prefer to consider a weaker version of it:
\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x_{1}\;t_{1}=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x_{2}\;t_{2}
implies that there exists
z
such that (
z=x_{1}
or
z\;\#\;t_{1}
), (
z=x_{2}
or
z\;\#\;t_{2}
),
and

t_{1}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]=t_{2}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{2}]
.
The latter, reformulated using destructor notation, is exactly
SwCg
∞
from Fig.
6
.
The reason why we prefer a weaker version (here due to a weaker conclusion) is the same as why
we preferred a weaker version of
SwCg
in the inductive case (there, due to a stronger hypothesis): because, to make the (co)recursors
as expressive as possible,
we want the models to have axioms as weak as possible. Sometimes we include in Fig.
6
two different destructor-based counterparts of a
constructor-based property, e.g.,
RnBvr
∞
and
RnBvr
{}_{\infty}^{\prime}
for
RnBvr
.

Save for the finite vs. countable nuance in the last group, all properties in Figs.
2
and
6
hold
for both terms and iterms. Their selection
becomes relevant when
regarding them as properties of models.
The duality between the Fig.
2
and Fig.
6
properties, which informs the naming of the latter, is neither perfect nor fully systematic. But this naming will allow us to draw parallels.

5.2.
Epi-corecursors

We introduce abstract epi-corecursors as a natural dual of epi-recursors. The idea is the same:
A definition of a morphism in a base category is underpinned by adding more structure coming from an extended category. The difference is that the base object is now not the source, but the target of the to-be-defined morphism, and the underpinning occurs not via initiality but via finality.

Def 17.

An
epi-corecursor
is a tuple
{\mathit{cr}}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},J,R)
where:

•

\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is a category called
the base category

\bullet

T
is an object in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
called
the base object

•

\underline{\mathcal{C}}
is a category called
the extended category

\bullet

J
is a final object in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}

•

R:\underline{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is a functor such that
R\;J=T

Just like for epi-recursors,
in typical epi-corecursor examples
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
will be categories of models, with the models in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
having more structure than those in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
, and

R
will be a structure-forgetting functor.
To define a morphism
g:B\rightarrow T
in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
(where
B
is some object in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
) using an epi-corecursor
{\mathit{cr}}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},J,R)
, we (1) extend
B
to an object
C
in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
(with
R\;C=B
) yielding a morphism
!_{C,\,J}:C\rightarrow J
in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
from the finality of
J
,
then (2) take
g
to be
R\;!_{C,\,J}
, the restriction of
!_{C,\,J}
to
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
.
Thus, we call a morphism
g:B\rightarrow T

definable by the
epi-corecursor
{\mathit{cr}}
if
g=R\,!_{C,\,J}
for some extension
C
of
B
.

5.3.
A hierarchy of nominal corecursors

To discuss concrete nominal corecursors, we slightly adapt the notions of signature and model
used for nominal recursors from §
3.2
. Namely, we use the same notions
except that we replace the constructor symbols
{\mathsf{vr}},{\mathsf{ap}},{\mathsf{lm}}
and their interpretations with
a destructor symbol
{{\mathsf{dest}}}
, interpreted accordingly.
All signatures
\Sigma
now extend not the constructor signature
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}=\{{\mathsf{vr}},{\mathsf{ap}},{\mathsf{lm}}\}
, but the destructor signature

\Sigma_{\mathsf{dtor}}=\{{{\mathsf{dest}}}\}
. A
\Sigma
-model
\mathcal{M}
has a carrier set
M
, interprets
the signature’s non-destructor symbols
as described in §
3.2
, and interprets
{{\mathsf{dest}}}
as an operation

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}:M\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+M\times M+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times M)
.
The iterm
\Sigma
-model
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma)
is the
\Sigma
-model whose carrier set is

\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}

and whose operations and relations are the standard ones for iterms (discussed in §
5.1
).

The notion of morphism of
\Sigma
-models
g:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is defined like in §
3.2
, but
replacing commutation with the constructors by sub-commutation with the destructor:

(1_{\mathsf{{Var}}}+g\times g+{\mathsf{image}}(1_{\mathsf{{Var}}}\times g))\,({{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}m)\sqsubseteq{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(g\;m)
for all
m
in the carrier set
M
.
The above relation
\sqsubseteq
on
\mathsf{{Var}}+M^{\prime}\times M^{\prime}+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times M^{\prime})

is defined by taking
u\sqsubseteq v
to mean that:
either
u={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x=v
for some
x
;
or
u={{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})=v
for some
m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime}
;
or
u={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
,
v={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K^{\prime}
and
K\subseteq K^{\prime}
for some
K,K^{\prime}
. Thus, the
sub
-commutation shows
in the abstraction case (which is nondeterministic), where we allow inclusion instead of equality.
To see why sub-commutation is the natural condition here,
note that for a morphism that targets iterms,

g:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma)
, it is equivalent to the conjunction of the following three conditions: (1)
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
;
(2)
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1},m_{2})
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(g\;m_{1})\;(g\;m_{2})
;
(3)
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
and
(x,m^{\prime})\in K
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;(g\;m^{\prime})
.

Our nominal (epi-)corecursors will underpin corecursive definitions having
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{dtor}})
as target
model by considering extensions of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{dtor}}
to larger signatures
\Sigma
, along with certain axiomatizations of
\Sigma
-models given by subsets of the properties in Fig.

(interpreted not on iterms, but on
\Sigma
-models).

(perm/free)

,

,

,

,

,

,

,

,

,

,
