Fig
.
7
.
Sets of properties underlying different nominal corecursors.
The highlighted properties are ones that turned out to be redundant in the
analogous nominal recursor, but must be added back for the corecursor.

Previous work
(
Kurz
et al. 2012
;
Blanchette et al. 2019
)

discovered corecursive counterparts of two nominal recursors.
Next we show that this is a quite pervasive phenomenon:

Thm 18.

Consider the eight choices, for
i\in\{1,2,3,5,6,7,8,9\}
, of
tuples
{\mathit{cr}}_{i}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{i},\allowbreak J_{i},R_{i})

given by the sets of properties
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
shown
in Fig.
7
.
Namely (analogously to what we assumed in Thm.
9
),
we assume that
\Sigma_{i}
consists of the
operation and relation
symbols
occurring in
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
, and:

•

\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is the category of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{dtor}}
-models and
T=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{dtor}})

•

\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
is the category of
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models and

J_{i}
is
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{i})

•

R_{i}:\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
is the forgetful functor sending
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models to their underlying
\Sigma_{\mathsf{dtor}}
-models

Then
{\mathit{cr}}_{i}
is an epi-corecursor.
In particular,
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{i})
is the final
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-model.

Next we unpack Thm.
18
’s statements of epi-corecursion principles,
exploring the connections with Thm.
9
’s
nominal epi-recursors.
We used for the corecursors the same names as for the recursors to which they roughly correspond—although, as we
will discuss, a corecursor will often “inherit” axioms from two different recursors. (We do not have a
{\mathit{cr}}_{4}
corecursor because the axioms specific to
r_{4}
were mixed into
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
and
{\mathit{cr}}_{6}
; either of
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
and
{\mathit{cr}}_{6}
could have alternatively been named “
{\mathit{cr}}_{4}
”.)

{\mathit{cr}}_{1}
and
{\mathit{cr}}_{3}
are corecursors in the style of nominal logic.
Like their recursor counterparts
r_{1}
and
r_{3}
, they
have the free-variable (support) operator completely determined from permutation (via
FvDPm
∞
), or alternatively swapping (via
FvDSw
∞
). However, these corecursors are not strictly speaking nominal-logic based, because this determination of free-variables involves not finiteness, but countability.
Another difference between
{\mathit{cr}}_{1}
/
{\mathit{cr}}_{3}
and
r_{1}
/
r_{3}
is that the freshness condition for binders
FCB
(or anything analogous to it) is no longer needed; but instead we need the (corecursive counterpart of) the bound-variable renaming axiom which was specific to the more expressive recursor
r_{5}
—in permutation or swapping form (
PmBvr
∞
or
SwBvr
∞,2
).
Thus, when switching from recursion to corecursion, the nominal-logic style definitional principles trade
FCB
for
PmBvr
∞
or
SwBvr
∞,2
; they
are the only ones
not
to become axiomatically heavier during this switch.

The
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
corecursor requires both the algebraic properties of permutation and freshness specific to
r_{2}
(
PmId
,
PmCp
and
PmFv
) and the bound-variable renaming property specific to
r_{5}
(converted from swapping to permutation form,
PmBvr
∞
).
The situation is similar for
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
, the swapping-based counterpart of
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
, which gets axioms from both
r_{4}
(with freeness
converted to freshness) and
r_{5}
. All these are in sharp contrast to the recursion case, where, at the recursor
r_{5}
, bound-variable renaming (
SwBvr
) was the only axiom needed (in addition to the “unavoidable” ones describing the
interaction of constructors with the other operators).
Similarly to
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
which “descends” from
r_{4}
and
r_{5}
,

{\mathit{cr}}_{6}
“descends” from
r_{4}
and
r_{6}
.
Unlike in the recursive case where
r_{4}
did not need
SwCp
and turned out not to need
SwId
and
SwIv
either, here all three axioms,
SwId
,
SwIv
and
SwCp
, are actually needed by its corecursor “descendants”
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
and
{\mathit{cr}}_{6}
. Additionally
{\mathit{cr}}_{6}
requires
FvSw
, another axiom we had discovered to be redundant for
r_{4}
.
Thus, for the principles discussed in this paragraph, the axiomatizations become heavier when switching from recursion to corecursion, because: (1) axioms from different recursors now need to be joined, and (2) previous axioms that were seen to be redundant for recursors must be added back to their corecursor counterparts.

As for the substitution- and renaming-based principles
{\mathit{cr}}_{7}
,
{\mathit{cr}}_{8}
and
{\mathit{cr}}_{9}
, their axiomatizations also become heavier in a similar way,
in that both algebraic axioms (e.g.,
RnId
,
RnIm
,
RnCh
,
RnCm
) and bound-variable renaming axioms must be present. But their axiomatizations are even heavier, because they feature (1)
two versions of the bound-variable renaming axioms (e.g.,
RnBvr
∞
and
RnBvr
{}_{\infty}^{\prime}
as opposed to just
RnBvr
∞
) as well as (2) countable support (
FSupFr
∞
). Roughly speaking, these additional axioms are needed to make corecursion go through (i.e., establish finality of the iterm model) because, substitution/renaming not commuting unconditionally with abstractions, stronger bound-variable avoidance facilities must be supplied by an (arbitrary) model;
this was not a problem for recursors, where fresh induction on (concrete) terms could handle that elegantly.

Specific to the substitution corecursor
{\mathit{cr}}_{7}
is that it features, for the variable case, not only
the destructor freshness axiom
FrVr
∞
, but also its
constructor
counterpart
FrVr
, and the implicit requirement that the signature
\Sigma_{7}
contains the variable-constructor symbol
{\mathsf{vr}}
. So a
\Sigma_{7}
-model
\mathcal{M}
has, in addition to the destructor
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\!
, a variable-constructor-like operator
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
; the two are required to act as mutual inverses by the following axiom
VrInv
(which, due its hybrid nature, fits neither Fig.
2
nor Fig.
6
):

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
if and only if
t=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
.
This monad-like
variable-injection setting is needed to accommodate the substitution of arbitrary elements
m\in M
for variables
x
.

Connection with previous corecursors.

Thm.
18
recovers, and slightly improves on, the two existing nominal corecursors from the literature we are aware of:
that developed by
Kurz
et al. 2012
for
\lambda
-terms and extended
by
Kurz
et al. 2013
to functors on nominal sets, and that developed by
Blanchette et al. 2019
in a functorial framework covering complex binders. Next we discuss these corecursors’ instantiations to the syntax of
\lambda
-calculus.
The Blanchette et al. corecursor
corresponds to
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
almost exactly, with the only difference that it assumes
FvPm
which is not needed. (See §
F.2
.)

Designed for nominal logic, the Kurz et al. corecursor
assumes finite support, and targets
not the entire
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
but the subset
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
of finitely supported iterms.
Their corecursor can be obtained from our

{\mathit{cr}}_{1}
by noting that, if we assume the source model
\mathcal{M}
to satisfy finite support (
FSupFv
), then
the image of the unique morphism
g:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{1})

guaranteed by
{\mathit{cr}}_{1}
is included in
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}^{\prime}
(thanks to
g
’s preservation of free variables). So we obtain a unique morphism from
\mathcal{M}
to the submodel of
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{1})
with carrier set
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}^{\prime}
, i.e., the term model of Kurz et al.

The above summary ignores one technicality: The
Kurz et al. destructor
does not have type
M\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+M\times M+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times M)
like ours, but
M\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+M\times M+[\mathsf{{Var}}]M
,
where

[\mathsf{{Var}}]M
is the nominal set of abstractions, obtained by quotienting
\mathsf{{Var}}\times M
to an
\alpha
-like equivalence relation
\sim
defined by
(x,m)\!\sim\!(x^{\prime},m^{\prime})
iff

m[z\leftrightarrow x]^{\mathcal{M}}=m[z\leftrightarrow x^{\prime}]^{\mathcal{M}}
for some fresh
z
.
Since
[\mathsf{{Var}}]M
consists of
\sim
-equivalence classes, we have

[\mathsf{{Var}}]M\subseteq{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times M)
, so the only difference is that
our destructor has a less constrained codomain. But our
{\mathit{cr}}_{1}
axiom
PmBvr
∞

constrains the elements of
{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times M)
from the image of the
destructor to contain
mutually
\sim
-equivalent items. If we also added

PmBvr
{}_{\infty}^{\prime}
to the axiomatization of
{\mathit{cr}}_{1}
, we would further constrain these to be entire
\sim
-equivalence classes, obtaining exactly the
Kurz et al. models. Hence, due to its models being less constrained,
{\mathit{cr}}_{1}
is (slightly) more expressive than the Kurz et al. corecursor.

A note on nominal abstractions.

The above recalled abstractions are a standard concept in nominal logic
(
Gabbay and Pitts 1999
)
, and using abstractions as primitives is a valid alternative when introducing nominal recursors and corecursors. For the recursors, the
\mathsf{{Lm}}
-constructor in models would have type
[\mathsf{{Var}}]M\rightarrow M
rather than
\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M\rightarrow M
.
However, like the authors of the nominal recursors reviewed in §
2.2
, we too favor the abstraction-free (hence quotient-free) (co)recursors, and this is for two reasons. First, they are likely easier to deploy:
During a recursive definition, it seems inconvenient for the user to have to provide an operator in
[\mathsf{{Var}}]M\rightarrow M
, which usually requires making a choice and showing that the choice is immaterial; providing instead a “free” operator in
\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M\rightarrow M
and verifying an additional axiom (such as
SwBvr
) seems more manageable. Second,
they can be more expressive than their abstraction-based alternatives. For example, most of the recursors in Thm.
9
do not require swapping/permutation to have the algebraic properties needed for
\sim
to be an equivalence, so quotienting is not an option unless we strengthen the model axiomatization, thus
placing a higher proof burden on the user.
Admittedly, these advantages are less consequential when talking about corecursors, where the relevant algebraic properties
are required across the board.

Comparing expressiveness.

We use a strength relation that is similar to that from our “head-to-head” comparison of epi-recursors (in §
4.1
):
Given epi-corecursors
{\mathit{cr}}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},J,R)
and
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}=\allowbreak(\underline{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},J^{\prime},R^{\prime})
,
we call
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}
stronger than
{\mathit{cr}}
, written
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}\geq{\mathit{cr}}
, if
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}
can define everything that

{\mathit{cr}}
can, in that:
for all objects
B
in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
and
g:B\rightarrow T
,
g
definable by
{\mathit{cr}}
implies
g
definable by
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}
. Again, we write
{\mathit{cr}}\equiv{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}
to state that
{\mathit{cr}}
and
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}
have equal strengths, i.e., both

{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}\geq{\mathit{cr}}
and
{\mathit{cr}}\geq{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}
hold.

Thm 19.

The epi-corecursors from Thm.
18
(and Fig.
7
) compare as follows w.r.t. expressiveness:

{\mathit{cr}}_{2}\equiv{\mathit{cr}}_{5}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{6}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{3}\equiv{\mathit{cr}}_{1}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{8}
and
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{9}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{7},{\mathit{cr}}_{8}
.

Let us discuss this hierarchy in connection with
the recursor hierarchy from Thm.
12
:

Permutation versus swapping.
Recall that, in the recursor hierarchy, choosing between permutation and swapping
was consequential to expressiveness as soon as we no longer assumed the tight coupling between freeness/freshness and swapping/permutation; namely, for the tight-coupling recursors
r_{1}
and
r_{3}
we had
r_{1}\equiv r_{3}
,
but for the for loose-coupling recursors
r_{2}
and
r_{4}

we only had
r_{4}\geq r_{2}
.
But on corecursors this nuance disappears: Swapping is now as expressive as permutation in both the
tight-coupling (
{\mathit{cr}}_{1}\equiv{\mathit{cr}}_{3}
)
and loose-coupling (
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}\equiv{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
) cases. This is because for swapping-based corecursors we cannot dispense with
the algebraic axioms
SwId
,

SwIv
and
SwCp
, which are sufficient to ensure the extension of swapping to a (well-behaved) permutation operator.

Congruence versus bound-variable renaming.
Recall that, for recursors, the congruence axiom
SwCg
led to higher expressiveness than the bound-variable renaming axiom
SwBvr
, yielding
r_{6}\geq r_{5}
.
And this was because (in the presence of
other mild axioms)
SwBvr
implies
SwCg
.
The same is true here for corecursors, in that
SwBvr
∞
implies
SwCg
∞
. However, in the presence of the other
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
axioms,
SwBvr
∞
is sufficient for proving a corecursion principle; whereas
SwCg
∞
is not, unless we add the additional axiom
FrSw
(which is not needed by
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
). And if we assume
FrSw
then

SwCg
∞
also implies
SwBvr
∞
. In short, congruence-based corecursion requires
FrSw
, and as such is less expressive than
bound-variable renaming-based corecursion, meaning that the hierarchy gets shifted, with
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{6}
.

Finiteness versus countability.
Proving
r_{2}\geq r_{1}
relied on the fact that, in a pre-nominal set (i.e., a model satisfying
PmId
,
PmCp
) equipped with equivariant constructors (satisfying
PmVr
,
PmAp
,
PmLm
) and assuming
FCB
, if we define freshness from permutation using finiteness (via
FvDPm
), then this freshness operator behaves well w.r.t. the constructors (satisfies
FrVr
,
FrAp
,
FrLm
).
This also works if we replace “finite” with “countable” and the constructors with the destructor, and use
PmBvr
∞
instead of
FCB
, which shows why we also have
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{1}

(and similarly for the swapping-based versions).

Symmetric versus asymmetric operators, second round.

Recall that, in the strict “head-to-head” comparison relation, recursors based on
symmetric operators (swapping and permutation)
were incomparable to those based on
asymmetric ones (renaming and substitution), but only a laxer comparison
deemed the symmetric ones more expressive.
But in the case of corecursors, the symmetric ones emerge as more expressive already in a head-to-head comparison. This is not too surprising if we recall the reason why symmetric-operator recursors eventually emerged as more expressive: because, if the model has finite support (which in the laxer criterion was possible by taking the minimal submodel), then swapping becomes definable from renaming.
Here, our asymmetric-operator based models already have countable support (which, as discussed, seems necessary for corecursion), hence can also define swapping from renaming similarly to how this is done in the finite-support case.

Laxer comparison relation?
It is worth asking whether (1) an analogue of the laxer comparison relation we introduced for epi-recursors is available for epi-corecursors, and whether (2) it would yield any
flattening of the corecursor hiererchy
(analogous to Thm
15
). While the answer to the first question is clearly ’yes’ because a perfectly dual concept applies to epi-corecursors,
to the second question we are inclined to answer
‘no’:
Now we would not be able to use submodels, but something akin to quotient models, and quotienting tends to not preserve (let alone strengthen)
our axiomatizations.

Summary.
Nominal corecursors can be construed and compared as epi-corecursors, following a similar methodology to that for nominal recursors. A corecursor axiomatization
corresponds to one or two recursor axiomatizations via identical and quasi-dual axioms. The corecursor axiomatizations are heavier.
We have a corecursor hierarchy that partly matches the strict-relation (
\geq
) recursor hierarchy but is more fine-grained, in particular it already subsumes asymmetric-operator principles to the symmetric-operator ones without the need for a laxer comparison relation (in the style of
\gtrsim
).

6.
Mechanized Results

We have mechanized in Isabelle/HOL the recursion theorem (Thm.
9
),
the two recursor comparison theorems (Thms.
12
and
15
),
the two negative (strictness) results on recursor comparison (Prop.
16
),
the corecursion theorem (Thm.
18
), and
the corecursor comparison theorem (Thm.
19
).
What we have
not
mechanized are the abstract criteria for comparing epi-recursors, namely
Props.
11
and
14
.
In our mechanized results, rather than invoking these criteria, we have inlined their content on a need basis.

The mechanization is available as an archive
(
Popescu 2023a
)
,
and is extensively documented
in App.
J
.
It uses Isabelle’s structuring mechanisms called
locales

(
Kammüller et al. 1999
;
Ballarin 2014
)
to represent the
model axiomatizations, and uses sublocale relationships for the transformations between these axiomatizations that underlie the
expressiveness comparisons.

We have also provided a top-level, locale-free reformulation of the
mechanized results, which
match closely the statements from the paper, and whose inspection does not require knowledge of locales.
The end results about recursors, Thms.
9
,
12
and
15

and Prop.
16
, are mechanized in homonymous Isabelle theories,
located in the archive’s directory

Stripped
\_
Down/LocaleFree
\_
versions
:

•

Thm.
9
is mechanized in the Isabelle theory

Theorem9
.
That theory contains the definitions of the epi-recursor structure for each
of the nine recursors
r_{i}
, and proofs that these structures indeed form epi-recursors,
e.g., their components are categories, functors etc. The initiality theorems are named
init
\_
I
i
where

i
is a number between
1
and
9
.

•

Thm.
12
is mechanized in the Isabelle theory

Theorem12
, where the main formal theorems are named r
i\_
ge
\_
r
j
(formalizing
r_{i}\geq r_{j}
) for the relevant choices of

i
and
j
.

•

Thm.
15
is mechanized in the Isabelle theory

Theorem15
, where the main formal theorems are named r
i\_
quasi
\_
ge
\_
r
j
(formalizing
r_{i}\gtrsim r_{j}
), again for the relevant choices of

i
and
j
.

•

Prop.
16
is mechanized in the Isabelle theory

Prop16
, where the main formal theorems are named
not
\_
r1
\_
ge
\_
r2
and
not
\_
r2
\_
ge
\_
r4

(formalizing
r_{1}\not\geq r_{2}
and
r_{2}\not\geq r_{4}
).

And similarly for corecursors, in directory

Corecursors/LocaleFree
\_
versions
:

•

Thm.
18
is mechanized in the Isabelle theory

Theorem18
,
which contains the definitions and proofs for the epi-corecursor structure,
including the finality theorems named
final
\_
J
i
.

•

Thm.
19
is mechanized in the Isabelle theory

Theorem19
, where the main formal theorems are named cr
i\_
ge
\_
cr
j
(formalizing
{\mathit{cr}}_{i}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{j}
) for the relevant choices of

i
and
j
.

App.
J.4

gives more details about the locale-free statements of the results.

7.
More Related Work

Definitional packages for syntax with bindings.

A direct application of our results would be on informing the
design of binding-aware definitional packages in proof assistants, in the style of Nominal Isabelle
(
Urban and
Kaliszyk 2012
)
. In addition to our theoretical results on expressiveness, one should also consider the pragmatic aspects of how lightweight the required structure (operations and relations on the target domain) is and how easy the conditions are to solve. Ideally, in a definitional package implementation one should provide the maximally expressive (co)recursor as the core, but also infer from it (via "borrowing") and make available other (co)recursors which may have pragmatic advantages. For example, the recursors
r_{3}
and
r_{8}

are minimalistic in terms of structure.

(Co)recursors in different paradigms.

Binding-aware recursors have also been developed in the other two major
paradigms.
Scope-safe versions of nameless recursion based on category theory have been studied extensively, e.g.,
Fiore
et al. 1999
;
Hofmann 1999
;
Bird and Paterson 1999
;
Altenkirch and
Reus 1999
;
Allais
et al. 2017
;
Kaiser
et al. 2018
.
A nameless recursor is in principle easier to deploy because the constructors are free; the price is additional index-shifting overhead
(
Berghofer and
Urban 2007
)
.
Nameless corecursion has been studied by
Matthes and
Uustalu 2004
, building on previous work by
Aczel et al. 2003
;
Moss 2001
;
Ghani
et al. 2003
.

Hybrid nameless/nominal solutions have also been proposed, notably
the locally named
(
McKinna and
Pollack 1999
;
Pollack
et al. 2012
)

and locally nameless
(
Aydemir et al. 2008
;
Charguéraud 2012
)
representations.

Pitts 2023
introduced locally nameless sets, an algebraic axiomatization of syntax under the locally nameless representation, and characterizes the locally nameless recursor
(
Charguéraud 2012
)
using initiality in a functor category (similarly to recursors in the nameless
setting
(
Fiore
et al. 1999
;
Hofmann 1999
)
).
He also proved that the category of locally nameless sets is isomorphic to that of finitely supported rensets
(
Popescu 2023b
)
and to categories given by other axiomatizations of renaming from the literature
(
Staton 2007
;
Gabbay and
Hofmann 2008
)
; this suggests that the expressive power of the locally nameless recursor might be located in the vicinity of
r_{8}
(which is based on rensets).
On the way to his results, Pitts gave an alternative axiomatization of finitely supported rensets, using instead of
RnCh
a simpler (unconditional) axiom, let us call it
RnCh
’:
t[x_{2}/x_{1}][x_{3}/x_{2}]=t[x_{3}/x_{2}][x_{3}/x_{1}]
. Replacing
RnCh
with
RnCh
’ would yield a recursor
r_{8}^{\prime}
such that
r_{8}\geq r_{8}^{\prime}
(since
RnCh
’ implies
RnCh
in the presence of
RnIm
) and
r_{8}\cong r_{8}^{\prime}
(since the converse implication is true for finitely supported rensets, hence for a suitable minimal submodel).

In
strong HOAS
, as implemented in dedicated logical frameworks

(
Pfenning and
Schürmann 1999
;
Baelde et al. 2014
;
Pientka 2010
)
,
the
\lambda
-constructor has type
(\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr})\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}
. Here, the difficulty with recursion is not the non-freeness of the constructors, but the fact that
binding constructors are not recursable
in the typical well-foundedness manner. Solutions to this have been designed using modality operators
(
Schürmann et al. 2001
)
and contextual types

(
Ferreira and
Pientka 2017
)
.
Recursion mechanisms have also been designed within
weak HOAS

(
Despeyroux
et al. 1995
)
, where the
\lambda
-constructor, having
type
(\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr})\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}
,
is
standardly recursable—yielding a free datatype that contains all terms but also additional entities referred to as “exotic terms”. Partly
due to the exotic terms, this free datatype is not
very helpful for recursively defining useful functions on terms.
But the situation is significantly improved in a variant called
parametric HOAS (PHOAS)

(
Chlipala 2008
)
,
which accommodates recursive definitions
in the style of the semantic-interpretation pattern (§
4.2
).

A nominal/HOAS hybrid can be found in Gordon and Melham’s characterization of the
\lambda
-term datatype
(
Gordon and Melham 1996
)
, which employs the nameful constructors but features weak-HOAS style recursion over
\mathsf{{Lm}}
.
Norrish 2004
inferred his swap/free
recursor
r_{4}
from the Gordon-Melham one.
Weak-HOAS recursion also
has interesting connections with nameless recursion: In presheaf toposes as in
Fiore
et al. 1999
,
Hofmann 1999
and

Ambler
et al. 2003
,
the function space
\mathsf{{Var}}\Rightarrow T
is isomorphic
to the De Bruijn level-shifting transformation applied to
T
; this effectively equates
the weak-HOAS and nameless recursors.

Recursion over non-free datatypes.

Some of the discussed
nominal recursors operate by characterizing terms as the non-free datatype determined as initial model of an equational theory
(
Burris and
Sankappanavar 1981
)
or more generally of a Horn theory
(
Makowsky 1987
)
, employing an infinite number of axioms. In such cases, and ignoring the Barendregt enhancement, nominal recursion
becomes a particular case of
Horn recursion. (This is not true for the nominal-logic recursor
r_{1}
, since

FvDPm
is not a Horn formula.)
Our concept of epi-recursor applies to general Horn recursion as well—provided one identifies a constructor-like subsignature of the given signature, i.e., such that the
initial model of the Horn theory has its carrier generated by its operations. In algebraic specifications, this property is called
sufficient completeness

(
Guttag and
Horning 1978
)
.

The non-free datatypes of sets and bags are degenerate cases of the above, where the constructors form the entire signature.

Tannen and
Subrahmanyam 1991

and
Buneman
et al. 1995

study Horn recursors for these datatypes
when designing database languages.
They prove connections between their axiomatizations that could be captured using our
\geq
relation between epi-recursors.

We thank the reviewers and the artifact reviewers for the careful reading of our paper, and for their insightful comments and suggestions, which have led to improvements both in the text and in the documentation of what has been mechanized.
We gratefully acknowledge support from the EPSRC grant EP/X015114/1 “Safe and secure COncurrent programming for adVancEd aRchiTectures (COVERT)”.

This appendix provides details, proof sketches and extensions for the concepts and results presented in the main paper. Specifically, it provides:

•

some technical lemmas on nominal sets that are relevant for regarding the perm/free recursor as an epi-recursor (App.
A
)

•

some additional examples of functions defined by nominal recursion
(App.
B
)

•

proof sketches for all the stated results on recursors (App.
C
)

•

the description of a uniform way to enhance the recursors with full-fledged recursion and Barendregt’s convention (App.
D
)

•

the definition of infinitary
\lambda
-terms (iterms) and their operators, and a description of the relevant proof principles for them (App.
E
)

•

more details on epi-corecursors and nominal corecursors (App.
F
)

•

proof sketches for all the stated results on corecursors (App.
G
)

•

a discussion of the notion of enhancing corecursors (App.
H
)

•

an example of a function defined by nominal corecursion, namely parallel substitution
(App.
I
)

•

a detailed presentation of our Isabelle mechanization (App.
J
)

Appendix A
More details on nominal sets

Next, we will give details on the justification for the following claim made in the main paper:
r_{1}
is the stripped down version of the perm/fresh recursor, where here “stripped down” refers to removing the Barendregt parameter
X
, i.e., taking
X=\emptyset
.

Consider the following result that gives a more direct description of the support function:

Lemma 20.

(
Pitts 2006
)

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{A}})
be a nominal set. Then, for every
a\in A
, there exists the smallest set
X\subseteq A
that supports
a
, denoted
{{\mathsf{supp}}}^{\mathcal{A}}(a)
.
Moreover, it holds that

{{\mathsf{supp}}}^{\mathcal{A}}(a)=\{x\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid\{y\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}}\not=a\}\mbox{ is infinite}\}
. ∎

In turn, this enables an alternative description of nominal sets:

Lemma 21.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{A}})
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{A}
is a nominal set;

(2)

There exists a (necessarily unique) function
{{\mathsf{FV}}}:A\rightarrow{{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathsf{{Var}})
such that
(A,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{A}},{{\mathsf{FV}}})
satisfies
PmId
,
PmCp
,

FvDPm

and
FSupFv
.

Proof sketch:

(1) implies (2): We take
{{\mathsf{FV}}}
to be
{{\mathsf{supp}}}^{\mathcal{A}}
. Then
PmId
,
PmCp
and
FSupFv
are part of the definition of nominal sets, and
FvDPm
is ensured by Lemma
20
.

(2) implies (1): Thanks to
PmId
and
PmCp
,
\mathcal{A}
is a pre-nominal set.

Next, we show that the operator defined by
FvDPm
is the same as the support operator, i.e., for all
a\in A
,
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a
is the smallest set of variables that supports
a
:

:

-
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a
supports
a
: Let
x,y\in\mathsf{{Var}}\smallsetminus({{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a)
.
If
x=y
, the desired fact,
a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}}=a
, follows from
PmId
. So let us assume
x\not=y
.
Since
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a
, thanks to
FvDPm
we have that the set
\{z\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!z]^{\mathcal{A}}\not=a\}
is finite. Similarly, the set
\{z\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid a[y\!\leftrightarrow\!z]^{\mathcal{A}}\not=a\}
is finite. Then we can find
z\in\mathsf{{Var}}\smallsetminus\{x,y\}
such that

a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!z]^{\mathcal{A}}=a
and
a[y\!\leftrightarrow\!z]^{\mathcal{A}}=a
.
Next, applying the properties of swapping in pre-nominal sets, we have:

a=a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!z]^{\mathcal{A}}[y\!\leftrightarrow\!z]^{\mathcal{A}}=a[x[y\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz]\!\leftrightarrow\!z[y\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz]]^{\mathcal{A}}=a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}}
, as desired.

:

-
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a
is included in any set that supports
a
:
Assume
X\subseteq\mathsf{{Var}}
supports
a
. Let
X^{\prime}=X\cap{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a
. Since both
X
and (as we have just proved)
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a
support
a
, we have that
X^{\prime}
supports
a
. And since,
by
FSupFv
,
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a
is finite, we have that
X^{\prime}
is finite.
To show
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a\subseteq X
, it suffices to show
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a\subseteq X^{\prime}
.
Let
x\in{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;a
, meaning that the set
\{y\mid a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}}\not=a\}
is infinite. By the finiteness of
X^{\prime}
, we obtain
y\notin X^{\prime}
such that
a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}}\not=a
. Then, since
X^{\prime}
supports
a
, it cannot be the case that
x\notin X^{\prime}
. Hence
x\in X^{\prime}
, as desired.

We have thus proved that
\mathcal{A}
is a pre-nominal set and that

{{\mathsf{FV}}}
coincides with
{{\mathsf{supp}}}^{\mathcal{A}}
—which, thanks to
FSupFv
, means that the finite support property holds for
\mathcal{A}
. We obtain that
\mathcal{A}
is a nominal set, as desired. ∎

From Lemma
21
, it follows
that
a nominal set
\mathcal{A}=(A,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{A}})
together with
\emptyset
-supported operations

\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow A
,

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}:A\rightarrow A\rightarrow A
and

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow A\rightarrow A
is the same as a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model.

It remains to show that the properties of the unique function
g:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow A

guaranteed by Thm
1
are the same as those defining

\Sigma_{1}
-morphisms.
Indeed, clauses (1)–(3) in Thm
1
are the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-part of the morphism conditions. Moreover,
g
’s commutation with permutation (in nominal terminology, equivariance) is the same as being supported by
\emptyset
, as a particular case of the following lemma:

Lemma 22.

Let
f:A\rightarrow B
be a function between two nominal sets

\mathcal{A}=(A,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{A}})
and
\mathcal{B}=(B,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{B}})
and
X
a set of variables. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

f
is supported by
X
;

(2)

f(a[\sigma])^{\mathcal{A}}=(f\,a)[\sigma]^{\mathcal{A}}
for all
\sigma\in\mathsf{{Perm}}

such that
{{\mathsf{supp}}}\,\sigma\cap X=\emptyset
.

Proof sketch:

We have the following equivalencies (where
\mathcal{F}
is the pre-nominal set of functions from
A
to
B
):

(1)

iff (by the definition of “supported”)

\forall x,y\notin X.\;f[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{F}}=f

iff (by the definition of swapping for functions)

\forall x,y\notin X.\;\forall a\in A.\;f(a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}})[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{B}}=f\;a

iff (by the idempotency of swapping in
\mathcal{B}
)

\forall x,y\notin X.\;\forall a\in A.\;f(a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}})=(f\;a)[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{B}}

It remains to show that the last property in the above chain of equivalencies is in turn equivalent to (2). It is clearly implied by (2), since it is a particular case of (2) for the permutation
\sigma
being
x\!\leftrightarrow\!y
. Conversely, the fact that it implies (2) follows by induction on the finite set
{{\mathsf{supp}}}\,\sigma
(employing the inductive characterization of finiteness) using the properties of permutation (including that any permutation is a composition of transpositions).
∎

Finally, the preservation of the freshness operator (which is required by the notion of
\Sigma_{1}
-morphism but is not explicitly stated in Thm
1
), is implied by commutation with permutation—more precisely, the following holds:

Lemma 23.

Let
f:A\rightarrow B
be a function between two nominal sets

\mathcal{A}=(A,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{A}})
and
\mathcal{B}=(B,\_[\_]^{\mathcal{B}})
that commutes with permutation (i.e., is equivariant, i.e., is supported by
\emptyset
). Then
{{\mathsf{supp}}}^{\mathcal{B}}(f\;a)\subseteq{{\mathsf{supp}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;a
for all
a\in A
.

Proof sketch:

Thanks to the definition of support, it suffices to check that

{{\mathsf{supp}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;a
supports
f\;a
(in
\mathcal{B}
). Indeed, assume
x,y\notin{{\mathsf{supp}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;a
; then
a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}}=a
, hence
f(a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}})=f\;a
. And since by equivariance
f(a[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{A}})=(f\;a)[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{B}}
, we obtain

(f\;a)[x\!\leftrightarrow\!y]^{\mathcal{B}}=f\;a
, as desired. ∎

This concludes the justification of the fact that
r_{1}
is the stripped down version of the perm/free recursor.

Appendix B
Other examples of nominal recursion

Next we show some more examples of nominal recursion taken from the literature.
In all these examples, checking the necessary properties for the target models, i.e.,
the
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
properties, is completely routine.

Example 24.

(the size (depth) of a term defined using
r_{4}
)

(
Norrish 2004
)

Consider the task of defining the size function on terms,

{{\mathsf{size}}}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.
The desired constructor-based recursive clauses are the following:

(i)

{{\mathsf{size}}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)=1

(ii)
{{\mathsf{size}}}\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})={{\mathsf{size}}}\;t_{1}+{{\mathsf{size}}}\;t_{2}+1

(iii)

{{\mathsf{size}}}\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)={{\mathsf{size}}}\;t+1

To make this work, we add clauses describing the intended behavior of
{{\mathsf{size}}}
with respect to swapping and free-variables:

(iv)

{{\mathsf{size}}}\;(t\,[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty])={{\mathsf{size}}}\;t

(v)

\emptyset\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t

(for this particular definition, this clause is vacuous)

This means
organizing the target domain

\mathbb{N}

as a model
\mathcal{A}=(\mathbb{N},\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}},\_[\_\,\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]^{\mathcal{A}},{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}})
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;x=1\hskip 30.1388pt\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;m\;n=m+n+1\hskip 30.1388pt\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;x\;m=m+1\\
m\,[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty]^{\mathcal{A}}=m\hskip 47.36096pt{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;m=\emptyset\end{array}

After checking that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the properties required by
r_{4}

(i.e.,
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
) we obtain a unique function
{{\mathsf{size}}}
satisfying clauses (i)–(v).

In the following example, we will use as target domain a set
\mathsf{ETr}

of “extended terms”, which are defined like terms but with an
additional constructor
{{\mathsf{Ct}}}\;c
, where
c
ranges over constants from
a set
C
; we will also assume that
C
includes
{\mathsf{ap}}
and
{\mathsf{lm}}
.

Example 25.

(HOAS encoding defined using
r_{7}
)

(
Popescu and
Gunter 2011
)

Consider the task of defining a function

{{\mathsf{enc}}}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow\mathsf{ETr}
that encodes terms into extended terms in a higher-order abstract syntax
(HOAS) fashion. This is a simplified version of HOAS encodings in logical frameworks such as
LF
(
Harper
et al. 1987
)
.
The desired constructor-based recursive clauses are the following:

(i)

{{\mathsf{enc}}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x

(ii)
{{\mathsf{enc}}}\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;({{\mathsf{Ct}}}\;{\mathsf{ap}})\;({{\mathsf{enc}}}\;t_{1}))\;({{\mathsf{enc}}}\;t_{2})

(iii)

{{\mathsf{enc}}}\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;({{\mathsf{Ct}}}\;{\mathsf{lm}})\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;({{\mathsf{enc}}}\;t))

To make this work, we add clauses describing the intended behavior of
{{\mathsf{enc}}}
with respect to substitution and freshness:

(iv)

{{\mathsf{enc}}}\;(t\,[s/x])=({{\mathsf{enc}}}\;t)[({{\mathsf{enc}}}\;s)/x]

(v)

x\,\#\,t
implies
x\,\#\,({{\mathsf{enc}}}\;t)

(Both (iv) and (v) have a stand-alone importance for HOAS encodings.)

This means
organizing the target domain

\mathsf{ETr}

as a model
\mathcal{A}=(\mathsf{ETr},\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}},\_[\_\,/\_]^{\mathcal{A}},\#^{\mathcal{A}})

where
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}
,
\_[\_\,/\_]^{\mathcal{A}}
and
\#^{\mathcal{A}}
are the usual variable-injection,
swapping and freshness on extended terms,
and application- and abstraction-like operators are defined as follows:

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;e_{1}\;e_{2}=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;({{\mathsf{Ct}}}\;{\mathsf{ap}})\;e_{1})\;e_{2}

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;x\;e=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;({{\mathsf{Ct}}}\;{\mathsf{lm}})\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;e)

After checking that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the properties required by
r_{7}

(i.e.,
\mathsf{Props}_{7}
) we obtain a unique function
{{\mathsf{enc}}}
satisfying clauses (i)–(v).

Next we show two examples that use the enhancements
discussed in App.
D
.

Example 26.

(eta normal form using enhanced
r_{4}
)

(
Norrish 2004
)

Consider the task of defining the function

{{\mathsf{enf}}}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
which checks whether a term is in
\eta
-normal form.
We will write
\sf{isAp}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
for the function that checks whether a term is an application,
and
{{\mathsf{getApL}}},{{\mathsf{getApR}}}:\mathsf{Tr}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}
for the functions that return the left- and right- argument
respectively if the term is an application (otherwise it does not matter, e,.g., they return the term itself);
thus,
{{\mathsf{getApL}}}\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})=t_{1}
and
{{\mathsf{getApR}}}\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})=t_{2}
.

The desired constructor-based recursive clauses are the following:

(i)

{{\mathsf{enf}}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)=\mathsf{{True}}

(ii)
{{\mathsf{enf}}}\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})=({{\mathsf{enf}}}\;t_{1}\wedge{{\mathsf{enf}}}\;t_{2})

(iii)

{{\mathsf{enf}}}\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)=({{\mathsf{enf}}}\;t\wedge(\sf{isAp}\;t\wedge{{\mathsf{getApR}}}\;t=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x\rightarrow x\in{{\mathsf{FV}}}({{\mathsf{getApL}}}\;t)))

To make this work, we add clauses describing the intended behavior of
\eta
with respect to swapping and free-variables:

(iv)

\eta\;(t\,[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty])=\eta\;t

(v)

\emptyset\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t

(again, this clause is vacuous here)

This means
organizing the target domain

{\sf Bool}

as a
(X,\Sigma_{4})
-model

\mathcal{A}=({\sf Bool},D=\mathsf{Tr}\times{\sf Bool},\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}},\_[\_\,\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]^{\mathcal{A}},{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}})
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;x=\mathsf{{True}}\hskip 30.1388pt\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;(t_{1},b_{1})\;(t_{2},b_{2})=(b_{1}\wedge b_{2})\\
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;x\;(t,b)=(b\wedge(\sf{isAp}\;t\wedge{{\mathsf{getApR}}}\;t=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x\rightarrow x\in{{\mathsf{FV}}}({{\mathsf{getApL}}}\;t)))\\
(t,b)\,[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty]^{\mathcal{A}}=b\hskip 47.36096pt{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;m=\emptyset\end{array}

After checking that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the properties required by
r_{4}

(i.e., is an
(X,\Sigma_{4})
-model satisfying
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
) we obtain a unique function
\eta
satisfying clauses (i)–(v).

The above definition takes advantage of the full-recursion enhancement,
but did not need the Barendregt enhancement. Indeed, the desired properties,
i.e.,
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
, already hold for the target model
in the stronger form, non-relativized to the finite set of variables
X
.
The next definition is the standard situation where the Barendregt enhancement comes handy.

Example 27.

(substitution defined using enhanced
r_{1}
)

(
Pitts 2006
)

Let
s
and
y
be a fixed term and a fixed variable.
Consider the task of defining the function
{{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}
that takes any term
t
and performs the (capture-free)
substitution of
s
for
y
in
t
.
(Thus,
{{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\,t
will be the same as
t[s/y]
.)
The desired constructor-based recursive clauses are the following:

(i)

{{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\,(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)=(\mbox{if $x=y$ then $s$ else $\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x$})

(ii)

{{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\,(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;({{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\,t_{1})\;({{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\,t_{2})

(iii)

{{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;({{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\,t)
if
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,s\cup\{y\}

To make this work, we add a clause describing the intended behavior of
{{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}
with respect to permutation:

(iv)

({{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\;t)[\sigma]={{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}\,(t[\sigma])
if
{{\mathsf{supp}}}(\sigma)\cap({{\mathsf{FV}}}\,s\cup\{y\})=\emptyset

This means taking
X={{\mathsf{FV}}}\,s\cup\{y\}
and
organizing the target domain

\mathsf{Tr}

as an
(X,\Sigma_{1})
-model

\mathcal{A}=(\mathsf{Tr},D=\mathsf{Tr}\times\mathsf{Tr},\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}},\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}},\_[\_\,\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]^{\mathcal{A}})
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;x=(\mbox{if $x=y$ then $s$ else $\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x$})\hskip 30.1388pt\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;(t_{1}^{\prime},t_{1})\;(t_{2}^{\prime},t_{2})=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2}\\
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}\;x\;(t^{\prime},t)=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t\hskip 30.1388pt(t^{\prime},t)\,[\sigma]^{\mathcal{A}}=t[\sigma]\end{array}

After checking that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies the properties required by
r_{1}

(i.e., is an
(X,\Sigma_{1})
-model satisfying
\mathsf{Props}_{1}
) we obtain a unique function
{{\mathsf{subst}}}_{s,y}
satisfying clauses (i)–(iv).

Note that above we made crucial use of the Barendregt enhancement, but have not used
the full-recursion enhancement (as seen in the fact that the first components of the pairs
are ignored in the definitions of the model operators).

Appendix C
Proof Sketches for the Recursor Results

C.1.
Proof idea for the nominal recursion theorems (Thm.
9
)

Our discussion in §
3.4

shows how
r_{1}
,
r_{4}
,
r_{6}
,
r_{7}
and
r_{8}
coincide with nominal recursors
from the literature—so we are in a position to cite these literature results as justification for their share of Thm.
9
. On the other hand, we cannot cite the literature for the variant epi-recursors
r_{2}
,
r_{3}
,
r_{5}
and
r_{9}
.

We will actually prove Thm.
9
in a uniform way,
taking advantage of the expressiveness comparisons between these recursors. We will infer the recursion theorems for all nine recursors from those of just two of them.
We will come back to this in App.
C.3
.

C.2.
Proofs of the recursor expressiveness comparison results

More detailed proof of Thm.
12
.

When proving each

r_{i}\geq r_{j}
, we instantiate Prop.
11
taking
r^{\prime}=r_{i}
and
r=r_{j}
. So here
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is the category of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models,
\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
that of
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models,
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
that of
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models;
R^{\prime}

is the forgetful functor from
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models, and
R
the forgetful functor from
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models;

B=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}})
,

I^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})
and
I=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{j})
.
In each case, we must define a pre-functor
F:\underline{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}

such that

R^{\prime}\circ F=R
and
F\;I=I^{\prime}
.
This essentially means showing how to transform
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models into
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models in such a manner that
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{j})

becomes
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})
—which gives
F
’s behavior on objects, while on morphisms
F
will be the identity. Each time,
F
will transform models by preserving the carrier set and the constructor-like operators, and possibly defining
(1) permutation-like from swapping like operators or vice versa, (2)
freshness-like operators from
free-variable-like operators, or (3) renaming-like from substitution-like operators;
these definitions are done just like for concrete terms (where, e.g., we can standardly define freshness from free-variables). In each case, the only interesting fact that needs to be checked is that
F
is well-defined on objects: when starting with a
\Sigma_{j}
-model satisfying
\mathsf{Props}_{j}
, the result
\Sigma_{i}
-indeed satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
. Everything else amounts to either well-known or trivial properties. Thus,
F\;I=I^{\prime}
means that the standard inter-definability properties (1)–(3) hold for terms, e.g.,
x\,\#\,t
iff
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t
; and
R^{\prime}\circ F=R
(i.e.,
F
commutes with the forgetful functors to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models) follows immediately from the fact that
F
does not change the carrier set or the constructor-like operators.

Next, we informally discuss these transformations and highlight the intuitions behind them. At the end, we also explain why we believe the stated inequalities are strict (in that the opposite inequalities do not hold), although, with the two exceptions expressed in Props.
16
,
we do not yet have
proofs
for the strictness conjectures.

Proof of
r_{1}\equiv r_{3}
:
Recall that
r_{1}
is the original nominal-logic recursor and
r_{3}
is its variation that uses swapping rather than permutation, the difference being the use of
the identity, involutiveness and compositionality properties for swapping (
SwId
,
SwIv
and
SwCp
) rather than the identity and compositionality for permutation (
PmId
and
PmCp
).
r_{1}\equiv r_{3}
holds because operations with these properties correspond bijectively and functorially to each other, allowing us to move back and forth between
\mathsf{Props}_{1}
-models and
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
-models
(
Pitts 2013
, Section 6.1)
. In one direction, starting with an operation
\_[\_]^{\mathcal{M}}:M\rightarrow\mathsf{{Perm}}\rightarrow M
satisfying
PmId
and
PmCp
, we define
\_[\_\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]^{\mathcal{M}}:M\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M
as its restriction to transposition permutations—and it satisfies SwId, SwIv and

SwCp
. Conversely, starting with an operation
\_[\_\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]^{\mathcal{M}}
satisfying
SwId
,
SwIv
and
SwCp
, we define
\_[\_]^{\mathcal{M}}
by
m[\sigma]=m[x_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty_{1}]\mathinner{.\mskip 3.0mu.\mskip 3.0mu.}[x_{n}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty_{n}]
where
(x_{1},y_{1})\cdot\mathinner{.\mskip 3.0mu.\mskip 3.0mu.}\cdot(x_{n},y_{n})
is any decomposition of
\sigma
into transpositions; thanks to
SwId
,
SwIv
and
SwCp
, we can prove that this definition is independent of the particular decomposition and that
\_[\_]^{\mathcal{M}}
satisfies
PmId
and
PmCp
.

Proof of
r_{2}\geq r_{1}
:

The difference between the two recursors is the following:
r_{1}
requires the definability of the free-variable (support) operator from permutation
FvDPm
, and the freshness condition for binders
FCB
. By contrast,
r_{2}
requires instead that the free-variable operator is related to the constructor by the usual inductive clauses
FvVr
,
FvAp
and
FvLm
, and is related to permutation via
PmFv
.
In particular,
r_{2}
is looser in that it does not require the free-variable operator to be
definable from
permutation, but only to be
related to
permutation by some weaker properties. And indeed, this looseness translates into higher flexibility, because any
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model can be proved to be in particular a
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model, more precisely:

•

PmFv
follows from
FvDPm
in the presence of
PmId
,
PmCp
;

•

FvVr
,
FvAp
and
FvLm
follow from
FvDPm
and
FCB

in the presence of
PmId
,
PmCp
,
PmVr
,
PmAp
,
PmLm
.

Thus,
r_{2}\geq r_{1}
follows from Prop.
11
taking
F
to be the identity functor, more precisely the inclusion functor between the categories
of
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-models and
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-models.
(Since working with permutation is much heavier than working with swapping,
we preferred to do this proof while taking advantage of the permutation-swapping connection. Namely, starting with a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model
\mathcal{M}
, and already knowing from before that
\mathcal{M}
with
its swapping operator corresponding to permutation satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
,
we proved that it also satisfies
SwFv
, as well as
{\small\textsf{FvVr}},{\small\textsf{FvAp}}
and
FvLm
(thus essentially establishing on the way that
r_{4}\geq r_{3}
).
Then, using again the permutation-swapping connection, we inferred
PmFv
from
SwFv
.)

Proof of
r_{4}\geq r_{2}
:
This holds essentially for the same reason why
r_{3}\geq r_{1}
holds, i.e., because the
restriction to transpositions of a permutation operator satisfying
PmId
and
PmCp
will satisfy
SwId
,
SwIv
and
SwCp
, together with the fact
PmFv
implies
SwFv
along this transposition-restriction operator.
Thus,
r_{4}\geq r_{2}
follows from Prop.
11
taking
F
to be the same functor
as that used for
r_{3}\geq r_{1}
.

But note that, this time, we don’t have
SwCp
on the swapping side, in that
r_{4}
does not require
SwCp
—so the converse construction described above when proving
r_{1}\geq r_{1}
(using decomposition into transpositions) does not hold, forbidding us from establishing the converse inequality
r_{2}\geq r_{4}
; and indeed, we later (in Prop.
16
) prove that
r_{2}\not\geq r_{4}
.
This reveals a perhaps unexpected phenomenon: that swapping-based recursors can be strictly more expressive than their permutation-based counterparts, as is indeed the case of
r_{4}
versus
r_{2}
(though not of
r_{3}
versus
r_{1}
).

Proof of
r_{5}\geq r_{4}
:
This follows by applying
Prop.
11
with the functor
F
that transforms the
free-variable operator into a freshness operator using negation. Indeed, save for the straightforwardly corresponding free-variable operator’s
properties
FvVr
,
FvAp
and
FvLm
and their freshness counterparts

FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm
,
the only difference between
r_{4}
and
r_{5}
is the replacement of

SwFv

with

SwBvr
.
And the latter follows from the former in the presence of
FvLm
.
2
2

2

Norrish 2004
’s original recursor also assumed, for the swapping-like operator, equivariance (
FvSw
) and two algebraic properties (
SwId
,
SwIv
), but these turn out to not be needed for his recursion theorem to hold. We discovered this redundancy when proving
r_{5}\geq r_{4}
and realizing that
FvSw
,
SwId
,
SwIv
are not needed to reduce
r_{4}
to
r_{5}
—which means that
r_{5}
can “lend” its recursion theorem to this axiom-lighter version of
r_{4}
.

Going in the other direction,
namely proving
r_{4}\geq r_{5}
, does not seem possible:
SwBvr
is a (more abstract) weaker assumption than
SwFv
,
even in the presence of all the other assumptions.

Proof of
r_{6}\geq r_{5}
:
This follows from the fact that, in the presence of the other axioms in
\mathsf{Props}_{5}
,
the bound-variable renaming property
SwBvr
implies the congruence property
SwCg
(though not the other way around). So again we use an identity functor
F
, more precisely the inclusion functor between the categories
of
(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-models and
(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-models.

Proof of
r_{9}\geq r_{7}
:
This follows by applying
Prop.
11
with the functor
F
that transforms the
substitution operator of a
(\Sigma_{7},\mathsf{Props}_{7})
-model
\mathcal{M}
into a renaming operator just like this is done for terms, by inserting the free-variable injection operator into the second argument:

m[y/x]^{\mathcal{M}}=m[(\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;y)/x]^{\mathcal{M}}
.
This yields a model satisfying
\mathsf{Props}_{9}

because the axioms for substitution straightforwardly imply those for renaming (though not the other way around, since substitution requires additional structure).

Proof of
r_{9}\geq r_{8}
:
The proof here takes advantage of the fact that, in a constructor-enriched renset (structures axiomatizing renaming that form the basis of recursor
r_{8}
), freshness is definable from renaming
(
Popescu 2023b
)
in several equivalent ways,
including via
FrDRn
; and with this definition, the
\mathsf{Props}_{9}
properties
follow from
\mathsf{Props}_{8}
. So
r_{9}\geq r_{8}
is proved using the functor that takes any
(\Sigma_{8},\mathsf{Props}_{8})
-model to a
(\Sigma_{9},\mathsf{Props}_{9})
-model that has the same carrier set, constructors and renaming operator, and has freshness defined via
FrDRn
.
∎

One may wonder whether any relation can be established between the strength of swapping-based recursors on the one hand, and renaming- or substitution-based recursors on the other hand. The answer seems to be negative: Substitution-like operators
\_[\_/\_]^{\mathcal{M}}:M\rightarrow{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M
are structurally more complex than swapping-like operators

\_[\_\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]^{\mathcal{M}}:M\rightarrow{\mathsf{{Var}}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M

as they (intuitively) refer to the replacement not of variables for variables, but of entities from the models for variables; so there seems to be no hope of defining the former from the latter in a freshness-swapping model; and it seems that not even renaming-like operators can be defined from swapping-like operators, since the latter but not the former preserve the free variables.

Conversely, defining a swapping-like operator in a substitution-based or renaming-based model seems superficially more plausible. However, as far as we see, the only way to achieve this while ensuring the required properties for swapping would be along the lines of the standard trick of
employing an additional fresh variable, namely picking a fresh
z
and defining

m[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty]^{\mathcal{M}}=m[z/x]^{\mathcal{M}}[x/y]^{\mathcal{M}}[y/z]^{\mathcal{M}}
. But this does not work since the substitution-based and renaming-based models do not guarantee the existence of fresh variables—and adding axioms guaranteeing that would severely restrict the recursors’ expressiveness. Thus, the swapping/permutation-based recursors and substitution/renaming-based recursors seem incomparable w.r.t. expressiveness (that is, using the “head-to-head” comparison relation
\geq
; but this situation changes when we switch to the laxer comparision
\gtrsim
, as discussed in
§
4.3
).

Proof of Prop.
14
.

(See Fig.
5
from the main paper.)
Assume
g:T\rightarrow B
is definable by
r
, meaning that
g=R\;!_{I,C}
for some
C
in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
. Let
g_{0}=R\;!_{I,o_{1}(C)}
. Because
R
preserves the initial segments and
B=R\;C
, we have
that
o(B)=R\;o_{1}(C)
, hence
g_{0}:T\rightarrow o(B)
.
We must show that (i)
g_{0}
is
r^{\prime}
-definable and (ii)
g=m(B)\;\circ\;g_{0}
.

To show (i), let
C^{\prime}=F\;o_{1}(C)
. From
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
, we have
R^{\prime}\;C^{\prime}=R^{\prime}\;(F\;o_{1}(C))=R\;o_{1}(C)=o(B)
.
Moreover, by the initiality of
I^{\prime}
and the fact that
F\;I=I^{\prime}
, we have
!_{I^{\prime},C^{\prime}}=\;!_{I^{\prime},F\;o_{1}(C)}=F\;!_{I,o_{1}(C)}
.
Hence, using that
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
, we have
R^{\prime}\;!_{I^{\prime},C^{\prime}}=R^{\prime}\;(F\;!_{I,o_{1}(C)})=R\;!_{I,o_{1}(C)}=g_{0}
, which
proves
(i).

Next we show (ii).
By the initiality of
I
, we have
m_{1}(C)\,\circ\;!_{I,o_{1}(C)}=\;!_{I,C}
; hence, by the functoriality of
R
, we have

R\;m_{1}(C)\circ R\;!_{I,o_{1}(C)}=R\;!_{I,C}
. Hence,
since
R
preserves the initial segments which implies
R\;m_{1}(C)=m(R\;C)=m(B)
, we obtain
m(B)\circ g_{0}=g
, as desired.
∎

More detailed proof of Thm.
15
.
When proving each

r_{i}\gtrsim r_{j}
, we instantiate Prop.
14
taking
r^{\prime}=r_{i}
and
r=r_{j}
. So here
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
is the category of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models,
\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
that of
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models,
and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
that of
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models;
R^{\prime}

is the forgetful functor from
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models, and
R
the forgetful functor from
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models to
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models;

B=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}})
,

I^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{i})
and
I=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{j})
.

We define the initial segment
(\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0},(m(\mathcal{A}):o(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{A})_{\mathcal{A}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})})
of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
as follows: For any
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-model
\mathcal{A}
of carrier
A
we take

o(\mathcal{A})
to be its minimal submodel (subalgebra),
i.e., the one generated by

\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{A}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{A}}
; we take
m(\mathcal{A}):o(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
to be the inclusion morphism; and we take
\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
to be the full subcategory given by constructor-generated models.
Each time, we will define the initial segment
(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\allowbreak(m_{1}(\mathcal{M}):o_{1}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{M})_{\mathcal{M}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}})})

so that, for each
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-model
\mathcal{M}
,

o_{1}(\mathcal{M})
is a submodel of
\mathcal{M}
whose carrier is generated by the constructors (
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}
) and will have the other operators from
\Sigma_{j}
defined in specific ways; and
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
will be the full subcategory given by the objects
o_{1}(\mathcal{M})
.
This way, it will be guaranteed that
R
preserves initial segments.

Let us first consider the
\cong
-chain going from
r_{1}
to
r_{6}
.

Since, by Thm.
12
,
r_{6}
(the swap/fresh recursor), is the strongest w.r.t.
\geq
, and
r_{1}
(the perm/free recursor) and
r_{3}
(the swap/free variant recursor) are the weakest and are equivalent with each other,
and because the relation
\gtrsim
is weaker than
\geq
,
it suffices to prove
r_{3}\gtrsim r_{6}
.

Proof of
r_{3}\gtrsim r_{6}
:

To satisfy the conditions of Prop.
14
, we need to define an initial segment of the category of
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-models and an initial-segment-preserving pre-functor to the category of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models.

To achieve this, it turns out that the natural route goes through the properties of the swap/free recursor
r_{4}
—not only those in the axiom-lighter version that we are considering, but also the additional properties used by
Norrish 2004
’s original recursor (
FvSw
,
SwId
,
SwIv
).
We proceed as follows: Given a
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-model
\mathcal{M}
of carrier
M
, we
define a submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
on the subset
M^{\prime}
of
M
generated by the constructor-like operations
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}

and prove that, modulo the translation between freshness and freeness (via negation),
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a
(\Sigma_{4},\mathsf{Props}_{4})
-model.
The operations on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
should of course be inherited from the ones of
\mathcal{M}
.
For this to be possible, we first need that
M^{\prime}
is closed under the
\Sigma_{6}
-operations. By definition it is closed under the constructor-like operations and,
thanks to
\mathcal{M}
satisfying
SwVr
,
SwAp
and
SwLm
, it is also closed under the swapping-like operation. Now, we take the freshness predicate on
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
to be
defined in the style of nominal logic, i.e., by
FrDSw
: given
x\in\mathsf{{Var}}
and
m\in M^{\prime}
, we define

x\,\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m
to mean that
m[x\wedge y]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\not=m
for only a finite number of variables
y
.

Alternatively, we could have defined
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
inductively by the clauses
FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm
, which would make
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
the minimal submodel of
\mathcal{M}
. Indeed, these two definitions will eventually turn out to be equivalent, but our proof needs to follow a delicate sequence of steps which require that we start with the nominal-like definition. For now, let us write
\$^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
for this alternative, inductively defined freshness predicate. Note that
\$^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
is smaller than the restriction of
\#^{\mathcal{M}}
to
M^{\prime}
.

To establish that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a submodel of
\mathcal{M}
, it remains to prove that, for items in
M^{\prime}
,
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
is smaller than
\#^{\mathcal{M}}
. We will actually prove the stronger statement that
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
is smaller than
\$^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
. The proof proceeds by expanding the definition of
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
, and needs that
FrDSw
and
FrSw
hold for

\$^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\!
. Both these last properties follow by induction on the definition of
\$^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\!
. (Note that a direct proof that
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
is smaller than
\#^{\mathcal{M}}
would not work along the same lines, since neither
FrDSw
and
FrSw
are guaranteed to hold for the restriction of
\#^{\mathcal{M}}
to
M^{\prime}
; the tighter predicate
\$^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
is actually needed.)

So
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a submodel of
\mathcal{M}
. We now need to prove that

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-model.

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
SwVr
,
SwAp
,
SwLm
because these are equations and its supermodel
\mathcal{M}
satisfies them.
3
3

3

Note that
we cannot say the same for
FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm
.

That
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
FrVr
and
FrAp
follows easily by applying the definition of
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
.
That
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
SwCg
follows immediately from
\mathcal{M}
satisfying
SwCg
and
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
being included in
\#^{\mathcal{M}}
.

For proving the next facts, we need to make heavy use of the fact that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies the structural properties of swapping,
SwId
,
SwIv
and
SwCp
.
All these three follow by easy induction on the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and the fact that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
SwVr
,
SwAp
,
SwLm
.

To prove that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
FrLm
, we first prove that
x\,\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\,\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}x\,d
holds for all
d\in M^{\prime}
, in particular, that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
FCB
.
A prerequisite for the latter is that

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
FrSw
, which
follows from the definition of
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
together
with the fact that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
SwCp
and
SwIv
.
Now, that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
FrLm
almost (but not quite) follows
from

SwCg
(for
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
); we actually need a stronger version of
SwCg
to hold for
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
, namely: For all
x,x^{\prime},z\in\mathsf{{Var}}
and
m,m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
, if (
z=x
or
z\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m
), (
z=x^{\prime}
or
z\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m^{\prime}
)
and
m[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}=m[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx^{\prime}]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
, then
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}x\,m=\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}x^{\prime}\,m^{\prime}
.
To prove the latter, we need to cover the cases not covered by
SwCg
, namely
(1) that of
z=x=x^{\prime}
, which follows from the fact that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
SwId
and
(2) that of
z=x
and
z\,\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m^{\prime}
(and a case symmetric to it),
which needs that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies

SwFr
and

FrDSw
, i.e., that
FrDSw
holds for
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}

(whereas so far we only know that it holds for
\$^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
).
That
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies

SwFr
follows from the definition of
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
together
with the fact that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
SwCp
.
That
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies

FrDSw
follows from the fact that (3)
FrDSw
holds for

\#
(the predicate on terms), and that (4) the unique
\Sigma_{6}
-morphism
g:\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{6})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
guaranteed by the initiality of
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{6})

has its image included in
M^{\prime}
and it turns out to preserve freshness not only in the form “
x\#t
implies
x\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}g\,t
”, but in the stronger form
“
x\#t
iff
x\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}g\,t
”. Fact (4) follows from applying

FrDSw
to both
\#
and
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
and using that
g
commutes with swapping.

In summary, along the above route, we proved that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a submodel of
\mathcal{M}
that satisfies the properties in
\mathsf{Props}_{6}
, meaning that the inclusion function is a morphism between
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
in the category of
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-models, as desired. On the way, we also proved that

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
FCB
,
SwIv
,
SwId
,
SwCp
,
FrSw
,
SwFr
and

FvDSw
. So, in the notations of Prop.
14
. we take
o_{1}(\mathcal{M})
to be
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and we take
m_{1}(\mathcal{M}):o(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
to be the inclusion morphism. The forgetful functor between
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-models and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-models (
R
in Prop.
14
’s notations) is easily seen to be initial-segment preserving.

We define Prop.
14
’s operator
F
to take any model
\mathcal{M}
to the model
F\;\mathcal{M}
that replaces
\#^{\mathcal{M}}
with
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}
standardly defined from
\#^{\mathcal{M}}
and to be the identity on morphisms.
We must show that
F\;\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies the properties in
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
. This is true, because
SwVr
,
SwAp
and
SwLm
are already in
\mathsf{Props}_{6}
and all the other properties in
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
have already been proved above.
It is easily seen that
F
is a pre-functor (a functor actually) such that
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
and

F\;\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{6})=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{3})
, as required by Prop.
14
.
This concludes the proof of
r_{3}\gtrsim r_{6}
.

(Note that, on the way to proving that our constructed submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{6}

and (via the freshness to free-variable translation) satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
, we actually proved that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies (again via the translation) all properties of
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
as well. So a natural question is whether the above-sketched fairly intricate proof could not be made more modular along a
r_{3}\gtrsim r_{4}\gtrsim r_{6}
relationship, i.e., split into (1) a proof that any
\mathsf{Props}_{6}
-model produces a
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
-submodel and (2) any
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
-model produces a
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
-submodel. In particular, for proving (1) one may hope to avoid defining the nominal-style freshness operator and work with
\$^{\mathcal{M}}
instead. After some trial and error, we came to believe that the answer to the above questions is
‘no’. It seems that the route through nominal-style freshness is necessary even for constructing a
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
-submodel. In particular, using

\$^{\mathcal{M}}
works for everything except for proving the satisfaction of

SwFr
(translated as
SwFv
). Indeed,
SwFv
has
two
hypotheses involving freshness, and we must induct on one of them; and, unlike when we work in the term model (where the constructors are “almost free”), here we do not have any well-behaved inversion rules
corresponding to
FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm

to apply to the other freshness hypothesis, which seem necessary to make the proof go through; for example, we cannot infer
z\not=x
from
z\,\$^{\mathcal{M}}\,\mathsf{{Vr}}\,x
.
In conclusion, if we want to build a submodel satisfying
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
, we seem to actually
need to build one that satisfies the stronger properties
\mathsf{Props}_{3}
.)

Let us now consider the
(\cong,\gtrsim)
-chain going from
r_{6}
to
r_{7}
.

Since, by Thm.
12
, we have that
r_{9}\geq r_{8},r_{7}
,
and again since
\gtrsim
is weaker than
\geq
, all we have to prove are two relationships:

r_{8}\gtrsim r_{9}
and

r_{6}\gtrsim r_{8}
.

Proof of
r_{8}\gtrsim r_{9}
:

Similarly to the previous proof,
given a
(\Sigma_{9},\mathsf{Props}_{9})
-model
\mathcal{M}
of carrier
M
, we
define a submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
on the subset
M^{\prime}
of
M
generated by the constructor-like operations
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}
.

Then we prove that
f:\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{9})\rightarrow\mathcal{M}
, the unique
\Sigma_{9}
-morphism ensured by the initiality of

\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{9})
, has its image equal to
M^{\prime}
, i.e., is also a morphism between
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{9})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. (The proof goes smoothly: one direction by induction on terms using depth (size) as measure and the other direction by induction on the definition of
M^{\prime}
.) This connection between
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{9})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
will allows us to “borrow” any equation from terms to elements of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
; in what follows, we will refer to it as “the term-model connection”.

So we define
M^{\prime}
to be the subset of
M
(the carrier of
\mathcal{M}
) generated by the constructor-like operations.
To organize
M^{\prime}
into a submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
, we need that
M^{\prime}
is closed under the renaming operator
\_[\_\,/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\!\!\_]
, which follows from the term-model connection. Now we can define the model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
to be formed on
M^{\prime}
using the restrictions of the
\mathcal{M}
operations and of the
\mathcal{M}
freshness predicate.
By virtue of being a full submodel of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
(i.e., a submodel where freshness is the restriction to
M^{\prime}
of
the freshness predicate from the supermodel, and not just a subset of it)
and all the properties in
\mathsf{Props}_{9}
being Horn clauses, it follows that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a
(\Sigma_{9},\mathsf{Props}_{9})
-model. So we take, as before,
o_{1}(\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and
m_{1}(\mathcal{M})
to be the inclusion morphism, and the forgetful functor is easily seen to preserve the initial segments.

We define
F
by taking
F\;\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
to be the restriction of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
obtained by forgetting the freshness operator, and taking
F
on morphisms to be the identity. Since all the properties of
\mathsf{Props}_{8}
are unconditional equations, we can prove that
F\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies all of them along the term-model connection, from the corresponding properties on terms.
Finally, (as before) it is easily seen that
F
is a functor such that
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
and
and

F\;\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{9})=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{8})
.
This concludes the proof of
r_{8}\gtrsim r_{9}
.

Proof of
r_{6}\gtrsim r_{8}
:

Similarly to the previous cases,
given a
(\Sigma_{8},\mathsf{Props}_{8})
-model
\mathcal{M}
of carrier
M
, we
define a submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
on the subset
M^{\prime}
of
M
generated by the constructor-like operations
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}
.
Like in the previous proof, we establish a term-model connection, which immediately gives us that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a submodel of
\mathcal{M}
; and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{8}
because its supermodel
\mathcal{M}
does and
\mathsf{Props}_{8}
consists of equations.

It remains to define a swapping-like operator on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and prove that it forms a
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-model. To this end, we first define
a freshness-like operator on
M^{\prime}
in the style of nominal-logic, but using renaming rather than swapping
(
Popescu 2023b
)
:
x\,\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\,m
iff
\{y\mid m[y/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}x]\not=m\}
is finite.
Next we prove that
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
has finite support—by induction on the definition of
M^{\prime}
. This allows us to define a “
{{\mathsf{pickFresh}}}
”
operator that, given any lists of variables and elements of
M^{\prime}
, produces a variable that is fresh for all items in the lists.
Now, a swapping-like operator
\_[\_\wedge^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\!\!\_]
is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{c}d[z_{1}\wedge^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{2}]=d\,[y/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{1}]\,[z_{1}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{2}]\,[z_{2}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y]\\
\mbox{ \ where $y={{\mathsf{pickFresh}}}\;[z_{1},z_{2}]\;[d]$}\end{array}

(So we define
F\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
to be the
\Sigma_{6}
-model obtained from
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
by replacing
\_[\_\,/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\!\!\_]
with
\_[\_\wedge^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\!\!\_]
in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, and we take
F
to be the identity on morphisms.)
In other words, the definition proceeds just like we would define swapping from renaming on terms.
Now the only question is whether we have enough assumptions on our abstract
(\Sigma_{8},\mathsf{Props}_{8})
-model
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
in order to infer the properties of swapping from those of substitutions, like we could for concrete terms (hence prove that
F\,\mathcal{M}^{\prime}

satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{6}
).

The rest of our proof consists of building a positive answer to this question.
To help with the proofs, we first establish a fresh induction principle for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
similar to the nominal-logic one for terms
(
Pitts 2006
)
. While this takes us a long way, it is not able to prove the following property stating that the definition of swapping is independent of the chosen fresh representative (which in turn is crucial for proving that swapping commutes with
\mathsf{{Ap}}
and
\mathsf{{Lm}}
, i.e., that
SwAp
and
SwLm
hold for
{\sf{F}}\;\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
), namely:

For all
m\in M^{\prime}
and
y,y^{\prime},z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathsf{{Var}}
,

if
y,y^{\prime}\not\in\{z_{1},z_{2}\}
and
y,y^{\prime}\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m
, then

\begin{array}[]{c}d\,[y/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{1}]\,[z_{1}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{2}]\,[z_{2}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y]\;=\\
d\,[y^{\prime}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{1}]\,[z_{1}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{2}]\,[z_{2}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y^{\prime}]\end{array}

The reason why fresh induction on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
cannot prove this property (unlike fresh induction on terms which could prove its term counterpart) is that, because of the freshness assumptions
y,y^{\prime}\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m
, we would need to apply inversion rules for freshness w.r.t. constructors (e.g., infer
y\,\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m_{1}
from
y\,\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m_{1}m_{2}
), which hold for terms but not for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
. And doing some kind of induction on the freshness assumption (after proving the minimality of
\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
) does not help either, since there are two such assumptions, and one of them would still need an inversion rule.

To deal with this difficulty, we rephrase the above definition by eliminating freshness completely. Namely, first we prove that (*)
y\,\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m[u/y]
whenever
u\not=y
. This allows us to rephrase the above choice-independence property as an equation:
4
4

4

We display this highlighting the main additions, and crossing out what has been removed.

For all
m\in M^{\prime}
and
y,y^{\prime},z_{1},z_{2},\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$u$}}\in\mathsf{{Var}}
,

if
y,y^{\prime}\not\in\{z_{1},z_{2},u\}

and
y,y^{\prime}\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m
, then

\begin{array}[]{c}d\,\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$[u/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y]$}}\,\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$[u/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y^{\prime}]$}}\,[y/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{1}]\,[z_{1}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{2}]\,[z_{2}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y]\\
=\\
d\,\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$[u/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y]$}}\,\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$[u/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y^{\prime}]$}}\,[y^{\prime}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{1}]\,[z_{1}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z_{2}]\,[z_{2}/^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}y^{\prime}]\end{array}

Indeed, the previous version follows from this one, using (*). Now, this version
can
be inferred from the term-model connection, using the corresponding property for terms.

After overcoming this difficulty, the desired properties in
\mathsf{Props}_{6}
all follow smoothly either using the term-model connection or by fresh induction. Again, it is easily seen that
F
is a functor such that
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
and

F\;\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{8})=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma_{6})
. This concludes the proof of
r_{6}\gtrsim r_{8}
.
∎

Proof of Prop.
16
.

Recall that
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}=\{{\mathsf{vr}},{\mathsf{ap}},{\mathsf{lm}}\}
is the constructor signature and all signatures
\Sigma_{i}
extend
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
. For proving
r_{i}\not\geq r_{j}
, we must provide a
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-model
\mathcal{M}
for which the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-reduct (i.e., the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-model obtained by forgetting the operators from
\Sigma_{j}\setminus\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
) cannot be the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-reduct of any
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-model.
Indeed, this would mean exhibiting a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-morphism
(namely the reduct of the unique morphism defined by
r_{j}
using
\mathcal{M}
)
that is
r_{j}
-definable but not
r_{i}
-definable.

We write
\mathsf{{Var}}^{\mathbb{N}}
for the set of streams of variables, i.e., families
{\mathit{xs}}=({\mathit{xs}}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
with
{\mathit{xs}}_{i}\in\mathsf{{Var}}
. Given
{\mathit{xs}}\in\mathsf{{Var}}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
y\in\mathsf{{Var}}
and
\sigma\in\mathsf{{Perm}}
, we write:

\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}
for the set of all variables appearing in
{\mathit{xs}}
,
\{{\mathit{xs}}_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
;

\mathsf{{rem}}_{y}\,{\mathit{xs}}
