for the stream obtained from
{\mathit{xs}}
by removing from it all occurrences of
y
;

\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}\,{\mathit{xs}}
for the stream obtained by mapping
\sigma
on
{\mathit{xs}}
,

(\sigma({\mathit{xs}}_{i}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
.

To prove
r_{1}\not\geq r_{2}
, recall that
\Sigma_{1}=\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}\cup\{{\mathsf{pm}},{\mathsf{fv}}\}
. We take the
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model
\mathcal{M}
to have as carrier the set
M=\mathsf{Tr}\cup A
, where
A=\{{\mathit{xs}}\in\mathsf{{Var}}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}\,\mbox{ is infinite}\}
. Note that both
\mathsf{{rem}}_{y}
and
\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}
(for
\sigma\in\mathsf{{Perm}}
) preserve the property that
\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}
is infinite. Let
(t_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of terms such that all are ground (
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t_{i}=\emptyset
)
and mutually distinct (
i\not=j
implies
t_{i}\not=t_{j}
). (For example, we can take
t_{i}
to be
(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x)^{i}\,(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)
for some fixed variable
x
.)

We define
\mathcal{M}
’s operators on
M
by extending the standard term operators from
\mathsf{Tr}
as follows, for any
{\mathit{xs}}\in A
:

\bullet

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}{\mathit{xs}}=\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,y\;{\mathit{xs}}=\mathsf{{rem}}_{y}\,{\mathit{xs}}
for any
y\in\mathsf{{Var}}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;{\mathit{xs}}\;t_{i}=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;{\mathit{xs}}_{i}
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;{\mathit{xs}}\;m=t_{0}
for any
m\in M\smallsetminus\{t_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;s\;{\mathit{xs}}=t_{0}
for any
s\in\mathsf{Tr}

\bullet

{\mathit{xs}}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}=\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}\,{\mathit{xs}}
for any
\sigma\in\mathsf{{Perm}}

Note that, on
A
, the free-variable-like and abstraction-like operators are natural, in particular
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}
removes all occurrences of the abstracted variable. On the other hand, the application-like operator is contrived: the only interesting case is
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;{\mathit{xs}}\;t_{i}
, where application emulates the
i
’th projection, retrieving the
i
’th element of the stream
{\mathit{xs}}
; in the other cases application simply returns the ground term
t_{0}
. We can check that
\mathcal{M}
thus defined satisfies the
\mathsf{Props}_{2}
properties (they are known to hold form terms, so it remains to check these properties when elements
{\mathit{xs}}
of
A
are involved):

•

PmId
,
PmCp
and
PmFv
hold on
A
thanks to standard properties of
\mathsf{{map}}
and
\mathsf{{Vars}}
for streams.

•

For
PmVr
and
FvVr
there is nothing to check because they does not involve elements of
A
.

•

PmLm
on
A
means
\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}(\mathsf{{rem}}_{y}\,{\mathit{xs}})=\mathsf{{rem}}_{(\sigma\,y)}(\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}{\mathit{xs}})
, again a standard property on streams.

•

To check
PmAp
, we distinguish between three cases (according to the above definition of
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
on arguments involving items from
A
):

–

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,({\mathit{xs}}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})\;(t_{i}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}\,{\mathit{xs}})\;t_{i}=\mathsf{{Vr}}\,(\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}\,{\mathit{xs}})_{i}=\mathsf{{Vr}}\,(\sigma\;{\mathit{xs}}_{i})=(\mathsf{{Vr}}\,x_{i})[\sigma]=(\mathsf{{Vr}}\,x_{i})[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}=(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,{\mathit{xs}}\,t_{i})[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}
.

–

Assume
m\in M\smallsetminus\{t_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
, and note that we also have
m[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}\in M\smallsetminus\{t_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Then:

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,({\mathit{xs}}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})\;(m[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})=t_{0}=t_{0}[\sigma]=t_{0}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}=(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,{\mathit{xs}}\;m)[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}
.

–

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(s[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})\;({\mathit{xs}}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(s[\sigma])\;({\mathit{xs}}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})=t_{0}=t_{0}[\sigma]=t_{0}[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}=

(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,s\;{\mathit{xs}})[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}}
.

•

FvLm
on
A
means
\mathsf{{Vars}}\,(\mathsf{{rem}}_{y}\,{\mathit{xs}})\subseteq\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}\smallsetminus\{y\}
, which actually
holds for streams in equality form.

•

Finally,
FvAp
holds trivially in cases where
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
returns
t_{0}
, since
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{0}=\emptyset
; and for
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,{\mathit{xs}}\,t_{i}
, it amounts to
x_{i}\in\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}
.

It remains to check that the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-reduct of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{Tr}\cup A
equipped with the above-defined constructor-like operators, cannot be the reduct of any
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model, i.e., there is no way to define the operators

\_[\_]^{\prime}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{Tr}\cup A
that, together with
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
, make it a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model. So let us assume otherwise,
i.e., that such operators
\_[\_]^{\prime}:(\mathsf{Tr}\cup A)\rightarrow\mathsf{{Perm}}\rightarrow(\mathsf{Tr}\cup A)
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}:(\mathsf{Tr}\cup A)\rightarrow{{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathsf{{Var}})
exist,
and reach a contradiction.

We note that
\_[\_]^{\prime}
is uniquely determined on
\mathsf{Tr}
because of
PmVr
,
PmAp
and
PmLm
, so on
\mathsf{Tr}{}
it must coincide with the standard permutation operator.
Moreover, for each
{\mathit{xs}}\in A
, thanks to
PmAp
used for
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,{\mathit{xs}}\,t_{i}
we have that, for any
i
,
({\mathit{xs}}[\sigma]^{\prime})_{i}=\sigma\;{\mathit{xs}}_{i}
, meaning that
\_[\sigma]^{\prime}
must be
\mathsf{{map}}_{\sigma}
on
A
. In other words,
\_[\_]^{\prime}
must be the same as
\_[\_]^{\mathcal{M}}
.

Now let
{\mathit{xs}}
be any element of
A
. We will show that
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}\,{\mathit{xs}}
is necessarily the entire set of variables
\mathsf{{Var}}
. Let
y\in\mathsf{{Var}}
. If
y\in\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}
, then
y={\mathit{xs}}_{i}
for some
i
, hence using
FvAp
for
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,{\mathit{xs}}\;t_{i}
we obtain
y\in{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}\;{\mathit{xs}}
.
Now assume
y\in\mathsf{{Var}}\smallsetminus\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}
. Then, for any
z\in\mathsf{{Vars}}\;{\mathit{xs}}
,
{\mathit{xs}}[z\leftrightarrow y]^{\prime}
, i.e.,

\mathsf{{map}}_{z\leftrightarrow y}\,{\mathit{xs}}
, is different from
{\mathit{xs}}
. Thus,
{\mathit{xs}}[z\leftrightarrow y]^{\prime}\not={\mathit{xs}}

for an infinite number of variables
z
, which by
FvDPm
implies
y\in{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}\,{\mathit{xs}}
.

We thus showed that
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}\,{\mathit{xs}}=\mathsf{{Var}}
for any
{\mathit{xs}}\in A
. But this contradicts
FCB
, according to which we must have
y\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,y\;{\mathit{xs}})
.

To prove
r_{2}\not\geq r_{4}
, recall that
\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}\cup\{{\mathsf{pm}},{\mathsf{fv}}\}
and
\Sigma_{4}=\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}\cup\{{\mathsf{sw}},{\mathsf{fv}}\}
. We take the
(\Sigma_{4},\mathsf{Props}_{4})
-model
\mathcal{M}
to have as carrier the set
M=\mathsf{Tr}\cup\{a\}
(where
a\not\in\mathsf{Tr}
), i.e., to consist of terms plus an additional element
a
. Let
x
be a fixed variable. We define
\mathcal{M}
’s operators on
M
by extending the standard term operators from
\mathsf{Tr}
as follows:

\bullet

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}a=\mathsf{{Var}}
(the set of all variables)

\bullet

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,y\;a=\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,y\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)
for any
y\in\mathsf{{Var}}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;a\;a=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;a\;t=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;t
for any
t\in\mathsf{Tr}

\bullet

\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;t\;a=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;t\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)
for any
t\in\mathsf{Tr}

\bullet

a[z_{1}\hskip-0.86108pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}}=\mathsf{{Vr}}\,(x[z_{1}\hskip-0.86108pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])
for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathsf{{Var}}

(Thus, the free variables of
a
are the entire set of variables, and the constructor and swapping operators on
a
yield the same results as for
\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
, i.e., have
\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
act in lieu of
a
.)

We can check that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{4}
(they are known to hold for terms, so we only need to check these properties when
a
is involved):

FvVr
,
FvAp
and
FvLm
immediately hold thanks to
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;a
being
\mathsf{{Var}}
;
moreover,

SwVr
,
SwAp
and
SwLm
hold because any application of constructor or swapping operator turns
a
into
\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
; finally,

SwFv
holds trivially for
a
, since,
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}a
being
\mathsf{{Var}}
, the hypothesis of
SwFv
is vacuously false.

It remains to check that the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-reduct of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{Tr}\cup\{a\}
equipped with the above-defined constructor-like operators, cannot be the reduct of any
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model, i.e., there is no way to define the operators
\_[\_]^{\prime}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{Tr}\cup\{a\}
that, together with
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}
, make it a
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model.
So let us assume otherwise,
i.e., that such operators
\_[\_]^{\prime}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\prime}
exist,
and reach a contradiction.

We note that
\_[\_]^{\prime}
is uniquely determined on
\mathsf{Tr}
because of
PmVr
,
PmAp
and
PmLm
, so on
\mathsf{Tr}{}
it must coincide with the standard permutation operator. Moreover,
PmId
and
PmCp
imply that
\_[\sigma]^{\prime}
is bijective on
\mathsf{Tr}\cup\{a\}
for any permutation
\sigma
.
Hence, because the restriction
\_[\sigma]^{\prime}
to
\mathsf{Tr}
is also a bijection on
\mathsf{Tr}
(being equal to the standard permutation operator), the only possibility is that
a[\sigma]^{\prime}=a
for any
\sigma
. But this stands in contradiction with
PmAp
, because together with
PmAp
it would imply that
(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,a\;a)[\sigma]^{\prime}=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(a[\sigma]^{\prime})\;(a[\sigma]^{\prime})=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,a\;a
, i.e.,
(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x))[\sigma]=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)
, which is false for any
\sigma
that modifies
x
.
∎

C.3.
Back to the proof of the recursion theorems

The heart of the proof of an epi-recursion principle,
i.e., of the fact that a tuple
r=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
forms an epi-recursor,
is a proof of initiality, namely the initiality of the object
I
in the category
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
. And indeed, this is the difficult part in the proof of all the nominal recursors listed in Thm
9
.

Next, we show how we can take advantage of the expressiveness comparisons to “borrow” a (quasi)weaker recursion principle from a (quasi)stronger one, and to infer all nominal recursors from only two of them—those located at the top of the expressiveness hierarchy.

Indeed, the idea behind our expressiveness comparison criteria (Props.
11
and
14
)
has been the possibility of one recursor to
simulate the behavior of another
recursor
, so it feels natural to use this idea for “borrowing” purposes.
To this end, we first introduce pre-epi-recursors, which are epi-recursors without the initiality condition, and a possible property of them called tightness:

Def 28.

A
pre-epi-recursor
is a tuple
r=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
subject to the same condition as an epi-recursor (Def.
17
), but without the requirement that
I
is the initial object of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.

A pre-epi-recursor is called
tight

if the following hold:

•

T
is a quasi-initial object in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
(in that for every object
B
in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
there exists at most one morphism from
T
to
B
).

•

The functor
R
is faithful (in that it is injective on morphisms). ∎

All the nominal pre-epi-recursors we discussed in this paper are tight, because
T
is a quotient of the term algebra (known to be quasi-initial)
and the functor
R
is the identity on morphisms.

Now, to make the borrowing possible,
we take advantage of the fact that the definitions of
\geq
and
\gtrsim
make sense, and also
Props.
11
and
14
hold,
not only for epi-recursors, but also for pre-epi-recursors.

Prop 29.

Assume the following:

•

(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
is a tight pre-epi-recursor

•

r^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},I^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is an epi-recursor

•

The hypotheses of Prop.
14
hold,
namely we assume:

–

a pre-functor
F:\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
,

–

an initial segment
(\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0},(m(B):o(B)\rightarrow B)_{B\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})})
of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
,

–

an initial segment
(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\allowbreak(m_{1}(C):o_{1}(C)\rightarrow C)_{C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}})})
of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
,

such that
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
contains
I
,

R
preserves the above initial segments,

F\;I=I^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}

(where
R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
is the restriction of
R
to
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
).

•

We additionally assume that the pre-functor
F
is full.

Then
r
is an epi-recursor (i.e.,
I
is initial).

Proof sketch:

We need to prove that
I
is initial. To this end, let
C
be an object in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.
Since
F
is full and
I^{\prime}=F\;I
, we obtain
h:I\rightarrow o_{1}(C)

such that
F\;h=\;!_{I^{\prime},F\;o_{1}(C)}
. We define
!_{I,C}
to be
m_{1}(C)\circ h
.

It remains to prove the uniqueness of
!_{I,C}
as a morphism from
I
to
C
. To this end, let
k:I\rightarrow C
.
Then both
R\;!_{I,C}
and
R\;k
are morphisms between
R\;I=T
and
R\;C
, hence
R\;!_{I,C}=R\;k
by the quasi-initiality of
T
. Finally,

!_{I,C}=k
follows from the faithfulness of
R
. ∎

Prop 30.

Assume the following:

•

(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},I,R)
is a tight pre-epi-recursor

•

r^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},I^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is an epi-recursor

•

The hypotheses of Prop.
11
hold, namely
we assume a pre-functor

F:\underline{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}

such that

R^{\prime}\circ F=R
and

F\;I=I^{\prime}
.

•

We additionally assume that the pre-functor
F
is full.

Then
r
is an epi-recursor (i.e.,
I
is initial).

Proof sketch:

This already follows from Prop.
29
, because
the hypotheses of Prop.
11
are stronger than those of
Prop.
14
. However, let us also give a direct proof:

We need to prove that
I
is initial. To this end, let
C
be an object in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.
Since
F
is full and
I^{\prime}=F\;I
, we obtain
h:I\rightarrow C

such that
F\;h=\;!_{I^{\prime},F\;C}
. We define
!_{I,C}
to be
h
.

It remains to prove the uniqueness of
!_{I,C}
as a morphism from
I
to
C
.
To this end, let
k:I\rightarrow C
.
Then both
R\;!_{I,C}
and
R\;k
are morphisms between
R\;I=T
and
R\;C
, hence
R\;!_{I,C}=R\;k
by the quasi-initiality of
T
. Finally,

!_{I,C}=k
follows from the faithfulness of
R
. ∎

Taking advantage of Prop.
30
, our proof of Thm.
9
goes along the following route:

•

We prove that
r_{1}
–
r_{9}
are all tight pre-epi-recursors, which is immediate.

•

We prove that
r_{6}
and
r_{9}

are epi-recursors, i.e., we do direct initiality proofs for
r_{6}
and
r_{9}
(as sketched in the main paper).

•

We use the fact that, according to Thm.
12
,
r_{6}
and
r_{9}
are stronger than all the others—and they are so by virtue of the criterion in Prop.
11
.

•

We verify the additional hypothesis of Prop.
30
, namely that
F
is full in all cases (which is again immediate). Finally, we apply Prop.
30
to borrow all the other recursion theorems from those of
r_{6}
and
r_{9}
.

Appendix D
Adding (Back) Enhancements to the Recursors

It was convenient to discuss and compare the expressiveness of the stripped down versions of the recursors—since their enhancements, while useful, require some heavier notation that can clutter the main ideas. Here, we add back the enhancements and
show how our results generalize to cover the enhanced recursors.

Recall from §
2.2
that the enhancements referred to support for the Barendregt variable convention and for full-fledged (primitive) recursion.
In the case of full-fledged recursion, we noted that different degrees of support are possible: the additional term parameters can affect constructor only, or the other operations as well (as we have seen with the swap/fresh and subst/fresh recursors); and they can be optimized for the freeness operator (as we have seen with the swap/free recursor).
The table in Fig.
8
summarizes the situation.

Existing

recursor

Full-fledged

recursion?

Barendregt

convention?

Perm/free

No

Yes

Swap/free

For constructors,

freeness-optimized

Yes

Swap/fresh

For all

operators

No

Subst/fresh

For all

operators

No

Renaming

No

Yes

Fig
.
8
.
Enhancements exhibited by nominal recursors from the literature

It turns out that
all the nominal recursors have the following in common:

(a)

all these enhancements work on all the recursors, and

(b)

the enhanced versions can still be presented as epi-recursprs (for suitably chosen categories of models) and their expressiveness comparisons discussed in the main paper still apply.

Concerning point (a),
we find it quite remarkable that the Barendregt convention enhancement can be applied democratically to recursors based on swapping/permutation and renaming/substitution alike, and also does not discriminate based on the particular axiomatization.
Concerning the different degrees of ful-fledged recursor enhancement listed in Fig.
8
—namely whetherit recursion affects the non-constructor operators too, and whether the freeness optimization is being considered—we show that, in each case, the strongest version of the enhancement is applicable.

Moreover, point (b) tells us that our general epi-recursion framework can be applied
directly to the enhanced recursors
, as opposed to having to regard the enhancements as a form of “hacks” that are added after the fact on top of some categorically clean recursion principles.

In what follows, we sketch the enhancement-extended version of our results.
We first extend the notion of model from §
3.2
as follows.
We fix a finite set
X
of variables (to be “avoided” according to the Barendregt convention).

Def 31.

Given a signature
\Sigma
, an
(X,\Sigma)
-model

\mathcal{M}
consists of a set
M
, called the
carrier set
,
a subset
D\subseteq\mathsf{Tr}\times M
which we will call the
domain
,
and operations and/or relations on
D
with values in
M
according the signature, more precisely:

•

if
{\mathsf{vr}}\in\Sigma

then
\mathcal{M}
has an operation
\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M
;

•

if
{\mathsf{ap}}\in\Sigma

then
\mathcal{M}
has an operation
\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}:D\rightarrow D\rightarrow M
;

•

if
{\mathsf{lm}}\in\Sigma

then
\mathcal{M}
has
\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow D\rightarrow M
;

•

if
{\mathsf{pm}}\in\Sigma

then
\mathcal{M}
has
\_[\_]^{\mathcal{M}}:D\rightarrow\mathsf{{Perm}}\rightarrow M
;

•

if
{\mathsf{sw}}\in\Sigma

then
\mathcal{M}
has
\_[\_\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]^{\mathcal{M}}:D\rightarrow D\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M
;

•

if
{\mathsf{sb}}\in\Sigma

then
\mathcal{M}
has
\_[\_\,/\_]^{\mathcal{M}}:D\rightarrow D\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M
;

•

if
{\mathsf{ren}}\in\Sigma

then
\mathcal{M}
has
\_[\_\,/\_]^{\mathcal{M}}:D\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow M
;

•

if
{\mathsf{fv}}\in\Sigma

then the model
\mathcal{M}
has
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}:D\rightarrow{{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathsf{{Var}})
;

•

if
{\mathsf{fr}}\in\Sigma

then the model
\mathcal{M}
has
\#^{\mathcal{M}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow D\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
.

It is also required that the domain
D
is closed under the operations modulo the avoidance of the variables in
X
when binding or substituting, in that the following hold (if applicable, i.e., if the given operation is in the signature
\Sigma
) for all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathsf{Tr}
and

m,m_{1},m_{2}\in M
such that
(t,m),\;(t_{1},m_{1}),\;(t_{2},m_{2})\in D
, all
x,y,z\in\mathsf{{Var}}
such that

x,y\notin X
,
and all
\sigma\in\mathsf{{Perm}}
such that
\{u\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid\sigma\;u\not=u\}\cap X=\emptyset
:

•

(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;z,\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;z)\in D

•

(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2},\;\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}(t_{1},m_{1})\,(t_{2},m_{2}))\in D
;

•

(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t,\;\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}x\,(t,m))\in D
;

•

(t[\sigma],\,(t,m)[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})\in D
;

•

(t[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty],\,(t,m)[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty]^{\mathcal{M}})\in D
;

•

(t[t_{1}/x],\,(t,m)[(t_{1},m_{1})/x]^{\mathcal{M}})\in D

(if
{\mathsf{sb}}\in\Sigma
);

•

(t[y/x],\,(t,m)[y/x]^{\mathcal{M}})\in D

(if
{\mathsf{ren}}\in\Sigma
). ∎

We can note a few things about this definition:

•

The closedness conditions only make sense for the constructor, permutation, swapping, renaming and substitution operators; and not for the free-variable operator or the freshness relation.

•

Full-fledged (primitive) recursion typically refers to having extra term arguments for the constructor operators only, but (as already pointed out) we consider them for the other operators and relations as well, since it makes the recursor more general.

•

The presence of the domain
D
in the definition, as opposed to working with the entire product
\mathsf{Tr}\times M
as would be customary, has a technical reason: In order to recover the results about the quasi-strength comparison relation
\gtrsim
(Thm.
15
), we must build submodels of these enhanced models; and those cannot have the form
\mathsf{Tr}\times M^{\prime}
for some
M^{\prime}\subseteq M
, but must be more flexible subsets
D^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Tr}\times M
. In short, this small generalization was needed in order to close the category of models under a notion of submodel that works for generalizing our results.

Now the notion of morphism between
(X,\Sigma)
-models is defined in a similarly
X
-avoiding manner:

Def 32.

Given two
(X,\Sigma)
-models
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
, a
morphism

between them is a function between their carrier sets
g:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}

that preserves the domain,
commutes with the operations and preserves the relations modulo
X
. More precisely,
the following properties hold (if applicable, i.e., if the given operation is in the signature
\Sigma
) for all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathsf{Tr}
and

m,m_{1},m_{2}\in M
such that
(t,m),\;(t_{1},m_{1}),\;(t_{2},m_{2})\in D
, all
x,y,z\in\mathsf{{Var}}
such that

x,y\notin X
,
and all
\sigma\in\mathsf{{Perm}}
such that
\{u\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid\sigma\;u\not=u\}\cap X=\emptyset
:

•

(t,m)\in D
implies
(t,g\;m)\in D^{\prime}
;

•

g(\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,z)=\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}z
;

•

g(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(t_{1},m_{1})\,(t_{2},m_{2}))=\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(t_{1},g\;m_{1})\,(t_{2},g\;m_{2})
;

•

g(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,x\;(t,m))=\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}x\;(t,g\;m)
;

•

g((t,m)[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})=(t,g\;m)[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
;

•

g((t,m)[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty]^{\mathcal{M}})=(t,g\;m)[x\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
;

•

g((t,m)[(t_{1},m_{1})/y]^{\mathcal{M}})=(t,g\;m)[(t_{1},g\;m_{1})/y]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
;

•

g((t,m)[x/y]^{\mathcal{M}})=(t,g\;m)[x/y]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
;

•

x\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,(t,m)
implies
x\;\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(t,g\;m)
;

•

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\,(t,g\;m)\subseteq X\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(t,m)
. ∎

(X,\Sigma)
-models and morphisms thus defined form a category.
We write
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr(\Sigma)
for the
(X,\Sigma)
-model whose carrier is the set of terms
\mathsf{Tr}
,
whose domain is the diagonal
\{(t,t)\mid t\in\mathsf{Tr}\}
,
and whose operations and relations are the obvious adaptations of the standard ones for terms.

It remains to interpret the properties in Fig.
2
in
(X,\Sigma)
-models. In other words, given a signature
\Sigma
, an
(X,\Sigma)
-model
\mathcal{M}
, a property
p
from Fig.
2
whose operations and relations are covered by
\Sigma
, we must state what it means for
\mathcal{M}
to satisfy
p
. The interpretation proceeds according to the following transformation rules:

(1)

Any variable participating in
\lambda
-bindings, swappings, permutations, substitutions or freshness assertions is assumed to not belong to
X
.

(2)

If it is the conclusion of the property’s implication, any equation or freshness relation
becomes a corresponding equation or relation referring to the operations in the model.

(3)

Any equation in the hypotheses
becomes a conjunction between the term equation itself and a corresponding equation referring to the operations in the model.

(4)

Any freshness relation in the hypotheses becomes a conjunction between

–

the freshness relation itself (on terms)

–

the implication between the freshness relation on terms and the corresponding one on items in the model, universally quantified on the participating variable (again assumed to not belong to
X
)

Let us illustrate the above on the same examples as those we considered in the main paper:
When we say that the
(X,\Sigma)
-model
\mathcal{M}
(with carrier
M
and domain
D
) satisfies
SwCg
, we mean the following:

For all
t,t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathsf{Tr}
and

m,m_{1},m_{2}\in M
such that
(t,m),\;(t_{1},m_{1}),\;(t_{2},m_{2})\in D
,

and all

x_{1},x_{2},z\in\mathsf{{Var}}
such that
x_{1},x_{2},z\notin X
,

if

z\not\in\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
,

z\;\#\;t_{1},t_{2}
,

(\forall z.\;z\;\#\;t_{1}\rightarrow z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\;(t_{1},m_{1}))
,

(\forall z.\;z\;\#\;t_{2}\rightarrow z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\;(t_{2},m_{2}))
,

t_{1}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]=t_{2}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]
, and

(t_{1},m_{1})[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]^{\mathcal{M}}=(t_{2},m_{2})[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]^{\mathcal{M}}
,

then

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;x_{1}\;(t_{1},m_{1})=\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;x_{2}\;(t_{2},m_{2})
.

Notice how:

•

the variables
x_{1},x_{2},z
are assumed not to be in
X
according to the above transformation rule (1);

•

the equation
\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x_{1}\;t_{1}=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x_{2}\;t_{2}

from the conclusion of
SwCg
has become

\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;x_{1}\;(t_{1},m_{1})=\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;x_{2}\;(t_{2},m_{2})
according to transformation rule (2);

•

the equation
t_{1}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]=t_{2}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]

from the hypotheses of
SwCg
has become the conjunction of

t_{1}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]=t_{2}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]
and

(t_{1},m_{1})[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]^{\mathcal{M}}=(t_{2},m_{2})[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]^{\mathcal{M}}
according to transformation rule (3);

•

the freshness hypothesis
z\;\#\;t_{1}
has become
the conjunction of
z\;\#\;t_{1}
and

(\forall z.\;z\;\#\;t_{1}\rightarrow z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\;(t_{1},m_{1}))
according to transformation rule (4)
(and similarly for
t_{2}
).

The treatment of the equations from the hypotheses—with considering both the original,
here,
t_{1}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]=t_{2}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]
, and the model version
(t_{1},m_{1})[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]^{\mathcal{M}}=(t_{2},m_{2})[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx_{1}]^{\mathcal{M}}
—honors the dual (term and semantic item) nature of full-fledged recursion. By contrast, including the original version in the conclusion too would be redundant, since that one follows anyway thanks to the properties of terms.

The treatment of the freshness relations in the hypothesis is more involved, and departs from what would seem to be a natural rule, which is: including both the original,
z\;\#\;t_{1}
, and
the model version
z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\;(t_{1},m_{1})
as hypotheses. The reason why we instead include

z\;\#\;t_{1}
and
(\forall z.\;z\;\#\;t_{1}\rightarrow z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\;(t_{1},m_{1}))
is because this way we obtain a weaker condition that still works, thus offering a stronger recursion principle. This is the freshness counterpart of the freeness optimization that is specific to the swap/free recursor (mentioned in Fig.
8
).

The properties involving the free-variable operator are interpreted using their freshness-based counterparts.
For example,
\mathcal{M}
(with carrier
M
and domain
D
) satisfying
FCB
means the following:

There exists
x\in\mathsf{{Var}}
such that

x\notin X
and

x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}x\,(t,m))

for all
t\in\mathsf{Tr}
and
m\in M
such that

(t,m)\in D
.

Indeed, thinking of the conclusion of
FCB
, namely
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)
, in terms of freshness, i.e., as
x\,\#\allowbreak(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)
, we apply transformation rule (2) yielding

x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}x\,(t,m))
.
The outcome is a generalization of the standard
FCB
used in nominal logic.

Along the same recipe, we obtain the interpretations for
FvVr
,
FvAp
and
FvLm
, where we can recognize generalizations of the “optimized” swap/free recursion clauses discussed in §
2.2
:

•

For all
x\in\mathsf{{Var}}
,

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\,(\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;x)\subseteq X\cup\{x\}

•

For all
(m_{1},t_{1}),(m_{2},t_{2})\in D
,
if
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;(m_{1},t_{1})\subseteq X\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t_{1}
and
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;(m_{2},t_{2})\subseteq X\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t_{2}

then

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{{Ap}}\,t_{1}\,t_{2})(\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}(m_{1},t_{1})\,(m_{2},t_{2}))\subseteq X\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{1}\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t_{2}

•

For all
(m,t)\in D
and
x\in\mathsf{{Var}}\!\smallsetminus\!X
,
if
{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(m,t)\subseteq X\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t

then

{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)\,(\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}x\;(t,m))\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}\,t\smallsetminus\{x\}

Indeed, these follow from the interpretations for their freshness-based counterparts

FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm
, which are produced according to the above transformation rules:

•

For all
x,z\in\mathsf{{Var}}
such that
z\notin X
,
if
z\not=x
then
z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\,(\mathsf{{Vr}}^{\mathcal{M}}x)

•

For all
z\in\mathsf{{Var}}\smallsetminus X
and
(t,m),(s,n)\in D
,
if
z\;\#\;s
and
(\forall z\in\mathsf{{Var}}\smallsetminus X.\;z\;\#\;s\rightarrow z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,(s,n))
,

z\;\#\;t
and
(\forall z\in\mathsf{{Var}}\smallsetminus X.\;z\;\#\;t\rightarrow z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,(t,m))
,
then

z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,\mathsf{{Ap}}^{\mathcal{M}}(s,n)\;(t,m)

•

For all
x,z\in\mathsf{{Var}}\smallsetminus X
and
(t,m)\in D
,
if
z=x
or (
z\;\#\;t
and
(\forall z\in\mathsf{{Var}}\smallsetminus X.\;z\;\#\;t\rightarrow z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,(t,m))
)
then
z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,\mathsf{{Lm}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;x\;(t,m)
.

All the recursion principles generalize from their stripped down versions
to the enhanced versions:

Thm 33.

Thm.
9
still holds if we replace
the categories of
\Sigma_{i}
-models with those of
(X,\Sigma_{i})
-models. ∎

Recall that Thms.
1
–
5
list
existing nominal recursors from the literature.
Thm.
9
’s recursors

r_{1}
,
r_{4}
,
r_{6}
,
r_{7}
and
r_{8}
were stripped-down versions of these recursors.
By contrast, Thm.
33
’s corresponding recursors are

further enhancements
of the original recursors, obtained by putting together all the
enhancements—because the strongest version of each enhancement now benefits each recursor.

In order to generalize our comparison results,
we were actually compelled to strengthen the recursors even beyond the sum of all enhancements. For example, both the perm/free recursor (specific to nominal logic) and Norrish’s swap/free recursor were based on axiomatizations of permutation and swapping: forming nominal sets in the case of the perm/free recursor and entities called
swapping structures
for the swap/free recursor
(
Norrish 2004
)
. At the same time, both recursors had Barendregt enhancements that allowed the flexibility of working modulo
X
, meaning that some axioms on the target domains operated modulo
X
—as seen in Thms.
1
and
2
. However, this flexibility was not affecting the notions of nominal set or swapping structure, which did not consider
X
.
Our systematic approach to adding performing the Barendregt enhancement, reflected in particular in the
r_{1}
and
r_{4}
versions of our Thm.
33
, makes this flexibility pervasive, thus strengthening Thms.
1
and
2
with what could be called
nominal sets up to
X
and
swapping structures up to
X
.
We have not investigated whether such stronger recursors can make a difference in practice, but it is in principle useful to have the strongest possible recursors at our disposal.

All the expressiveness comparisons results for the stripped down recursors carry over to the enhanced recursors as well:

Thm 34.

Thms.
12
and
15
still hold for the enhanced recursors
(employing
(X,\Sigma_{i})
-models) described in Thm.
33
. ∎

The proofs follow the same lines as those we sketched for Thms.
12
and
15
, using the categorical criteria from Props.
11
and
14
. One phenomenon worth mentioning is that the goal of extending our comparison results to the enhanced recursors have forced us to perform more general enhancements than originally intended (and thought possible). For example, the aforementioned notion of performing
Barendregt enhancement more comprehensively in
r_{1}
and
r_{4}
(yielding nominal sets up to
X
and swapping structures up to
X
) were required in order to prove that, via
r_{3}
, they are quasi-stronger than (the naturally enhanced version of)
r_{6}
.

Appendix E
Non-well-founded infinitary terms

E.1.
Pre-iterms

We start with the set
\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
of pre-iterms, which is (co)freely generated by the grammar:

p::=\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x\mid\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}\mid\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p

So
\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
is a standard coinductive datatype (codatatype)
(
Rutten 2000
;
Kozen and Silva 2017
)

having constructors
\mathsf{{PVr}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
,
\mathsf{{PAp}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}

and
\mathsf{{PLm}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
.

Recall from §
5.1
that
we wrote
{{\mathsf{V}}}
,
{{\mathsf{A}}}
and
{{\mathsf{L}}}
for the three injections into the sum type
\mathsf{S}=\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty})
(so that

{{\mathsf{V}}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{S}
,

{{\mathsf{A}}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{S}
and

{{\mathsf{L}}}:{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathsf{S}
).
We will overload this notation to pre-iterms, thus writing

{{\mathsf{V}}}
,
{{\mathsf{A}}}
and
{{\mathsf{L}}}
also for the three injections into the sum type
\mathsf{S^{\prime}}=\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}+\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
(so that

{{\mathsf{V}}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{S^{\prime}}
,

{{\mathsf{A}}}:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{S^{\prime}}
and

{{\mathsf{L}}}:\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{S^{\prime}}
).

We write
{{\mathsf{PDest}}}:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}+\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
for this codatatype’s destructor. Note that the destructor is the inverse of the constructors in the following sense:

•

t=\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x\allowbreak\mathrel{\;\Leftarrow\penalty\kern-6.88889pt\Rightarrow\;}\allowbreak{{\mathsf{PDest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x

•

t=\mathsf{{PAp}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2}\allowbreak\mathrel{\;\Leftarrow\penalty\kern-6.88889pt\Rightarrow\;}\allowbreak{{\mathsf{PDest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{A}}}(t_{1},t_{2})

•

t=\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;t^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\;\Leftarrow\penalty\kern-6.88889pt\Rightarrow\;}\allowbreak{{\mathsf{PDest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{L}}}(x,t^{\prime})

Thus,
(\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty},PDest)
is the final coalgebra for the functor on sets defined as follows:

•

on objects, it takes any set
A
to
\mathsf{{Var}}+A\times A+\mathsf{{Var}}\times A
;

•

on morphisms, it takes any function
f:A\rightarrow B
to

f+f\times f+\mathsf{{id}}_{\mathsf{{Var}}}\times f
.

Above, we used the following notation. For any two functions
u_{1}:A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1}
and
u_{2}:A_{2}\rightarrow B_{2}
, we let:

•

u_{1}+u_{2}:A_{1}+A_{2}\rightarrow B_{1}+B_{2}
be the function defined by
(u_{1}+u_{2})({{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}\,a_{1})={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}(u_{1}\,a_{1})
and
(u_{1}+u_{2})({{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}\,a_{2})={{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}(u_{2}\,a_{2})
, where
{{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}
and
{{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}
denote the two injections for the sum types;

•

u_{1}\times u_{2}:A_{1}\times A_{2}\rightarrow B_{1}\times B_{2}
be the function defined by
(u_{1}\times u_{2})(a_{1},a_{2})=(u_{1}\,a_{1},u_{2}\,a_{2})
.

(Thus, we use
+
and
\times
for the actions of the sum and product functors not only on objects, but also on morphisms.)

Like any ordinary codatatype,
the pre-iterm codatatype features the following
structural coinduction proof principle
, which states
that equality is the largest destructor-bisimulation on pre-iterms:

Prop 35.

Assume
\varphi:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
is a relation on pre-iterms such that, for all
p,q\in\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
, if
\varphi\;p\;q
then one of the following is true:

•

there exists
x
such that
p=\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x=q
;

•

there exist
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}
such that

p=\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}
,
q=\mathsf{{PAp}}\;q_{1}\;q_{2}
,
\varphi\;p_{1}\;q_{1}
and
\varphi\;p_{2}\;q_{2}
;

•

there exist
x,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
such that

p=\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x^{\prime}\;p^{\prime}
,
q=\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;q^{\prime}
and
\varphi\;p^{\prime}\;q^{\prime}
.

Then
\varphi
is included in equality, in that
\forall p,q.\;\varphi\;p\;q\Rightarrow p=q
.

The pre-iterm codatatype also features the following corecursion definition principle (coiteration to be more precise), which is just an expression of the fact that
(\mathsf{PTr},{{\mathsf{PDest}}})
is a final coalgebra:

Prop 36.

If
(M,D)
is a coalgebra of suitable type, namely

D:M\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+M\times M+\mathsf{{Var}}\times M
, then there exists a unique coalgebra morphism between
(M,D)
and
(\mathsf{PTr},{{\mathsf{PDest}}})
, i.e., a unique function
g:M\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}
that commutes with the destructors, in that, for all
m\in M
,

{{\mathsf{PDest}}}(g\;m)=(1_{\mathsf{{Var}}}+g\times g+1_{\mathsf{{Var}}}\times g)\,(D\;m)
. This commutation condition can also be phrased as the conjunction of three conditions, one for each summand:
5
5

5

In the first equation below, the first
{{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
is an element of
\mathsf{{Var}}+M\times M+\mathsf{{Var}}\times M
, whereas the second
{{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
is an element of
\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}+\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
. This is because of our ambiguous notation for
{{\mathsf{V}}}
; but the context should always disambiguate such situations.

•

D\;m={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
implies
{{\mathsf{PDest}}}(g\;m)={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x

•

D\;m={{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1},m_{2})
implies
{{\mathsf{PDest}}}(g\;m)={{\mathsf{A}}}(g\;m_{1},g\;m_{2})

•

D\;m={{\mathsf{L}}}(x,m^{\prime})
implies
{{\mathsf{PDest}}}(g\;m)={{\mathsf{L}}}(x,g\;m^{\prime})

and further, using pre-iterm constructors instead of destructor:

•

D\;m={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x

•

D\;m={{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1},m_{2})
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{PAp}}\;(g\;m_{1})\;(g\;m_{2})

•

D\;m={{\mathsf{L}}}(x,m^{\prime})
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;(g\;m^{\prime})

In addition to the above, another definition and proof mechanism that is useful for concepts involving pre-iterms (and iterms as well), but that in itself is not bound to codatatypes, is
rule coinduction
: Given any monotonic operator on predicates of some type (e.g.,
n
-ary predicates/ relations on pre-iterms), we can take its greatest fixed point, which is also the greatest post-fixed point—whose existence (and uniqueness) is guaranteed by the Knaster-Tarski theorem
(
Tarski 1955
)
. Usually, this monotonic operator is described using a set of rules, and the greatest fixed point is the largest predicate that is consistent with (i.e., backwards-closed under) these rules. Hence, to prove that this greatest (post)fixed point includes another predicate
\varphi
, it suffices to show that
\varphi
is consistent with these rules.
We refer to
(
Pierce 2002
, §21.1)
for more details.

The swapping operator on pre-iterms,
\_[\_\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
, is defined corecursively by the following clause:

\begin{array}[]{l}p[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]=\mbox{case $p$ of}\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x\Rightarrow\mathsf{{PVr}}\;(x[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}\Rightarrow\mathsf{{PAp}}\;(p_{1}[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\;(p_{2}[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathsf{{PLm}}\;(x[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\;(p^{\prime}[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\end{array}

The above corecursive definition can also be expressed in destructor form:

\begin{array}[]{l}{{\mathsf{PDest}}}\;(p[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])=\mbox{case ${{\mathsf{PDest}}}\;p$ of}\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid{{\mathsf{V}}}\;x\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{V}}}(x[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid{{\mathsf{A}}}(p_{1},p_{2})\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{A}}}(p_{1}[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}],p_{2}[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid{{\mathsf{L}}}(x,p^{\prime})\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{L}}}(x[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}],p^{\prime}[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\end{array}

What this definition means is that we organize the source domain of the (uncurried version of) the to-be-defined function,
M=\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{{Var}}
, into a coalgebra
(M,D)
by defining
D:M\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+M\times M+\mathsf{{Var}}\times M
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}D\;(p,z_{1},z_{2})=\mbox{case ${{\mathsf{PDest}}}\;p$ of}\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid{{\mathsf{V}}}\;x\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{V}}}(x[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid{{\mathsf{A}}}(p_{1},p_{2})\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{A}}}((p_{1},z_{1},z_{2}),(p_{2},z_{1},z_{2}))\\
\hskip 21.52771pt\mid{{\mathsf{L}}}(x,p^{\prime})\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{L}}}(x[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}],(p^{\prime},z_{1},z_{2}))\end{array}

and define
p[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]
as
g\,(p,z_{1},z_{2})
, where
g:(M,D)\rightarrow(\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty},{{\mathsf{PDest}}})
is the unique coalgebra morphism guaranteed by the corecursion principle.
(In this particular case, the parameters
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
stay fixed, so they could have been left out
of the source coalgebra’s carrier.)

The permutation operator on iterms,
\_[\_]:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Perm}}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
, is defined similarly to swapping. (Swapping can of course be alternatively defined from permutation.)

The freshness relation
\_\#\_:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
is defined coinductively by the following rules:

\frac{z\not=x}{z\;\#\;\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x}\hskip 55.97205pt\frac{z\;\#\;p_{1}\;\;\;\;\;\;z\;\#\;p_{2}}{z\;\#\;\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}}\hskip 55.97205pt\frac{z=x\mbox{ \ or \ }z\;\#\;p^{\prime}}{z\;\#\;\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p^{\prime}}

(What this means is that
\_\#\_
defined to be the largest predicate
\varphi:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
that is consistent with the above rules, in the following sense: For all
x\in\mathsf{{Var}}
and
p\in\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
, if
\varphi\;z\;p
then one of the following is true:

•

there exists
x
such that
p=\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x
and
z\not=x
;

•

there exist
p_{1},p_{2}
such that
p=\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}
,

\varphi\;z\;p_{1}
and
\varphi\;z\;p_{2}
;

•

there exists
x,p^{\prime}
such that
p=\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p^{\prime}

and (z = x or
\varphi\;z\;p^{\prime}
).)

An important property that we wish to have for iterms, and to this end we first need to ensure it for pre-iterms, is that for any (pre-)iterm we have a supply of fresh variables. In fact, as a virtue of the cardinality of
\mathsf{{Var}}
being
\aleph_{1}
, we have the following: For any pre-iterm
p
, since the set
\{x\mid x\;\#\;p\}
is countable, there exist uncountably (in particular, infinitely) many fresh variables for
p
.

The
\alpha
-equivalence relation on pre-iterms,
\_\!\equiv\!\_:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
, is defined coinductively by the following rules:

\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x\equiv\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x\hskip 55.97205pt\frac{p_{1}\equiv q_{1}\;\;\;\;\;\;p_{2}\equiv q_{2}}{\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}\equiv\mathsf{{PAp}}\;q_{1}\;q_{2}}\hskip 55.97205pt

\frac{p[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx]\equiv q[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pty]\hskip 30.1388ptz=x\mbox{ or }z\;\#\;p\hskip 30.1388ptz=y\mbox{ or }z\;\#\;q}{\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p\equiv\mathsf{{PLm}}\;y\;q}

Note that the last rule in this definition is in the style of the
SwCg
and
SwCg
∞
properties (on iterms). We obtain the same concept (i.e., we obtain the same relation
\equiv
) if we replace it with the following rule, in the style of
SwBvr
and
SwBvr
∞
:

\frac{p[x^{\prime}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx]\equiv p^{\prime}\hskip 30.1388ptx^{\prime}=x\mbox{ or }x^{\prime}\;\#\;p}{\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p\equiv\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x^{\prime}\;p^{\prime}}

Note that all these operators (swapping, permutation, freshness and
\alpha
-equivalence) would be defined in the same way for (finitary) preterms, i.e., we would write the same equations and rules, but replacing “coinductive” (“greatest fixed point”) with “inductive” (“least fixed point”) and “recursive” with “corecursive”.

E.2.
Iterms

It can be shown that
\equiv
is an equivalence and is compatible with the swapping and permutation operations and freshness predicate, in that:

•

p\equiv q
implies
p[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]\equiv q[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]

•

p\equiv q
implies
p[\sigma]\equiv q[\sigma]

•

x\;\#\;p
and
p\equiv q
implies
x\;\#\;q

We define iterms by quotienting pre-iterms,
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}=\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}/\equiv
. For a pre-iterm
p
, let us write
p/\equiv
for its
\alpha
-equivalence class.

We define the corresponding operators on iterms,

\_[\_\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108pt\_]:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Perm}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
,

\_[\_]:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Perm}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
,
\_\#\_:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
,
by lifting to iterms the pre-iterm operators and taking advantage of their compatibility with
\equiv
. For example,
given
t\in\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
we define

t[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]
to be
(p[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])/\equiv
, where
p
is some pre-iterm such that
t=p/\equiv
(whose choice is immaterial thanks to compatibility).

The free-variable operator
{{\mathsf{FV}}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathsf{{Var}})
is defined as expected, by
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;t=\{x\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid\neg\;x\;\#\;t\}
.

All the properties involving swapping and/or permutation and/or equality and/or freshness/freeness on iterms from Figs.
2
and
6
can now be proved by first establishing their pre-iterm counterparts (with
\equiv
instead of equality) and then lifting them to iterms.

The following is the natural structural coinduction principle for iterms:

Prop 37.

Assume
\varphi:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
is a relation on iterms such that, for all
t,s\in\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
, if
\varphi\;t\;s
then one of the following is true:

•

there exists
x
such that
t=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x=s
;

•

there exist
t_{1},t_{2},s_{1},s_{2}
such that

t=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2}
,
s=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;s_{1}\;s_{2}
,
\varphi\;t_{1}\;s_{1}
and
\varphi\;t_{2}\;s_{2}
;

•

there exist
x,t^{\prime},y,s^{\prime}
such that

t=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t^{\prime}
,
s=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;y\;s^{\prime}
,
(
y=x
or
y\;\#\;t^{\prime}
) and

\varphi\,(t^{\prime}[y\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx])\,s^{\prime}
.

Then
\varphi
is included in iterm equality, in that

\forall t,s.\;\varphi\;t\;s\Rightarrow t=s
.

Note that the above principle reflects the aforementioned alternative,
SwBvr
/
SwBvr
∞
-like definition of
\alpha
-equivalence. A principle that instead reflects the
SwCg
/
SwCg
∞
-like definition is also possible, but is more tedious to use in proofs.

The above principle cannot be inferred directly from the (alternative) definition of
\alpha
-equivalence, which by definition only gives us the following proof principle:

Assume
\varphi:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
is a relation on pre-iterms such that, for all
p,q\in\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
, if
\varphi\;p\;q
then one of the following is true:

•

there exists
x
such that
p=\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x=q
;

•

there exist
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}
such that

p=\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}
,
q=\mathsf{{PAp}}\;q_{1}\;q_{2}
,
\varphi\;p_{1}\;q_{1}
and
\varphi\;p_{2}\;q_{2}
;

•

there exist
x,p^{\prime},y,q^{\prime}
such that

p=\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p^{\prime}
,
q=\mathsf{{PLm}}\;y\;q^{\prime}
,
(
y=x
or
y\;\#\;p^{\prime}
) and

\varphi\;(p[y\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx])\;q^{\prime}
.

Then
\varphi
is included in
\alpha
-equivalence, in that

\forall p,q.\;\varphi\;p\;q\Rightarrow p\equiv q
.

But in order to produce Prop.
37
, we need a stronger version whose hypotheses are weaker, in that pre-iterm equality is replaced by
\alpha
-equivalence—i.e., we need a form of
\alpha
-coinduction up to
\alpha
-equivalence. It turns out that we can prove such a stronger version, if we also assume that the predicate
\varphi
is compatible with
\alpha
-equivalence. This stronger principle is shown below, where we highlight the differences from the previous one:

Assume
\varphi:\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
is an
\alpha
-equivalence-compatible
relation on pre-iterms such that, for all
p,q\in\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
, if
\varphi\;p\;q
then one of the following is true:

•

there exists
x
such that
p\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\equiv$}}\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\equiv$}}q
;

•

there exist
p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}
such that

p\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\equiv$}}\mathsf{{PAp}}\;p_{1}\;p_{2}
,
q\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\equiv$}}\mathsf{{PAp}}\;q_{1}\;q_{2}
,
\varphi\;p_{1}\;q_{1}
and
\varphi\;p_{2}\;q_{2}
;

•

there exist
x,p^{\prime},y,q^{\prime}
such that

p\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\equiv$}}\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;p^{\prime}
,
q\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\equiv$}}\mathsf{{PLm}}\;y\;q^{\prime}
,
(
y=x
or
y\;\#\;p^{\prime}
) and

\varphi\;(p^{\prime}[y\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx])\;q^{\prime}
.

Then
\varphi
is included in
\alpha
-equivalence, in that

\forall p,q.\;\varphi\;p\;q\Rightarrow p\equiv q
.

Now this last principle easily yields Prop.
37
, using the iterm to pre-iterm projection to transport the statement.

The situation of the freshness predicate on iterms versus the one on pre-iterms is similar
to one we just discussed, of iterm equality versus pre-iterm
\alpha
-equivalence. Namely, transporting directly to iterms the coinduction principle from the definition of pre-iterm freshness does not give a proof principle that is strong enough. So we need to play a game similar to the one above, working with an
\alpha
-compatible predicate and replacing equality with
\alpha
-equivalence, which then yields the desired principle (equivalent to the one we would get if we defined freshness directly on iterms coinductively, rather than defining it from pre-iterm freshness):
6
6

6

One may ask why we have not chosen to use this alternative definition of freshness, namely to define iterm freshness directly on iterms without using pre-iterm freshness. While we could have done that, the formal development would not have been simplified, since in order to recover some of the desired properties (those of the interaction between freshness and equality) we would have still needed to
connect iterm freshness with pre-iterm freshness..

Prop 38.

Assume
\varphi:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
is a relation such that, for all
z\in\mathsf{{Var}}
and
t\in\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
, if
\varphi\;z\;t
then one of the following is true:

•

there exists
x
such that
t=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
and
z\not=x
;

•

there exist
t_{1},t_{2}
such that
t=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2}
,

\varphi\;z\;t_{1}
and
\varphi\;z\;t_{2}
;

•

there exists
x,t^{\prime}
such that
t=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t^{\prime}

and (
z=x
or
\varphi\;z\;t^{\prime}
).

Then
\varphi
is included in
\_\#\_
, in that

\forall x,t.\;\varphi\;x\;t\Rightarrow x\;\#\;t
.

Similarly to what happens in the inductive world (for terms), the substitution operator

\_[\_/\_]:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
is not straightforward to define, because there is no corresponding well-behaved substitution operator that can be defined on pre-iterms. In fact, substitution is one of the cases where the nominal corecursors described in this paper can be deployed—see §
I
. But next we describe a route that does not appeal to corecursors.

We will first define a “pre-substitution” operator
\_[\_/\_]^{\prime}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
(thus targeting pre-iterms rather than iterms but still having iterms as source domain) by pre-iterm corecursion. To prepare for this definition, let us introduce the following operators:

•

\sf{isVr}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
and
\sf{getVr}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}
, where

\sf{isVr}\;t
tests if the iterm
t
has the form
\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
, and in this case
\sf{getVr}\;t
returns this unique
x
.

•

\sf{isAp}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
and
\sf{getAp}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
, where

\sf{isAp}\;t
tests if
t
has the form
\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2}
, and in this case
\sf{getAp}\;t
returns this unique pair
(t_{1},t_{2})
.

•

\sf{isLm}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{\sf Bool}
and
\sf{getLm}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
, where

\sf{isLm}\;t
tests if
t
is a
\mathsf{{Lm}}
-abstraction, and in this case
\sf{getLm}\;s\;z\;t
returns
some
pair
(x,t^{\prime})
such that
t=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t^{\prime}
,
x\;\#\;s

and
x\not=z
.

The correctness of all these definitions follows from the properties of iterms. For example, the possibility to write any
\mathsf{{Lm}}
-abstraction iterm as
\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t^{\prime}
where
x
is fresh for
s
and
z
follows from
SwBvr
and the existence of infinitely many fresh variables for any term.

We let
\sf{rep}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
be the function that chooses a pre-iterm representative, i.e., such that
t=(\sf{rep}\;t)/\equiv
for all
t\in\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
.

We are now ready for the corecursive definition of pre-substitution:

\begin{array}[]{l}t[s/z]^{\prime}=\mbox{if $\sf{isVr}\;t$ then (if $z=\sf{getVr}\;t$ then $\sf{rep}\;s$ else $\mathsf{{PVr}}\;(\sf{getVr}\;t)$)}\\
\phantom{t[s/z]^{\prime}=\;}\mbox{else if $\sf{isAp}\;t$ then let $(t_{1},t_{2})=\sf{getAp}\;t$ in
$\mathsf{{PAp}}\;(t[s/z]^{\prime})\;(t[s/z]^{\prime})$}\\
\phantom{t[s/z]^{\prime}=\;}\mbox{else let $(x,t^{\prime})=\sf{getLm}\;s\;z\;t$ in
$\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;(t^{\prime}[s/z]^{\prime})$}\end{array}

Finally, substitution on iterms is defined by
t[s/z]=t[s/z]^{\prime}/\equiv
.
The desired characteristic equations of substitution, namely:

•

(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)[s/z]=
(if
x=z
then
s
else
\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
)

•

(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})[s/z]=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(t_{1}[s/z])\;(t_{2}[s/z])

•

(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)[s/z]=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;(t[s/z])
if
x\not=z
and
x\;\#\;s
.

can now be established by structural iterm coinduction (Prop.
37
)
using the freshness-related properties of
\mathsf{{Lm}}
-abstractions.
The proof of uniqueness, i.e., the fact that substitution is the only operator on iterms satisfying the above equations, also follows by structural iterm coinduction.
7
7

7

We took the trouble to sketch the development leading to the characteristic equations of iterm substitution because they have acted as an inspiration for our proof of the nominal corecursor theorem, Thm.
18
—more precisely, for the direct proof of the
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
corecursion principle—see the §
G
proof sketch of Thm.
18
.

Renaming is of course a particular case of substitution, defined as

t[x/y]=t[\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x/y]
. After the characteristic equations of substitution have been established, all the Figs.
2
and
6
properties involving substitution or renaming follow by iterm coinduction or rule coinduction. In these proofs, each time we need to split into cases according to the structure of an iterm, we make sure that in the abstraction case,
\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t
, the binding variable
x
is fresh for the rest of the proof context—which in particular ensures that the above
\mathsf{{Lm}}
-clause for substitution can be applied. (This is of course a way to enforce Barendregt’s variable convention. A local form of this convention, i.e., fresh cases analysis, seems sufficient in the coinductive world. On the other hand, a binding-aware coinductive notion analogous to fresh induction seems neither needed nor in fact possible.)

Iterms as an abstract (co)datatype.

Given the fact that iterms are less well-known than terms, a valid question to ask is whether our definitions are correct, i.e., whether they capture correctly the notion of infinitary
\lambda
-calculus terms where the identity of bound variables does not matter. The possible uncertainty about this seems to be fed by the definitions via pre-iterms being rather low-level and tedious, not to mention that concepts such as
\alpha
-equivalence can be defined in several ways. Moreover, our way of defining iterms is certainly not the only way. For example,
(
Kurz
et al. 2012
)
define the iterms of finite support as the metric completion of the set of (finitary)
\lambda
-terms.

To resolve this possible uncertainty, the concept of abstract datatype comes handy. After having proved for the above defined iterms:

•

all the properties listed in Figs.
2
and
6
(with “countable” replacing “finite” for the last group in Fig.
2
),

•

the structural conduction principle described by Prop.
37
and

•

the corecursion principles described by Thm.
18
,

we have reached a highly redundant unique characterisation of iterms together with its operators as an abstract datatype (i.e., unique up to an operator-preserving bijection). So we can forget about how iterms were defined, in particular, can forget about pre-iterms and
\alpha
-equivalence.
This process of “forgetting” is also useful from a proof development perspective, since the available proof and definition principles for iterms form a self-sufficient layer of abstraction.

Appendix F
More Details on Epi-Corecursors and Nominal Corecursors

F.1.
Miscellanea

The concept of epi-corecursor is depicted in Fig.

.

\scriptstyle{!_{C,\,J}}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{B=R\,C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{T=R\,J}

.
Epi-corecursor

The criterion that we used for proving that various epi-corecursors are more expressive than others is morphism-dual (though not functor-dual, i.e., the pre-functor’s direction is not reversed) to that
we used for recursors (Prop.
11
):

Prop 39.

Let
{\mathit{cr}}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},J,R)
and

{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},J^{\prime},\allowbreak R^{\prime})
, and assume
F:\underline{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is a pre-functor
such that

R^{\prime}\circ F=R
and

F\;J=J^{\prime}
.
Then
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}\geq{\mathit{cr}}
.

In the main paper, we mentioned that a gentler/laxer comparison relation is available for epi-corecursors as well. This is indeed obtained immediately by morphism-dualizing the one from epi-recursors:

Def 40.

{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},J^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is
quasi-stronger
than
{\mathit{cr}}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},J,R)
, written
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}\gtrsim{\mathit{cr}}
,
when there exists a final segment
(\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0},(m(B):B\rightarrow o(B))_{B\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})})
of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
such that,
for all

g:B\rightarrow T
definable by
{\mathit{cr}}
, there exists
a morphism
g_{0}:o(B)\rightarrow T
such that
g_{0}
is definable by
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}
and
g=g_{0}\circ m(B)
.

The effective criterion for checking
\gtrsim
, Prop.
14
,
can also be morphism-dualized from epi-recursors to epi-corecursors:

Prop 41.

Let
{\mathit{cr}}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},J,R)
and

{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},J^{\prime},\allowbreak R^{\prime})
. Assume

(\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0},(m(B):B\rightarrow o(B))_{B\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})})
is a final segment of
\underline{\mathcal{B}}

and
(\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\allowbreak(m_{1}(C):C\rightarrow o_{1}(C))_{C\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{C}})})
is a final segment of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}

such that
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
contains
J
and

R
preserves the above final segments,
and
F:\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is a pre-functor such that

F\;J=J^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}\circ F=R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}

(where
R_{\upharpoonright\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
is the restriction of
R
to
\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
).
Then
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}\gtrsim{\mathit{cr}}
.

So, as shown in Fig.
10
, we start with a morphism
g
definable by
{\mathit{cr}}
and use the two final segments to factor it
as a morphism
g_{0}
definable by
{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}
and a remainder morphism
m(B)
.

\textstyle{\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\subseteq\underline{\mathcal{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\exists F}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Fig
.
10
.
Gentler criterion for comparing corecursor expressiveness

We also claimed in the main paper, that, unlike in the case of nominal recursors, this gentler comparison relation and criterion are unlikely to bring anything new in terms of concrete nominal corecursor comparisons. This is because, whereas in the case of recursor models
C
we could fruitfully take
m_{1}(C):o_{1}(C)\rightarrow C
to be submodels where properties like finite support would hold and would enable the equivalence of different axiomatizations, here, in the dual case, our best bet would be to take
m_{1}(C):C\rightarrow o_{1}(C)
to be something like quotients—which would be unlikely to preserve even the given axiomatizations (with the
conditional
equations and Horn clauses being particularly problematic), let alone produce stronger ones.

F.2.
Details on the connection with the
(
Blanchette et al. 2019
)
corecursor

The syntax of
\lambda
-calculus is obtained by instantiating the
(
Blanchette et al. 2019
)
binder type
F
to the four-argument functor
F(A_{1},A_{2},T_{1},T_{2})=A_{1}+T_{1}\times T_{1}+A_{2}\times T_{2}
and their binder dispatcher
\theta
to
\{(2,2)\}
. Here
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
refer to (hypothetical) types of free and bound variables respectively, and
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
to (hypothetical) types of terms; and
\theta
says that the second type of variables binds in the second type of terms. Our set
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
of iterms and its constructors and destructor are obtained as the final solution of the equation (isomorphism)
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\cong_{\theta}F(\mathsf{{Var}},\mathsf{{Var}},\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty},\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty})
, i.e.,
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\cong_{\theta}\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}+\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
,
where the index
\theta
indicates the quotienting modulo the
\alpha
-equivalence induced by the binder dispatcher
\theta
. (Their equation is actually solved polymorphically in the variable type, but above we instantiated that type to
\mathsf{{Var}}
.) Their free-variable
operator
{\mathsf{FVars}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{{\mathcal{P}}}(\mathsf{{Var}})
is exactly our
{{\mathsf{FV}}}
.
When restricted to permutations, their map operator

\mathsf{{Fmap}}:(\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}})\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}

is our permutation operator
[\_]:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Perm}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
with reverse order of arguments.

The
(
Blanchette et al. 2019
)
corecursor is described in §7.2 of the cited paper. Again fixing the type of variables to
\mathsf{{Var}}
(which they instead keep polymorphic),
their comodels (introduced in their definition 25), which are the targets for their recursors, become our
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-models (for the perm/free variant corecursor
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
) from §
5.3
, provided we remove one of their unnecessary axioms:

•

their term-like structure axioms (from their definition 20) correspond to our
PmId
,
PmCp
,
PmFv
and
FvPm
axioms;
8
8

8

This is one axiom more than what we assume in
\mathsf{Props}_{2}
; namely their definition 20’s last axiom, which corresponds to
FvPm
, is not in
\mathsf{Props}_{2}
because it is not needed for our recursor.

•

their
DRen
axiom corresponds to our
PmBvr
∞
axiom

•

their
MD
axiom, when split according to the three summands of the underlying sum type, corresponds to our
PmVr
∞
,
PmAp
∞
and
PmLm
∞
;

•

their
VD
axiom, again when split across the sum type, corresponds to our
FrVr
∞
,
FrAp
∞
and
FrLm
∞
.

The conclusion of their corecursion theorem (Theorem 26) states the existence and uniqueness of a function subcommuting with the destructor, commuting with mapping and preserving the free variables—which in this case is the same as a morphism of
\Sigma_{2}
-models.

Appendix G
Proof Sketches for the Corecursor Results

Proof of Prop.
39
.

The proof is dual to that of Prop.
11
:
Assume
g:B\rightarrow T
is definable by
r
, meaning that
g=R\;!_{C,J}
for some
C
in
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.
Let
C^{\prime}=F\;C
.
By the finality of
J^{\prime}
and the fact that
F\;J=J^{\prime}
, we have that

!_{C^{\prime},J^{\prime}}=F\;!_{C,J}
. Hence

g=R\;!_{C,J}=R^{\prime}\;(F\;!_{C,J})=R^{\prime}\;!_{C^{\prime},J^{\prime}}
,
meaning that
g
is definable by
r^{\prime}
. ∎

Proof of Prop.
41
.
Again, dual to that of
Prop.
14
. ∎

Proof of Thm.

.

The proof of all inequalities

,
so we show how to (functorially)

\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{j})

becomes
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{i})
. As before for recursors, we informally discuss these transformations and highlight the intuitions behind these expressiveness results.

Proof of
{\mathit{cr}}_{3}\equiv{\mathit{cr}}_{1}
:

The correspondence between the swapping and permutation operators
proceeds like in the proof of

from Thm.

(which in turn is based on

and
\Sigma_{1}
-models of
PmId
,

(i.e., pre-nominal sets axiomatized via permutation).
Moreover, it is not hard to prove that, along this correspondence:

•

the properties expressing commutations of swapping or permutation with (the three components of) the
destructor, namely
SwVr
∞
,
SwAp
∞
,

∞
versus

∞
,

,

•

and so do the support-defining and bound-variable-renaming properties, namely

∞
,

∞,2
versus

∞
,
PmBvr
∞
.

If we ignore the destructor part, what we ended up proving here is a variation of

countably-supported pre-nominal sets (though in the presence of an uncountable number of variables/atoms).

Proof of
{\mathit{cr}}_{6}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{3}
:
We show that any
(\Sigma_{3},\mathsf{Props}_{3})
-model is
a
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-model via the usual translation of freeness into freshness.
We need to show that (via this freeness-freshness translation)

First, we note that the conjunction of

) implies
SwCg
∞
: we fulfil the existential
in the statement of

∞
by taking
z
to be
x_{2}
.
So we are left to show that, if we define freeness from swapping via

(i.e., employing the countability predicate),
the “expected” properties that connect freeness/freshness with the destructor
(

) and with swapping (

All these follow from the closure properties of countable sets and the structural properties of swapping (i.e., the pre-nomional
set axioms).
Thus,
{\mathit{cr}}_{6}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{3}
follows from Prop.
39
using the freeness-to-freshness translation functor.

Proof of
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{6}
:

Here, the signatures are equal (
\Sigma_{5}=\Sigma_{6}
), and we employ the identity functor
after showing that the
\mathsf{Props}_{6}

\mathsf{Props}_{5}
axioms. We do this by showing
that, in the presence of the other

SwCg
∞

SwBvr

.
Indeed, assume
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;n={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
and
\{(x,m),(x^{\prime},m^{\prime})\}\subseteq K
.

SwCg

gives us a (quasi)fresh

m[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx]=m^{\prime}[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx^{\prime}]
.
From the algebraic properties of swapping and
SwFr
,
we get

m[x^{\prime}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx]=m[z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx][z\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptx^{\prime}]=m^{\prime}

(In summary, thanks to the algebraic properties of swapping and freshness,

by taking a roundabout
through an “auxiliary” fresh variable

.)

Proof of
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}\equiv{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
:
Already from the proof of

It is immediate to show that (if we further apply the freeness-freshness translation) this correspondence extends to
SwFr
versus
PmFv
(simply using that
\_[z_{1}\leftrightarrow z_{2}]

\_[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]

,

,

\{

,

,

\}
.

Proof of
{\mathit{cr}}_{9}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{8}
:

The signatures are the same, and we show that every
\mathsf{Props}_{8}
-model is a
\mathsf{Props}_{9}
-model.
First,
FrVr
∞
,
FrAp
∞
and

∞
follow from the

∞
contability-based definition of freshness from renaming, the corresponding properties of renaming (
RnVr
∞
.

∞
and

1,∞
), and the closure properties of countable sets.
It remains to show that freshness (again, as defined from renaming via

) satisfies

,

and

.
And indeed,

implies that, whenever

, there exists

. Using this, we can show that

, and

“(

or

) implies

z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,m[x/y]
”, follows from

∞
and the closure properties of countable sets.
(We note that, in the analogous case of freshness from swapping, namely in the proof of

without any help of an axiom analogous to

Proof of
{\mathit{cr}}_{9}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{7}
:

After defining a renaming operator from the substitution operator
as usual, we can show that all the “Sb”-axioms instantiate to the corresponding “Rn”-axioms.
We note the following nuance though, which differs from the inductive case: In order to prove

1,∞
from

, we need to infer
x\not=z
from

. This not

∞
but its converse, which in turn follows from
VrInv
and

.

Proof of
{\mathit{cr}}_{3}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{8}
:

From
(
Popescu 2023b
)
, we know that every renset, i.e., model of
RnId
,
RnIm
,
RnCh
,
RnCm

of finite support
gives rise (in a functorial manner) to a nominal set (in the swapping-based axiomatization
(
Pitts 2013
, Section 6.1)
), i.e., a model of
SwId
,
SwIv
,
SwCp

of finite support.
The idea is to define swapping, say, of
z_{1}
with
z_{2}
, from renaming using the standard trick of an intermediate fresh variable
y
: first rename
z_{1}
to
y
, then
z_{2}
to
z_{1}
, and finally
y
to
z_{2}
; such a fresh
y
exists thanks to the renset being finitely supported; the nominal set properties then follow from the renset properties, after showing that the choice of
y
does not matter.

A similar proof works here, but using countable support (
FSupFr
∞
) instead of finite support and taking advantage of the fact that we have uncountably many variables. Moreover (again using the freshness-freeness translation),
FvDSw
∞
follows from
FrDSw
∞
, and
SwBvr
∞
follows from
RnBvr
∞
.
Finally, the destructor-commutation properties of swapping,
SwVr
∞
,
SwAp
∞
and
SwLm
∞
, follow from the corresponding properties of renaming,
RnVr
∞
,
RnAp
∞
and
RnLm
1,∞
.
In order to infer

∞
from (the definition of swapping from renaming and)

1,∞
, we also need

{}_{\infty}^{\prime}
; this is because
SwLm
∞
expresses unconditional commutation, whereas
RnLm
1,∞
conditions commutation by freshness, and
