{}_{\infty}^{\prime}
is needed to provide the necessary “refresher” to bridge this gap.

Proof of
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{9}
:

The proof is similar to that of
{\mathit{cr}}_{3}\geq{\mathit{cr}}_{8}
, noting that the construction of swapping from renaming and the proof of its properties are independent from the tight coupling of freshness/freeness with renaming or swapping (via
FvDSw
or
FrDRn
).
∎

In order to prove Thm.
18
without having to prove eight different corecursion theorems, we use a similar trick to that for recursors described
in §
C.3
. Namely:

•

we prove the corecursion theorem only in
a most expressive case,
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
;

•

we use a slight generalization of Thm.
19
(which assumes pre-epi-corecursors rather than epi-corecursors, but has essentially the same proof as the one sketched above for Thm.
19
) to borrow the result for
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
to the other seven cases, thus inferring the other seven epi-corecursors from
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
.

The relevant definition and proposition follow—they are dual to those from §
C.3
.

Def 42.

A
pre-epi-corecursor
is a tuple
{\mathit{cr}}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},J,R)
subject to the same condition as an epi-corecursor, but without the requirement that
J
is the final object of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
.

A pre-epi-corecursor is called
tight

if the following hold:

•

T
is a quasi-final object in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
(in that for every object
B
in
\underline{\mathcal{B}}
there exists at most one morphism from
B
to
T
).

•

The functor
R
is faithful (in that it is injective on morphisms). ∎

All our pre-epi-corecursors
{\mathit{cr}}_{i}
are tight.
Indeed, the model
T=\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{dtor}})
is quasi-final because its coinduction principle, Prop.
37
, is stronger than
that of the pre-iterm model, Prop.
35
; in other words, Prop.
35
holds for iterms as well, making

T
a fully abstract (hence quasi-final) model.
Moreover, in each case the morphism component of the functor
R
is the identity.

Prop 43.

Assume the following:

•

(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}},J,R)
is a tight pre-epi-recursor

•

{\mathit{cr}}^{\prime}=(\underline{\mathcal{B}},T,\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime},J^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is an epi-corecursor

•

The hypotheses of
Prop.
39

hold, with the additional property that the pre-functor
F
is full (i.e., it is surjecive on morphisms).

Then
r
is an epi-corecursor (i.e.,
J
is final).

Proof sketch:

Dual to the proof of Prop.
29
. ∎

Proof of Thm.
18
.

In light of the above discussion, is suffices to prove that
{\mathit{cr}}_{2}
is an epi-corecursor, i.e., that
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{2})
is the final
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model. So let
\mathcal{M}
be a
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model. We need to show that there exists a unique morphism
g:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{2})
.
As usual, we write
M
for the carrier of
\mathcal{M}
,
{{\mathsf{PDest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}
for its destructor, etc.
The proof follows a similar route to (and is essentially aa generalization of) that we described for
the substitution operator in §
E.2
.

We first define a function to pre-iterms,
g^{\prime}:M\rightarrow\mathsf{PTr}_{\infty}
, using (standard) pre-iterm corecursion:

\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime}\,m=\mbox{case ${{\mathsf{PDest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}m$ of}\\
\hskip 34.44434pt\mid{{\mathsf{V}}}\;x\Rightarrow\mathsf{{PVr}}\;x\\
\hskip 34.44434pt\mid{{\mathsf{A}}}\,(m_{1},m_{2})\Rightarrow\mathsf{{PAp}}\;(g^{\prime}\,m_{1})\;(g^{\prime}\,m_{2})\\
\hskip 34.44434pt\mid{{\mathsf{L}}}\;K\Rightarrow\mbox{let $(x,m^{\prime})\in K$ in }\mathsf{{PLm}}\;x\;(g^{\prime}\,m^{\prime})\end{array}

and then define
g:M\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
by
g\;m=(g^{\prime}\,m)/\equiv\,
.
Note that the definition of
g^{\prime}
, hence that of
g
too, depends on a choice of a pair
(x,m^{\prime})
in
K
(which is guaranteed to be non-empty).

The above definitions immediately imply that
g
commutes with the variable and application cases of the destructor, namely

(1)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
, and

(2)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{A}}}\,(m_{1},m_{2})
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(g\;m_{1})\;(g\;m_{2})

but the problematic case is the abstraction case, where so far we only know:

(3)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
implies that
there exists

(x,m^{\prime})\in K
such that
g\;m=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;(g\;m^{\prime})
.

What we want for a morphism is a stronger version of (3) that replaces “there exists” with “for all’.

By freshness coinduction (Prop.
38
), using that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
FrVr
,
FrAp
and
FrLm
, we can now prove:

(4)

g
preserves freshness/freeness, in that
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m
implies
x\;\#\;(g\;m)
; or, using free-variable notation for iterms,
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m
implies
x\notin{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;(g\;m)
; i.e.,

{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;(g\;m)\subseteq{{\mathsf{FV}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m
.

To prove that
g
commutes with permutation is trickier, and requires a generalization. Namely, we prove:

(5)

g(m[\sigma]^{\mathcal{M}})[\tau]=g(m)[\tau\circ\sigma]
for all
m\in M
and
\tau,\sigma\in\mathsf{{Perm}}
.

This follows by iterm coinduction (Prop.
37
) using (1)–(4) and the fact that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
PmVr
,
PmAp
,
PmLm
, as well as
PmCp
,
PmFv
,
PmBvr
—this last group of properties is needed in addition to
PmLm
for the case when
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m
is an abstraction.

From (5) and the fact that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
PmId
, we immediately get

(6)

g(m)[\tau]=g(m)[\tau]
for all
m\in M
and
\tau\in\mathsf{{Perm}}
,

i.e., commutation of
g
with permutation.
Now, from (3), (6) and the fact that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
PmBvr
∞
, we obtain the stronger version of commutation with abstractions:

(3’)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
implies that
g\;m=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;(g\;m^{\prime})

for all

(x,m^{\prime})\in K
.

Properties (1), (2), (3’), (4), (6) mean that
g
is a morphism of
\Sigma_{2}
-models. Finally, the uniqueness of such a morphism, actually even more strongly the uniqueness of any function satisfying (1), (2) and (3’), follows by iterm coinduction (Prop.
37
).
∎

A note on the above proof: To prove the central fact (5), it was important to work with entire permutations rather than just swapping, essentially because the abstraction case in the proof by iterm coinduction adds a composition with a transposition (a reminiscence of the definition of
\alpha
-equivalence). This is why it seems hopeless to have a corecursor that is based on swapping (i.e., single-transposition permutation) without assuming the axioms necessary to extend swapping to permutation—which contrasts with the situation of recursors, where that was possible and yielded for swapping
\geq
-stronger recursors than for permutation (as seen with
r_{4},r_{5}
and
r_{6}
).

Appendix H
Enhancements to the Corecursors

In §
D
we discussed the notion of enhancing the nominal recursors along two main axes: (1) shifting from iteration to full recursion and (2) adding support for Barendregt’s variable convention.

The Baredregt enhancement does not seem to make sense in the case of nominal corecursors. But such an enhacement does not seems to be needed in the first place, essentially because any type of bound-variable avoidance condition can be integrated in the domain of the chosen model—indeed, unlike in the case of recursion, this is possible for corecursion because we have flexibility in the domain (rather than the codomain) of the to-be-defined function. We will illustrate this phenomenon in §
I
with the corecursive counterpart of the paradigmatic situation that in the recursive case calls for Barendregt’s convention: the definition of (parallel) substitution on iterms.

On the other hand, the enhancement of coiteration to full (structural) corecursion is possible for nominal corecursors, and is fairly straightforward: A full corecursion principle can be inferred from the coiteration principle similarly to how this is done for standard codatatypes. Below we illustrate this on the swap/fresh variant corecursor,
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
.

So we know from Thm.
18
that

\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{5})
is the final
(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-model. This means that,
for all
(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-models
\mathcal{M}
, there exists a unique morphism
g:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{5})
, i.e., a unique function
g:M\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
such that the following hold (where for better readability we write the sub-commutation of
g
with the destructor in the alternative form that employs constructors for iterms):

(1)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x

(2)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{A}}}\,(m_{1},m_{2})
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(g\;m_{1})\;(g\;m_{2})

(3)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
implies that
g\;m=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;(g\;m^{\prime})
for all
(x,m^{\prime})\in K

(4)

g(m[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}})=g(m)[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]

(5)

x\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\;m
implies
x\;\#\;(g\;m)

For full recursion, we consider
generalized
\Sigma_{5}
-models

\mathcal{M}
, whose destructors
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}
have type not
M\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+M\times M+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times M)
, but

M\rightarrow(\mathsf{{Var}}+M\times M+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times M))\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$+\,\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}$}}
. The purpose of this
\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
summand is (just like for standard codatatypes) the possibility to allow an immediate exit from the corecursion calls by returning an iterm.
Morphisms of generalized
\Sigma_{5}
-models are defined as one would expect, in that commutation with the destructor now means

((1_{\mathsf{{Var}}}+g\times g+{\mathsf{image}}(1_{\mathsf{{Var}}}\times g))\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$+\,1_{\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}}$}})\,({{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}m)\sqsubseteq{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(g\;m)
. Above, writing
{{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}
and
{{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}
for the two injections into the sum type,
\sqsubseteq
on
(\mathsf{{Var}}+M^{\prime}\times M^{\prime}+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times M^{\prime}))+\,\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}

is defined by taking
u\leq v
to mean that:
either
u={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{V}}}\;x)=v
for some
x
;
or
u={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime}))=v
for some
m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime}
;
or
u={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{L}}}\;K)
,
v={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{L}}}\;K^{\prime})
and
K\subseteq K^{\prime}
for some
K,K^{\prime}
;
or
u={{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}(t)={{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}(t)=v
for some
t\in\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
.
This condition is equivalent to the conjunction of the following four conditions:

•

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{V}}}\;x)
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
;

•

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1},m_{2}))
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(g\;m_{1})\;(g\;m_{2})
;

•

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{L}}}\;K)
and
(x,m^{\prime})\in K
implies
g\;m=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;(g\;m^{\prime})

•

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\,m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}\;t
implies
g\;m=t
.

Iterms become a generalized
\Sigma_{5}
-model by extending the destructor
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty})
to a “generalized destructor”

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\prime}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow(\mathsf{{Var}}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}+{{\mathcal{P}_{\!\not=\emptyset}}}(\mathsf{{Var}}\times\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}))\mbox{\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$+\;\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}$}}
defined by:

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\prime}\;t={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t)
. Let us call this model
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{5})
.

The notion of a generalized model satisfying a property from
\mathsf{Props}_{5}
is extended from (standard) models in a straightforward manner, by simply inserting the
{{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}
injection. For example,
\mathcal{M}
satisfying
SwAp
∞
means: For all
m,m_{1}m_{2}\in M
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathsf{{Var}}
, if
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1},m_{2}))
then

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(m[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}})={{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1}[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}},m_{2}[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}})
.

Now, the full recursion principle states that
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{5})
is the final generalized

(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-model, and can be proved from the iteration principle as follows. Let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be a generalized
(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-model. We build from it a (standard)
(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-model
\mathcal{M}

on the carrier set
M^{\prime}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}

by combining the operators of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
with those of iterms:

•

M=M^{\prime}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}

•

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;({{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}\;m)=\mbox{case ${{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m$ of}

\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip 107.63855pt\mid{{\mathsf{V}}}\;x\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{V}}}\;x)\\
\hskip 107.63855pt\mid{{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1},m_{2})\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{A}}}(m_{1},m_{2}))\\
\hskip 107.63855pt\mid{{\mathsf{L}}}{\sf{K}}\Rightarrow{{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}\,({{\mathsf{L}}}\,\{(x,{{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}\;m)\mid(x,m^{\prime})\in{{\mathsf{L}}}\;K\})\end{array}

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}\;({{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}\;t)={{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}({{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t)

•

({{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}\;m)[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}}={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}(m[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}})

({{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}\;t)[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}}={{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}(t[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])

•

z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,({{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}\;m)\allowbreak\mathrel{\;\Leftarrow\penalty\kern-6.88889pt\Rightarrow\;}\allowbreak z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m

z\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\,({{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}\;t)\allowbreak\mathrel{\;\Leftarrow\penalty\kern-6.88889pt\Rightarrow\;}\allowbreak z\;\#\;t

That
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{5}
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and the model of iterms do. From coiteration, this gives us a unique morphism
g:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}(\Sigma_{5})
, i.e., a unique function
g:M^{\prime}+\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}=M\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
satisfying clauses (1)–(5) above. Finally, we define
g^{\prime}:M^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
by
g^{\prime}\,m=g({{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}\;m)
. Then (1)–(5) imply that
g^{\prime}
satisfies the clauses:

(1’)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\,m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{V}}}\;x)
implies
g^{\prime}\,m=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x

(2’)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\,m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{A}}}\,(m_{1},m_{2}))
implies
g^{\prime}\,m=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;(g^{\prime}\,m_{1})\;(g^{\prime}\,m_{2})

(3’)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\,m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{L}}}\;K)
implies that
g^{\prime}\,m=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;(g\;m^{\prime})
for all
(x,m^{\prime})\in K

(4’)

g^{\prime}(m[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}})=g^{\prime}(m)[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]

(5’)

x\;\#^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}m
implies
x\;\#\;(g^{\prime}\,m)

and additionally the following follows by iterm coinduction:

(6’)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\,m={{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}\,t
implies
g^{\prime}\,m=t

Clauses (1’)–(6’) mean that
g^{\prime}
is a morphism of generalized
\Sigma_{5}
-models, so
g^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{5})
. The uniqueness of
g^{\prime}
follows again by iterm coinduction.

Appendix I
Example of deploying a nominal corecursor

Next we show how the (capture-free) parallel substitution operator can be defined using the swap/fresh variant recursor
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
. To keep the definition simple, we will use the full recursion enhancement of
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
described in §
H
. Let
{{\mathsf{Env}}}
be the set
(variable-term) environments
, which are functions
\rho:\mathsf{{Var}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
whose support
{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho
is countable, where
{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho
is defined to consist of all the variables
x
that are changed by
\rho
(in that
\rho\;x\not=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x
) and all the free variables of the images of such variables,
{{\mathsf{FV}}}\;(\rho\;x)
; in other words,
{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho=\bigcup_{x\in\mathsf{{Var}},\rho\;x\not=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x}(\{x\}\cup{{\mathsf{FV}}}(\rho\;x))
. (Note that, since iterms have countably many free variables, for
{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho
to be countable it suffices that the smaller set
\{x\in\mathsf{{Var}}\mid x\not=\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x\}
is countable.)
We wish to define
{{\mathsf{psubst}}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{{\mathsf{Env}}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
satisfying the following clauses:

(1’)

{{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;(\mathsf{{Vr}}\;x)\;\rho=\rho\;x

(2’)

{{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;(\mathsf{{Ap}}\;t_{1}\;t_{2})\;\rho=\mathsf{{Ap}}\;({{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t_{1}\;\rho)\;({{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t_{2}\;\rho)

(3’)

{{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;(\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;t)\;\rho=\mathsf{{Lm}}\;x\;({{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t\;\rho)
if
x\notin{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho

which can be reformulated as follows using the iterm destructor:

(1)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
implies

{{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t\;\rho=\rho\;x

(2)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{A}}}(t_{1},t_{2})
implies

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\,({{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t\;\rho)={{\mathsf{A}}}\,({{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t_{1}\;\rho,{{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t_{2}\;\rho)

(3)

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
implies that there exists
K^{\prime}
such that

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\,({{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t\;\rho)={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K^{\prime}
and
\{(x,{{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t^{\prime}\;\rho)\mid(x,t^{\prime})\in K\mbox{ and }x\notin{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho\}\subseteq K^{\prime}

Asking how this to-be-defined function is supposed to interact with swapping and freshness, we obtain the following additional desired clauses:

(4)

({{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t\;\rho)[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]={{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;(t[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])\;(\rho[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])

where
\rho[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]
is defined as
\lambda x.\;\rho(x[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]

(5)

x\;\#\;t
and
x\notin{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho
implies

x\;\#\;({{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t\;\rho)

Now, clauses (1)–(5) determine the following generalized
\Sigma_{5}
-model
\mathcal{M}
of carrier set
M=\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\times{{\mathsf{Env}}}
(where clause (1) represents an “exit” point and thus takes advantage of the extra flexibility of full corecursion):

(1m)

If
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{V}}}\;x
then we define
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(t,\rho)={{\mathsf{In}}}_{2}\,(\rho\;x)

(2m)

If
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{A}}}(t_{1},t_{2})
then we define

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(t,\rho)={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{A}}}\,((t_{1},\rho),(t_{2},\rho)))

(3m)

If
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
then we define

{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(t,\rho)={{\mathsf{In}}}_{1}({{\mathsf{L}}}\,\{(x,(t^{\prime},\rho))\mid(x,t^{\prime})\in K\mbox{ and }x\notin{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho\})

(4m)

We define
(t,\rho)[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}}=(t[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}],\rho[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}])

(5m)

We define
x\;\#^{\mathcal{M}}\;(t,\rho)

to mean

x\;\#\;t
and
x\notin{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho

Note that the above definitions (1m)–(5m) of the operators of
\mathcal{M}
mirror the clauses (1)--(5).
9
9

9

This is similar to the situation we discussed for nominal recursors in §
3.1
.
Here we have some flexibility about the model’s destructor in the abstraction case, because clause (3) states an inclusion; (3m) chooses the minimal solution to satisfy (3).
Thus, stated about a presumptive function
{{\mathsf{psubst}}}:\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}\rightarrow{{\mathsf{Env}}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
, clauses (1)–(5)
mean exactly that the curried version of
{{\mathsf{psubst}}}
, namely

\lambda(t,\rho).\;{{\mathsf{psubst}}}\;t\;\rho
, is a morphism of generalized
\Sigma_{5}
-models
between
\mathcal{T}\hskip-2.58334ptr_{\infty}^{\prime}(\Sigma_{5})
(the generalized model of iterms) and
\mathcal{M}
. Thus, thanks to the recursion theorem for
{\mathit{cr}}_{5}
(the full recursion version)
all we need to do in order to obtain the desired function
{{\mathsf{psubst}}}
satisfying (1)–(5) is to show that
\mathcal{M}
is
(\Sigma_{5},\mathsf{Props}_{5})
-model, i.e., it satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{5}
.

And this last fact follows routinely from the definitions and the properties of iterms. For example, the fact that
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
SwFr
means:

For all
m\in M
, if
z_{1},z_{2}\,\#^{\mathcal{M}}m
then

m[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]^{\mathcal{M}}=m
.

which means, using the definitions of
\mathcal{M}
’s carrier and operators:

For all
t\in\mathsf{Tr}_{\infty}
and
\rho\in{{\mathsf{Env}}}
, if
z_{1},z_{2}\,\#\,t
and
z_{1},z_{2}\notin{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho

then

t[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]=t
and
\rho[z_{1}\hskip-1.07639pt\wedge\hskip-0.86108ptz_{2}]=\rho
.

This follows immediately from the fact that iterms satisfy
SwFr
and from the definitions of swapping and support for environments.

One may wonder where in the above development we needed that the environments have countable support: It was in ensuring that the destructor
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}^{\mathcal{M}}(t,\rho)
is well defined for the case when
{{\mathsf{Dest}}}\;t={{\mathsf{L}}}\;K
, in that it returns
{{\mathsf{L}}}\;K^{\prime}
for a
non-empty
set
K^{\prime}
.
Indeed, in the absence of the countable support assumption, the existence of a pair
(x,t^{\prime})\in K
such that
x\notin{{\mathsf{supp}}}\;\rho
is not guaranteed.

Note that, similarly to term substitution, iterm substitution must avoid the capturing of free variables in the case of
\mathsf{{Lm}}
-abstractions, as shown in clause (3’). However, here we do not need any kind of Barendregt enhancement, but have a different mechanism of ensuring that: Corecursion requires us to operate with the destructor-based clause (3) instead, which makes it clear that such avoidance conditions can be factored in the domain of the to-be-defined function (substitution).

Appendix J
Isabelle Mechanization

We have mechanized our results about nominal (co)recursors as epi-(co)recursors and their comparisons in the theorem prover Isabelle/HOL
(
Nipkow
et al. 2002
)
. More precisely, we have mechanized the following results:

•

the recursion theorem (Thm.
9
), also in the enhanced-recursor version (Thm.
33
);

•

the two recursor comparison theorems (Thm.
12
and Thm.
15
), also in the enhanced-recursor version (Thm.
34
);

•

the two negative (strictness) results on recursor comparison (Props.
16
);

•

the corecursion theorem (Thm.
18
);

•

the corecursor comparison theorem (Thm.
19
).

What we have
not
mechanized are the abstract criteria for comparing epi-recursors and epi-corecursors (Prop.
11
, Prop.
14
and Prop.
39
). In our mechanized results, rather than invoking these criteria, we have inlined their content on a need basis, as we will explain below.

The mechanization is
provided as a publicly available archive,
containing the Isabelle sources as well as a browsable html version (documented by a README file and by comments in the sources).
For the recursors, it covers both the stripped-down versions discussed in the main paper and their enhancements discussed in App.
D
.

We made heavy use of Isabelle’s locales
(
Kammüller et al. 1999
;
Ballarin 2014
)
, which we found to be an excellent abstraction mechanism for representing the expressiveness relationships between (co)recursors. The readers not interested in locales but wishing to inspect the end mechanized results in a manner than closely matches the formulations from the paper can skip to §
J.4
.

A locale fixes some types, constants and assumptions.
One can perform definitions and prove theorems inside a locale, and everything happens relative to the entities fixed in that locale. Viewed from outside the locale, all these definitions and theorems are (1) polymorphic in that locale’s fixed types, (2) universally quantified over that locale’s constants, and (3) conditioned by that locale’s assumptions.

A locale can be
interpreted
at the top level of an Isabelle theory by providing concrete types and constants for that locale’s parameter types and constants, and verifying the locale’s assumptions; after a successful interpretation, all the definitions performed and theorems proved in a locale are automatically instantiated with these concrete types and constants.
A locale
L_{2}
can also be interpreted relative to another locale
L_{1}
by establishing a
sublocale
relationship
L_{1}\leq L_{2}
. This amounts to showing that the entities of
L_{1}
can provide an interpretation of those of
L_{2}
; i.e., in the context of the fixed types, constants and assumptions of
L_{1}
, one indicates some types and constants that instantiate those of
L_{2}
, and verifies the assumptions of
L_{2}
.

The traditional application of locales is in modularizing the development of algebraic structures, such as groups, rings, fields etc.
(
Ballarin 2014
;
Ballarin 2020
)
.
Then (top-level) interpretations provide particular examples of such structures, e.g., interpreting the ring locale into the particular ring of integers. Moreover, sublocale relationships are useful for showing the inclusion between two types of structure, e.g., fields are particular kinds of rings, or more generally for showing that one type of structure induces another type of structure.

Our own results in this paper are also algebraic / model-theoretic in nature. We used locales and sublocales to represent and connect our different recursor and corecursor models.

J.1.
Mechanization of the results about recursors

For each of the nine types of models underlying the nominal recursors, we have introduced a locale, as shown in Fig.
11
.

Isabelle locales corresponding to recursors

Each locale fixes the carrier type
M
of a model and the operations and relations on the model: constructor, permutation, swapping, substitution, renaming, free-variable and freshness operators.
Then it postulates the respective axioms. (In the case of the enhanced recursors, the locale also fixes the domain
D\subseteq\mathsf{Tr}\times M
and assumes that
D
is closed under the operations, as explained in App.
D
; it also fixes a set of variables
X
, assumes its finiteness, and the axioms are stated relative to
X
, again as explained in App.
D
.) In short, each locale axiomatizes a class of models, namely that of
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models (and
(X,\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-models) for each recursor
r_{i}
.

J.1.1.
Mechanization of the recursor comparison results

Recall that the results on strength comparison reported in Thm.
12
(and extended to enhanced recursors in Thm.
34
) essentially show that
one recursor is stronger than another, say
r_{i}\geq r_{j}
, by showing that any
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-model
\mathcal{M}
is (or can be regarded as) a
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-model—that is, after defining on
\mathcal{M}
the
\Sigma_{i}
-operations. We expressed this in Isabelle as follows: Say
L_{i}
and
L_{j}
are the locales for these two classes of models. Working inside locale
L_{j}
, we defined the
\Sigma_{i}
operations and proved for them the
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
properties. This allowed us to prove the locale relationship
L_{j}\leq L_{i}
, which is a statement of
r_{i}\geq r_{j}
. This shallow embedding of the
\geq
relationship allowed us to concretely borrow for
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models the
r_{i}
recursor, in other words to infer the
r_{j}
recursor from the
r_{i}
recursor.

Fig
.
12
.
The Isabelle theories for recursors

To illustrate this more concretely, let us consider one of the statements of Thm.
12
, say
r_{4}\geq r_{2}
, comparing the swap/free recursor
r_{4}
with the perm/free variant recursor
r_{2}
. All the theories we reference below are located in the directory
Stripped
\_
Down
from the archive. The mechanisation of this result has the following components:

•

The class of models for each recursor corresponds to an Isabelle locale, which fixes a carrier set (as an unspecified type
{}^{\prime}D
) and operations on it as indicated by the recursor’s signature, and assumes the recursor’s characteristic properties (sometimes called “axioms” in the paper). Namely:

–

The models of
r_{2}
(the perm/free variant recursor) are mechanized as the locale

\_
model
(located in theory
PermFree
\_
PermFreeV

,

and

, and permutation- and free-variable-like operators permD and FvarsD, and assumes the model properties required by

– the Isabelle notations for these properties coincide with the ones from the paper, e.g.,

–

Similarly, the models of

(located in theory

\_

\_
SwapFree

•

The definition of an operator, let us refer to it as

(since this will represent an instance of the pre-functor

from the paper’s Prop.

), that maps

–

In the context of the

\_
model
\_

\_

\_

\small\textsf{PermFreeV$\_$model}<\small\textsf{SwapFree$\_$model}

\_

\_

\_

\_
model
locale and known to be the unique morphism between the term model and the (arbitrary)
(\Sigma_{4},\mathsf{Props}_{4})
-model fixed in the
SwapFree
\_
model
locale.

A similar, but slightly more involved mechanism was used for mechanizing
the quasi-strength comparison results of Thm.
15

(extended to enhanced recursors in Thm.
34
). Remember that
r_{i}\gtrsim r_{j}
was proved by showing that any
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-model
\mathcal{M}
has a
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-submodel—in that there exists a submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
that on the one hand still satisfies
\mathsf{Props}_{j}
, and on the other hand can be regarded as a
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-model (again, via defining on
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
the
\Sigma_{i}
-operations). We expressed this in Isabelle as follows: Working inside locale
L_{j}
, we identified a suitable subset
M^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{M}
’s carrier
M
(and, for enhanced recursors, a suitable subset of
\mathcal{M}
’s domain

D
) and proved that it is closed under the operations and satisfies the
\mathsf{Props}_{j}
properties—as discussed in the proof sketch of Thm.
15
from App.
C
, this was in each case a minimal set closed under the constructors, defined inductively. In other words, we built a
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-submodel. To capture this using locales, we defined the locale
L_{j}^{\prime}
that extends
L_{j}
with a subset
M^{\prime}
that forms a
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-submodel, and proved
L_{j}\leq L_{j}^{\prime}
by defining
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
to be the aforementioned minimal submodel of
\mathcal{M}
. Then we defined the

\Sigma_{i}
operations on
M^{\prime}
(more precisely, we defined them on the entire type and proved that
M^{\prime}
is closed under them), after which we
proved for them the
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
properties. This allowed us to prove the locale relationship
L_{j}\leq L_{i}
, using the submodel
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
rather than the model
\mathcal{M}
as basis for constructing the model for
L_{i}
.
These two locale inclusions together
form a statement of
r_{i}\gtrsim r_{j}
. Again, this mechanized relationship is effective, in that it allowed us to infer the
r_{j}
recursor from the
r_{i}
recursor.

J.1.2.
Mechanization of the recursion theorems

As a byproduct of the above network of sublocales that allows borrowing recursors, we were able to infer all the nine recursors from just two of them, namely
r_{6}
(the swap-fresh recursor) and
r_{9}
(the renaming/fresh variant recursor)—as discussed in the proof sketch of Thm.
9
from
App.
C
. For
r_{6}
and
r_{9}
, we performed direct proofs of initiality. The initial morphism was constructed by first defining inductively a relation and then proving that it is a function and it commutes with the relevant operations and preserves freshness. This approach is distinct from (and we believe simpler than) previous techniques from the literature used to prove nominal recursion principles. For example,
Norrish 2004
bases the proof of his swap/free recursor on a previous recursor by
Gordon and Melham 1996
, which in turn uses the lifting of a function from preterms after proving that it respects
\alpha
-equivalence. Similarly,
Pitts 2006
’s proof of the perm/free recursor lifts a function from preterms.
Our approach is simpler in that it does not delve into preterms, but operates entirely at the abstraction level of terms.

To illustrate the borrowing process, let us give again a concrete example, considering the swap/fresh recursor
r_{6}
: In the context of the
SwapFresh
\_
model
locale, i.e., fixing a
(\Sigma_{6},\mathsf{Props}_{6})
-model, consisting of a type
{}^{\prime}D
and some operators

VrD
,
ApD
,
LmD
,
swapD
and
freshD
satisfying the
\mathsf{Props}_{6}
properties, we proved the existence and uniqueness of a
\Sigma_{6}
-model morphism from the term model to this (arbitrary) fixed model. This was done by defining a function
ff0
from terms to
{}^{\prime}D
, proving that it is is a morphism of
\Sigma_{6}
-models (i.e., commutes with the constructors, swapping and freshness operators), and proving that any other morphism of
\Sigma_{6}
-models must be equal to
ff0
. All this work was performed in the theory
Swap
\_
Fresh
\_
recursor
. The relevant theorems (as indicated via comments in the formalization) are called
ff0
\_
Vr
,
ff0
\_
Ap
,
ff0
\_
Lm
,
ff0
\_
swap
and
ff0
\_
fresh
(together stating the morphism property) and
ff0
\_
unique
(stating the uniqueness property). Note that the uniqueness property is actually stated in a stronger form: not only is
ff0
the unique
\Sigma_{6}
-morphism, but is even unique among
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-morphisms, i.e., unique among functions commuting with the constructors.

Now, we could have done direct proofs of soundness for all our recursors (like we did for
r_{6}
and
r_{9}
), but we noticed that we can instead use the expressiveness relationships we discovered between them as a mechanism for
borrowing
soundness from the more expressive ones. However, this was not possible with the relationships as stated in the paper because those already assumed the recursors to be sound (in fact our very notion of recursor assumed soundness); but it
became
possible with a slight generalization of our results. Because this generalization does not bring much conceptually and might have distracted the reader from the main ideas, we decided not to include it in the main paper but to discuss it in the appendix (App.
C
) as a “formal engineering optimization”.

J.1.3.
Theory structure

The theory structure of our Isabelle development for recursors is shown in Fig.
12
. Everything is based on a formalization of terms as
\alpha
-equivalence classes of preterms, in the theory
Lambda
\_
Terms
. Due to the need to borrow some properties from the terms model to arbitrary models (for given signatures) via the initial morphism (as explained in the proof sketch of Thm.
15
from App.
C
), a large theory of terms had to be formalized, comprising a wealth of results about the term operators, depth-based and fresh induction principles.
Moreover, the auxiliary theory
Swap
\_
vs
\_
Perms
performs the conversions between swapping-based and permutation-based axioms: starting with the classic result on switching between the two alternative axiomatizations of nominal sets as described in Pitts’s monograph
(
Pitts 2013
, Section 6.1)
, and extending this correspondence in various ways as needed by the various recursors: to covering a separate freshness predicate (not reducible to swapping or permutation), to relaxing nominal sets to “nominal sets modulo
X
” for a more comprehensive application of Barendregt’s convention (as discussed in App.
D
), etc.

The names of the other theories in Fig.
12
are
self-explanatory. For example:

•

the theory
RenamingFreshV
\_
model
formalizes the models for the renaming/fresh variant recursor (and of course contains the locale with the same name);

•

the theory
SwapFresh
\_
SwapFreshV
\_
SwapFree
\_
models
formalizes the models corresponding to the swap/fresh, swap/fresh variant and swap/free recursors (and contains the corresponding locales);

•

the theory
Renaming
\_
model
\_
is
\_
RenamingFreshV
\_
submodel
proves that each renaming model is (can be regarded as) a renaming/fresh variant model, via the sublocale statement
\small\textsf{Renaming$\_$model}\leq\small\textsf{RenamingFreshV$\_$model}
;

•

the theory
SwapFresh
\_
model
\_
has
\_
SwapFreeV
\_
submodel
proves that each swap/fresh model has a swap/fresh submodel that is (can be regarded as) a swap/freee variant model, via the sublocale statements

\small\textsf{SwapFresh$\_$model}\leq\small\textsf{submodel$\_$SwapFresh$\_$model}
and
\small\textsf{SwapFresh$\_$model}\leq\small\textsf{SwapFreeV$\_$model}
.

Note that there are three theories whose names refer explicitly to a recursor:
RenamingFreshV
\_
recursor
,
SwapFresh
\_
recursor
and

SubstFresh
\_
recursor
. The first two of these contain direct formalizations of the renaming/fresh variant and swap/fresh recursors. As discussed in the proof sketch of Thm.
9

in App.
C
, these two recursors (which are at the top of the
\geq
hierarchy) have been used to derive all the other recursors. In all but one case, we have performed this derivation right after the sublocale result that enables it. For example, the swap/free variant recursor is derived from the swap/fresh recursor in theory
SwapFresh
\_
model
\_
has
\_
SwapFreeV
\_
submodel
, right after the sublocale relationship
\small\textsf{SwapFresh$\_$model}\leq\small\textsf{SwapFreeV$\_$model}
is established.
The exception is the subst/fresh recursor, to which we dedicated its own theory
SubstFresh
\_
recursor
—this was done in order to highlight the slightly more involved structure of the borrowing argument, which requires fresh induction.

The theory
All
imports all the relevant top theories (and a few of the relevant non-top ones for better documentation) and contains comments that map the formalization to the paper.
10
10

10

For the theories that are located in the figure below
All
, i.e., import this theory, please see §
J.4
.

J.2.
Mechanization of the negative results

The two negative results expressed in Prop.
16
are mechanized in the theory
Prop16
.
The mechanized statement follows closely the presentation from §
4.4
, in each case stating that there exist morphisms definable by one recursor but not by the other.
The mechanized proofs also follow closely Prop.
16
’s proof sketch given in the paper (and the extended proof sketch given in App.
C
). For each of the two
r_{i}\not\geq r_{j}
results, we:
(1) build a
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-model (and prove that it is indeed a
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-model),
and (2) show that there exists no extension of
the
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-part of that model to a
(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})
-model.
(In each case, such an extension would consist of freeness and permutation operators that, together with the constructor-like operators, satisfy the
\mathsf{Props}_{i}
-properties.)

Our approach to mechanizing the corecursors is similar to that we took for recursors. Namely, we have a locale for each of the eight types of models underlying the nominal corecursors, as shown in Fig.

The proof of Thm.
19
shows that

(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})

\Sigma_{j}

\mathsf{Props}_{i}

\mathsf{Props}_{j}

L_{j}\leq L_{i}

L_{i}

(\Sigma_{i},\mathsf{Props}_{i})

. This again allowed us to borrow for
(\Sigma_{j},\mathsf{Props}_{j})
-models the
r_{i}

r_{j}
corecursor from the
r_{i}
corecursor.

J.4.
Locale-free, top-level statements of the main results

We would like to stress that our mechanization, while walking a tight rope in order to minimize the number of recursion principles that are proved directly (without borrowing), does
not
suffer from any bootstrapping problem or incur any additional assumptions. Rather, it certifies
Thms.
9
,
12
,
15
,

18
and
19
from the main paper (and also Thms.
33
and
34
from the appendix)
as they are claimed in the paper, but using the Isabelle locale jargon. (And Prop.
16
has a faithful formalization as well, but that does not make use of locales—see §
J.2
.)

For readers who are interested in inspecting the mechanized statements of the results but not in understanding the locale jargon, we have also reformulated the results in a manner that matches closely the statements from the main paper.

In Fig.
12
(for recursors), the locale-free statements are in the theories that inherit
All
, culminating with theories that have suggestive names, namely
Theorem9
,
Theorem12
,
Theorem15
and
Prop16
. The main results in these theories, which can be found using the keyword “theorem”,
are statements of Thm.
9
, Thm.
12
, Thm.
15
and Prop.
16
that, just like their paper counterparts,
refer to the nominal recursors
r_{i}
using their epi-recursor structure and the notion of definability (introduced in the theory
Definability
\_
by
\_
Recursors
). Most of the results are formalized using categories of models assumed to have the carrier sets as the entire type (which is very convenient in HOL formalizations); however, for Thm.
15
and one half of Prop.
16
, we need the greater flexibility offered by considering explicit carrier sets (as subsets of the underlying types), so we formalized the set-based versions of these categories as well.

For example, the part of Thm.
9
that refers to
r_{1}
states the recursion (initiality) principle as follows
(in theory
Theorem9
):

theorem init
\_
I1:

isObjectC1 (VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)
\Longrightarrow

\exists!
G. isMorphismC1 G I1 (VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)"

This states that for any object of the category
C1
(the category of models for
r_{1}
),
there exists a unique morphism
G
from the term model
I1
to
C1
.
Note that an object in
C1
is a tuple (VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD) consisting of
constructor-like, permutation-like and free-variable-like operators on a carrier type (not shown
explicitly in the tuple). The predicates
isObjectC1
and
isMorphismC1
are defined appropriately
(in particular, the models are required to satisfy the
\mathsf{Props}_{1}
properties), and shown to form a category
(via a separate Isabelle statement). The model
I1
is defined as the term model for the signature
\Sigma_{1}
:

definition I1 where I1 = (Vr, Ap, Lm, perm, Fvars)

Indeed,
Vr
,
Ap
,
Lm
,
perm
,
Fvars
are defined to be the standard operators on terms.

In fact, we define all the components of the epi-recursor
r_{1}
=
(B,T,C1,I1,R1)
: the base category
B
and its
base object
T
, the category
C1
and its object
I1
, the functor
R1
, and wee prove that
they are indeed categories and functor, that
R1
applied objects to
I1
yields
T
, etc. In short,

(B,T,C1,I1,R1)
is shown to be an epi-recursor. And the same is done for all the other recursor
r_{i}
. All this is done
in theory
Theorem9
.

The recursion theorem is alternatively expressed in combinator form (also in theory
Theorem9
):

theorem REC1
\_
I1:

isObjectC1 (VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)
\Longrightarrow

isMorphismC1 (REC1 VrD ApD LmD) I1 (VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)

The above says that
REC1 VrD ApD LmD
is the unique morphism from the term model
I1
to the model
(VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)
.
Note that the combintor
REC1
only depends on the constructor-like operators

VrD
,
ApD
and
LmD
, and not on the other two operators,
permD
and
FvarsD
;
however, the fact that
REC1 VrD ApD LmD
is a morphism between
I1
and

(VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)
of course relies crucially on
permD
and
FvarsD
and the
\mathsf{Props}_{1}
properties.

Definability by recursor
r_{1}
is expressed as follows (in theory
Definablity
\_
by
\_
Recursors
):
12
12

12

“
fun
” is another way of introducing definitions in Isabelle. We prefer it here because, unlike “
definition
”, it allows pattern matching.

fun definableByR1 where

definableByR1 f (VrD,ApD,LmD) =

\exists
permD FvarsD. isObjectC1 (VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)
\wedge

f = REC1 VrD ApD LmD

Thus, for a morphism
f
between the term
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-model and
another
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ctor}}
-model (VrD,ApD,LmD), the definability predicate says that there exists a extension of (VrD,ApD,LmD)
to a
C1
object
(VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)
(i.e., a
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model)
such that
f
can be defined as the unique morphism between the term
(\Sigma_{1},\mathsf{Props}_{1})
-model
and
(VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)
(namely, using the
REC1
combinator). The above is done for all the recursors
r_{i}
.

Now, for example, the
r_{5}\geq r_{4}
part of Thm.
12
is expressed just like in the paper,
but expanding the definition of
\geq
,
as an implication between definabilities (in theory
Theorem12
):

theorem r5
\_
ge
\_
r4:

definableByR4 f (VrD, ApD, LmD)
\Longrightarrow

definableByR5 f (VrD, ApD, LmD)

And the
r_{2}\not\geq r_{4}
part of Prop.
16
is expressed again like in the paper with the definition of
\geq
expanded,
stating the existence of an
f
definable by
r_{4}
but not by
r_{2}
(in theory
Prop16
):

theorem not
\_
r2
\_
ge
\_
r4:

\exists
f. definableByR4 f (VrD, ApD, LmD)
\wedge

\neg
definableByR2 f (VrD, ApD, LmD)

Set-based versions of the concepts.

While working with models as tuples of operators on the entire type (i.e., assuming that the carrier of the model
is an entire type) does not lose generality, sometimes we need more flexibility—for example, when we wish to consider
submodels, whose carriers are usually not the entire type. Making disjoint copies and performing type definitions
could get us by, but in these cases it is more convenient to employ a more flexible, “set-based” version of the models,
with explicit carrier sets. In this more flexible setting, for example the models for
r_{1}
are now

(D,VrD,ApD,LmD,permD,FvarsD)
which in addition to the operators also feature a subset
D
of the carrier
type, assumed to be closed under these operators, e.g.,
ApD d1 d2

\in
D whenever
d1,d2

\in

D
.
We use primed notation to indicate these set-based concepts, e.g.,
isObjectC1’
,

definableByR1’
, etc. While the primed versions are semantically equivalent to the originals, they are needed for modeling certain phenomena in a more finite-grained manner. They are formalized in
the theories
Set
\_
Based
\_
Recursors
and
Definablity
\_
by
\_
Recursors
.

The theory
Theorem12
\_
setBased
infers the set-based version of Thm.
12
by transferring to sets the results from theory
Theorem12
.
For example, the
r_{5}\geq r_{4}
part of Thm.
12

is expressed as follows in the set-based setting (in theory
Theorem12
\_
setBased
):

theorem r5
\_
ge
\_
r4’:

definableByR4’ f (D, VrD, ApD, LmD)
\Longrightarrow

definableByR5’ f (D, VrD, ApD, LmD)

The primed set-based versions yield the original versions, e.g.,
r5
\_
ge
\_
r4’
yields
r5
\_
ge
\_
r4
by taking
D
to be universal set (comprising the entire type).

Because the
r_{1}\not\geq r_{2}
part of Prop.
16
requires a set defined by a predicate,
we also formalize it using the more general primed models. Namely, after defining
a particular
(\Sigma_{2},\mathsf{Props}_{2})
-model as described in the proof of Prop.
16
,
which in the formalization we call
(E,VrE,ApE,LmE)
, we prove:
13
13

13

So in the following statement,
E,VrE,ApE,LmE
are
not
universally quantified variables (as are, for example,
D,VrD,ApD,LmD
in the theorem
r5
\_
ge
\_
r4’
above), but certain defined constants.

theorem not
\_
r1
\_
ge
\_
r2:

\exists
f. definableByR2’ f (E, VrE, ApE, LmE)
\wedge

\neg
definableByR1’ f (E, VrE, ApE, LmE)

The same is true for Thm.
15
, where we must consider initial segments formed by submodels. For example, the

r_{6}\gtrsim r_{8}
part of Thm.
15
is formalized as follows (in theory
Theorem15
):

theorem r6
\_
quasi
\_
ge
\_
r8’:

definableByR8’ g (D, VrD, ApD, LmD)
\Longrightarrow

\exists
g0. isMorphismB’ g0 T’ (ob’ (D, VrD, ApD, LmD))
\wedge

\exists

g = mo’ (D, VrD, ApD, LmD)
\circ
g0
\wedge

\exists

definableByR6’ g0 (ob’ (D, VrD, ApD, LmD))

Its formulation matches that from the paper, but again expands the definition of
\gtrsim
:
mo’

and
ob’
formalize the initial segment
(\underline{\mathcal{B}}_{0},(m(B):o(B)\rightarrow B)_{B\in\mathsf{Obj}(\underline{\mathcal{B}})})
from Def.
13

(where
mo’
is the morphism operator
m
and
